# The Art of Electronics, 3rd ed. (Horowitz & Hill), part 2 of 4 (§5.10-§9.5) - Anvil rulebook
## 0. Citation
[1] P. Horowitz and W. Hill, *The Art of Electronics*, 3rd ed. New York, NY, USA: Cambridge University Press, 2015. ISBN 978-0-521-80926-9 (hardback).

BOOKTAG: AOE. This file is part 2 of a 4-part extraction; it covers text lines 18822-38215 of the plain-text conversion (printed pp. 319-636). Rule ids AOE-2001 to AOE-2867 (867 rules).

Chapters covered by THIS extraction:
- Ch.5 Precision Circuits, §5.10-§5.17 and the Chapter 5 review (pp. 319-390)
- Ch.6 Filters, complete with review (pp. 391-424)
- Ch.7 Oscillators and Timers, complete with review (pp. 425-472)
- Ch.8 Low-Noise Techniques, complete with review (pp. 473-593)
- Ch.9 Voltage Regulation and Power Conversion, §9.1-§9.5 (pp. 594-636)

Chapters NOT read by this extraction (with reason):
- Front matter and TOC (pp. i-xxx): read for orientation only.
- Ch.1-4 and Ch.5 §5.1-§5.9: outside the assigned range (part 1).
- Ch.9 §9.6-§9.13 (switching regulators onward), Ch.10-15 and the appendices: outside the assigned range (parts 3-4).

## 1. Design rules
### Ch.5 §5.10-§5.14: choosing precision op-amps, auto-zero amplifiers, DMM front ends, difference amplifiers (pp. 319-356; lines 18822-21122)
| id | domain | rule statement | formula / limit (units) | inputs | applicability & conditions | verify by | source | conf |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| AOE-2001 | components | Choose FET-input (JFET/CMOS) op-amps for low input current and low current noise; choose bipolar-input op-amps for low offset voltage, low offset drift and low voltage noise. | selection rule: Ib/in-limited -> FET input; Vos/drift/en-limited -> BJT input | Ib budget (A), Vos budget (V), en budget (V/sqrt(Hz)), source resistance (ohm) | precision op-amp selection; exceptions: auto-zero CMOS (low Vos), superbeta BJT (low Ib) | review | p.319 §5.10.1 | low |
| AOE-2002 | components | World-class low voltage noise and world-class low input bias current cannot be had in one op-amp: the quietest parts run BJT inputs at high collector current, so Ib and current noise are high. | example LT1028A: en = 0.85 nV/sqrt(Hz) @1 kHz but Ib = 25 nA typ / 90 nA max, in = 4700 fA/sqrt(Hz) | en, Ib, in (datasheet) | op-amp selection trade-off | review | p.319 §5.10; Table 5.5 | medium |
| AOE-2003 | components | Micropower op-amps cannot settle fast: high fT and fast slew require supply current. | example LT6003: Iq = 0.001 mA, GBW = 0.003 MHz, SR = 0.001 V/us | Iq (mA), GBW (MHz), SR (V/us) | op-amp selection trade-off | review | p.319 §5.10; Table 5.5 | medium |
| AOE-2004 | components | Auto-zero (chopper-stabilized, zero-drift) op-amps have the smallest offset voltage and drift of any op-amp class. | Vos typically in the +/-1 uV range; drift in the +/-0.05 uV/degC range | Vos budget (uV), TCVos budget (uV/degC) | when budget is below what bipolar precision parts give; accept AZ artifacts (§5.11) | review | p.322 §5.10.1 | high |
| AOE-2005 | components | External offset-null, compensation and shutdown pins exist only on single-op-amp package types; duals are the more useful/popular choice (some distributors stock no singles). | n/a | package count, feature-pin needs | Table 5.5 selection | inspect | p.322 §5.10.2 | low |
| AOE-2006 | power | Include shutdown-pin current and wake time in power/timing budgets: LT6010 drops from 135 uA to 12 uA in shutdown, but the SHDN pin itself draws another 15 uA and ON/OFF takes 25 us; OPA364 draws 0.9 uA when off. | I_off,total = Iq,shdn + I_SHDNpin (uA); t_on = t_off = 25 us (LT6010) | Iq,shdn (uA), I_SHDNpin (uA), t_wake (us) | duty-cycled/battery designs using op-amp shutdown | calc | p.322 §5.10.3 | high |
| AOE-2007 | requirements | Run precision signal chains at a large full-scale level when supplies permit (e.g. +/-10 V full scale): a fixed offset is a smaller fraction of a larger span. Low-voltage op-amps give no offset-voltage improvement (except choppers). | error_frac = Vos / V_FS; Vos = 40 uV: 40 uV / 20 Vpp = 2 ppm vs 40 uV / 4 V = 10 ppm | Vos (V), V_FS (V) | precision dc amplifiers | calc | p.322 §5.10.3 | medium |
| AOE-2008 | components | Verify common-mode input range and output swing against the actual rails, not just the supply-voltage range: LT1007 (operates down to 4 V) keeps inputs and outputs no closer than 2 V from the rails (nearly useless at +/-2 V); LT1490 (44 V part) runs on 3 V with rail-to-rail inputs and outputs. | Vsig,min >= V- + headroom; Vsig,max <= V+ - headroom (inputs and output separately); LT1007 headroom = 2 V | V+, V- (V), input/output headroom (V), signal range (V) | any op-amp, especially at low supply | calc | p.322 §5.10.3 | high |
| AOE-2009 | components | A "single-supply" op-amp at minimum operates inputs and output to the negative rail (ground); expect degraded performance with the output near either rail. Zero-volt/below-rail output may need an added pulldown resistor with a minimum pull-down current (OPA364: 0.5 mA). | I_pulldown >= I_min (OPA364: 0.5 mA) | output swing target (V), I_pulldown (mA) | single-supply ground-referenced outputs | calc | p.322-323 §5.10.4, fn 33 | high |
| AOE-2010 | reliability | MOSFET-input op-amps can shift offset over years from sodium-ion migration / phosphorus polarization in the gate insulator, worse at high temperature and large differential input; avoid holding large dc differential voltage on MOSFET inputs in long-life precision designs, or use phosphorus-doped-gate parts (TI LinCMOS). | worst case 0.5 mV over years; datasheet typ 5 mV Vos change after 3000 h at 125 degC with 2 V across the input; LinCMOS: 50 uV eventual drift per volt of differential input | V_diff,dc (V), T (degC), life (h) | CMOS op-amps and comparators under closed-loop or differential stress | review | p.322 fn 32 | high |
| AOE-2011 | components | Low supply current severely limits other parameters: precision op-amps exist down to 10-60 uA, and 0.85 uA at 1.8 V (LT6003); JFET op-amps offer no low-power parts. Low Iq also reduces self-heating drift. | Iq down to 0.85 uA (LT6003) | Iq budget (uA) | battery and low-drift designs | review | p.322-323 §5.10.3-5.10.4 | high |
| AOE-2012 | requirements | Budget precision errors with maximum (guaranteed) specs, not typicals: "<10 uV typical" offsets are common but typical is not reliable in a manufactured instrument. Table 5.5 admission required Vos,max of about 250 uV or less. | Vos,max <= 250 uV (precision class) | Vos,max (uV) | precision op-amp classification | review | p.323 §5.10.5 | high |
| AOE-2013 | test | Measure small op-amp offsets with a x1000-gain fixture (Fig. 5.36) using small (10 ohm) resistances at the DUT inputs so input-current effects are negligible; add a 200 ohm output resistor to drive a cable. | Vos = Vout / 1000; R at inputs = 10 ohm; R_out = 200 ohm | Vout (V) | offset characterization | measure | p.323 Fig. 5.36 | high |
| AOE-2014 | components | Superbeta BJT-input op-amps give JFET-like low input current with better high-temperature Ib and better offset/drift than FET parts, but none has inputs operating to either supply rail. | n/a | Ib(T), CM range | high-temperature precision | review | p.323 §5.10.5; p.325 §5.10.7, Fig. 5.38 | low |
| AOE-2015 | components | Check offset versus common-mode voltage for RRIO op-amps (not captured in selection tables); parts with charge-pump-powered input stages (OPA364, MAX9617) eliminate the input-pair transition problem. | dVos over Vcm range from datasheet (Fig. 5.29) | Vcm range (V), Vos(Vcm) | RRIO op-amps | review | p.323 §5.10.5 | low |
| AOE-2016 | requirements | Offset drift with temperature is not production tested and max TCVos specs may be unreliable (some makers no longer specify it); characterize if the budget depends on it. | n/a | TCVos (uV/degC) | precision over temperature | measure | p.323 §5.10.5 | low |
| AOE-2017 | components | Long-term offset drift: typical precision datasheet values 300-400 nV/month; LT1007 200 nV/month; chopper-stabilized ~50 nV/month; 6 nV/month measured (LMP2011). Drift may be random-walk-like, so a spec in nV/sqrt(month) may be more apt. | Vos_drift(t) ~ k * sqrt(t) (random-walk hypothesis), t in months | k (nV/sqrt(month)), t (months) | calibration-interval budgeting | calc | p.323 §5.10.5, fn 34 | medium |
| AOE-2018 | components | Op-amp voltage-noise density is flat above the 1/f corner fc and rises approximately as sqrt(1/f) below it; auto-zero op-amps stay flat at low frequency. | en(f) ~ en_w * sqrt(fc/f) for f < fc; en(f) = en_w for f > fc | en_w (nV/sqrt(Hz)), fc (Hz) | conventional (non-AZ) op-amps | calc | p.323 §5.10.6, Fig. 5.37 | high |
| AOE-2019 | components | Integrated noise over a band is the square root of the integral of en^2; in the white region it is en times sqrt(bandwidth). | Vn^2 = integral from fa to fb of en(f)^2 df; Vn = en * sqrt(BW) (white) | en (V/sqrt(Hz)), fa, fb, BW (Hz) | any noise budget | calc | p.324 §5.10.6 | high |
| AOE-2020 | components | For bandwidths of 1-10 kHz or more, use en at the high-frequency end of the band (or a frequency-weighted average) in Vn = en*sqrt(BW), not the 1 kHz value; critical for transimpedance amplifiers, whose en-C current noise rises with frequency. | in,eq = en * 2*pi*f*Cin | en(f_high) (V/sqrt(Hz)), Cin (F), f (Hz) | wideband and TIA circuits | calc | p.324 §5.10.6 | high |
| AOE-2021 | components | For low-frequency precision amplifiers rank op-amps by integrated noise including the 1/f corner, not by en at 1 kHz: LT1012 (14 nV/sqrt(Hz), fc = 2.5 Hz) is quieter than OPA277 (8 nV/sqrt(Hz), fc = 20 Hz) with a ~1 Hz cutoff; OPA277 is dramatically quieter than TLC2272 (9 nV/sqrt(Hz), fc = 330 Hz). | compare Vn = sqrt(integral en^2 df) over 0.01 Hz .. f_hi | en_w, fc, f_hi | low-frequency precision | calc | p.324 §5.10.6A, Fig. 5.54 | high |
| AOE-2022 | components | Use the 0.1-10 Hz peak-to-peak noise Vnpp as the "dc noise" figure of merit and primary clue to long-term drift. Values range up to 11 uVpp; LT1028 best at 35 nVpp; LMP7731 80 nVpp (RRIO, SOT-23); ADA4075 60 nVpp. | Vnpp(0.1-10 Hz) (nV) | Vnpp | precision dc | review | p.325 §5.10.6 | high |
| AOE-2023 | components | To extend a 0.1-10 Hz Vnpp spec to a lower start frequency (e.g. 0.01 Hz), multiply the listed value by the square root of the number of additional low-end decades wanted (as stated in the text; 1/f noise has equal power per decade); valid when the 1/f corner is several decades higher, so white noise can be ignored. | Vnpp,new = Vnpp(0.1-10 Hz) * sqrt(N_additional_decades) (as stated) | Vnpp (V), N decades | 1/f-dominated op-amps (not auto-zero) | calc | p.325 §5.10.6 | medium |
| AOE-2024 | components | Parts with very low typical Ib often have poor guaranteed maxima because automated testing below ~10 pA is hard: LMC6482A 20 fA typ vs 4 pA max (200x worse); LMP7721 has a 20 fA max (over $10). Budget with the max value. | Ib,max / Ib,typ up to 200 | Ib,typ, Ib,max (A) | fA-pA input designs | review | p.325 §5.10.7 | high |
| AOE-2025 | components | JFET and CMOS op-amp "bias current" is leakage that rises exponentially with temperature; evaluate Ib at the maximum operating temperature (Fig. 5.38), not at 25 degC. | Ib(T_max) from datasheet curve | T_max (degC), Ib(T) | FET-input op-amps at elevated temperature | review | p.325 §5.10.7, Fig. 5.38 | medium |
| AOE-2026 | components | Low-input-current op-amps generally have higher Vos, drift and noise. Exceptions: OPA627/ADA4627 JFET (expensive), OPA827 (lower noise, cheaper); CMOS LMP7715 5.8 nV/sqrt(Hz) is the best low-noise CMOS; AD8616 (1 pA bias, 7 nV/sqrt(Hz)) is a low-cost compromise; AD743 2.9 nV/sqrt(Hz) but 1 mV offset. | n/a | en, Ib, Vos | low-Ib selection | review | p.325 §5.10.7 | medium |
| AOE-2027 | components | High-speed op-amps typically have input bias currents of 200 nA to 20 uA and offsets above 0.5 mV. Exceptions: OPA656/657 LV JFET (2 pA typ Ib, 290 V/us, 20 ns settling); OPA380 90 MHz TIA op-amp (50 pA and 25 uV max offset via auto-zero with an isolating filter against excess current noise). | Ib = 200 nA .. 20 uA; Vos > 0.5 mV (typical high-speed) | Ib, Vos | fast precision / TIA | review | p.325 §5.10.7 | high |
| AOE-2028 | test | Bias-current test (Fig. 5.39): measure Ib with one source resistor shorted, offset current with Rs1 = Rs2; choose Rs so Ib*Rs is at least some tens of mV so offset-voltage errors can be ignored; 200 ohm output resistor for cables. | Ib * Rs >= "some 10s of mV" | expected Ib (A), Rs (ohm) | nA-level input currents | measure | p.326 Fig. 5.39 | high |
| AOE-2029 | test | For pA-and-below Ib, integrate the current (1 fA across 1 Gohm is only uV, swamped by offsets): integrator built with a fA-input op-amp (LMP7721: Ib = 3 fA typ, 20 fA max), feedback capacitor made from a short Teflon-dielectric shielded cable (standard 50 ohm coax is almost exactly 1 pF/cm), reset by electromechanical relay (not MOS switch, e.g. COTO 9202). | Ib = C * dVout/dt; C ~ 1 pF/cm * length (cm) | C (pF), ramp rate (V/s) | pA and fA currents | measure | p.326 Fig. 5.40A | high |
| AOE-2030 | test | Simplest fA test: DUT as follower with a small capacitor from (+) input to ground; read the ramp at the output. Mica and film capacitors showed memory effects, an air variable capacitor worked; reset by collapsing the supply rails; enclose the setup in a metal box. | Ib = C*dV/dt; example 365 pF x 0.20 mV/s = 73 fA (LMC6482) | C (F), dV/dt (V/s) | fA Ib measurement | measure | p.326 Fig. 5.40B | high |
| AOE-2031 | components | Current noise matters when source impedance approaches the op-amp noise impedance Zn = en/in; it can be ignored only when Zs << Zn. Typical in: 0.1-50 fA/sqrt(Hz) (CMOS, JFET), up to 5 pA/sqrt(Hz) (low-noise BJT at high Ib), 20 fA/sqrt(Hz) (superbeta LT1012). LT1028: Zn = 850 ohm, so keep circuit resistances at 300 ohm or less to realize its en; LT1013: Zn = 315 kohm. | Zn = en/in (ohm); e_eq = in * Zs; pass if Zs << Zn | en (V/sqrt(Hz)), in (A/sqrt(Hz)), Zs (ohm) | op-amp noise design | calc | p.326 §5.10.8 | high |
| AOE-2032 | components | Do not rely on op-amp current-noise values printed in parentheses (typically BJT parts): measured values are often 5x-100x larger. | in,design = (5..100) x in,parenthesized | in (A/sqrt(Hz)) | BJT op-amp current noise | review | p.321 Table 5.5 note (o) | high |
| AOE-2033 | components | The current-noise 1/f corner is typically much higher than the voltage-noise corner (OPA277: 20 Hz voltage vs 200 Hz current; LT1007: 2 Hz vs 120 Hz), so the noise impedance Zn falls at low frequency: LT1028 Zn drops to 212 ohm at 10 Hz, requiring circuit resistances of 100 ohm or less there. | Zn(f) = en(f)/in(f); require Zs << Zn(f_low) | en(f), in(f), Zs | low-frequency low-noise design | calc | p.327 §5.10.8 | high |
| AOE-2034 | components | Input current noise has a shot-noise lower bound set by the input (bias or leakage) current; many datasheets simply list this calculated value for the typical Ib. | in = sqrt(2*q*Ib), q = 1.602e-19 C; Ib = 10 pA -> in = 1.8 fA/sqrt(Hz) (scale by sqrt(Ib)); LT1013 Ib = 12 nA -> 62 fA/sqrt(Hz) calc vs 70 fA/sqrt(Hz) spec | Ib (A) | any op-amp input (minimum in) | calc | p.327 §5.10.8 | high |
| AOE-2035 | components | For BJT op-amps with input bias-current cancellation, current noise is set by the uncancelled base current, not the net Ib: compute shot noise of the uncancelled base current times sqrt(2) (for the cancellation current). LT1007: 56 fA/sqrt(Hz) calculated from 10 nA vs 400 fA/sqrt(Hz) spec (7x); LT1028: ~10x (1000 vs 90 fA/sqrt(Hz)); LT6010 (Ib = 20 pA): 2.5 fA/sqrt(Hz) calculated vs 100 fA/sqrt(Hz) spec (40x). | in ~ sqrt(2) * sqrt(2*q*I_B,uncancelled) | I_B,uncancelled (A) | bias-cancelled BJT op-amps (see Table 8.3a bias-cancel column) | calc | p.327 §5.10.8 | high |
| AOE-2036 | components | Treat datasheet current-noise values that were computed from the net (cancelled) Ib, or from AZ low-frequency specs, as optimistic; measure in when it matters: LT1012 datasheet 6 fA/sqrt(Hz) vs measured ~55 fA/sqrt(Hz); AD8628A spec 5 fA/sqrt(Hz) vs measured 30x larger; MCP6V06 spec 0.6 fA/sqrt(Hz) vs measured 170 fA/sqrt(Hz). | in,measured/in,datasheet = 10x (LT1012), 30x (AD8628A), ~280x (MCP6V06) | in (datasheet), source Z | high-Z / low-current precision inputs | measure | p.327-328 §5.10.8, fn 41 | high |
| AOE-2037 | components | Chopper/auto-zero op-amp current-noise specs are usually given at 10 Hz, below the region of very high switch charge-injection current noise; expect far higher in at higher frequencies (Fig. 5.52). | in spec frequency = 10 Hz (AZ) vs 1 kHz/10 kHz (conventional) | in(f) | auto-zero op-amps with high source impedance | review | p.328 §5.10.8; Table 5.5 note (k) | high |
| AOE-2038 | components | Bias-current-cancelled op-amps generally lack rail-to-rail input stages, and near-rail inputs can degrade them: LT1677 has high bias current and degraded offset in the bottom 1.4 V and top 0.7 V of its common-mode range. | keep Vcm >= V- + 1.4 V and <= V+ - 0.7 V (LT1677) | Vcm range (V) | bias-cancelled BJT op-amps | calc | p.327 fn 40 | high |
| AOE-2039 | components | Evaluate CMRR at the signal frequency and over the actual common-mode range, not just the dc minimum: dc CMRR spans 70 dB (LMC6482) to 130 dB (OPA277); LMC6482 falls above 1 kHz to 80 dB at 10 kHz; OPA277 and AD8622 also degrade to ~80 dB by 10 kHz; LT1007 holds 114 dB typ at 10 kHz. The CMRR spec often applies only to a limited common-mode range. | Vos,cm = dVcm / 10^(CMRR_dB/20) at f | dVcm (V), CMRR(f) (dB) | non-inverting and difference circuits | calc | p.328 §5.10.9 | high |
| AOE-2040 | components | Prefer the inverting configuration to eliminate common-mode swing at the op-amp inputs: the time-honored cure for CMRR errors, RRIO input-stage offset shifts (LT6200: ~1 mV shifts within 1.5 V of the rail) and JFET input-capacitance distortion. | inverting: Vcm = const | topology | precision and low-distortion circuits where inverting gain is acceptable | review | p.328 §5.10.9; p.329 fn 43; p.330 | low |
| AOE-2041 | power | Budget PSRR at dc and at ripple/crosstalk frequencies, per rail: dc PSRR typically 60-80 dB (LMC6482) up to 130 dB (OPA277), 100 dB (AD8622); ac PSRR is often much worse on one rail (compensation capacitor referenced to it; OPA277 is an extra 25 dB worse on the negative rail). ac PSRR matters at 100-120 Hz (and harmonics) and at high frequency (crosstalk). Add an RC filter in the supply rails of sensitive low-level input stages. | Vos,ps = dVs / 10^(PSRR_dB/20) per rail at f | dVs ripple (V) per rail, PSRR(f) (dB) | low-level input stages | calc | p.328 §5.10.9 | high |
| AOE-2042 | components | Slew rate scales with gain-bandwidth for a given input-stage design. | S (V/us) = 0.32 * m * fT (MHz); m = input-stage factor per §4x.9 (m = 1 for LT6200: 165 MHz -> ~50 V/us) | fT (MHz), m | voltage-feedback op-amps | calc | p.328 §5.10.10; p.329 | high |
| AOE-2043 | components | Full-power bandwidth is set by slew rate: a sine of amplitude A has peak slew omega*A. | FPBW = S / (pi * Vpp) (Hz), S in V/s | S (V/s), Vpp (V) | large-signal sine outputs | calc | p.328 §5.10.10 | high |
| AOE-2044 | components | The first component of settling time is the slewing delay; higher fT and slew rate are often the primary determinant of fast settling. | t_slew = dV / S | dV step (V), S (V/s) | settling-time budgets | calc | p.328 §5.10.10 | high |
| AOE-2045 | control-loop | GBW is the gain x frequency product in the -6 dB/octave region (extrapolated 0 dB crossing); true fT (where open-loop gain = 1) is lower because of higher poles. For unity-gain-compensated op-amps fT ~ GBW; decompensated op-amps are stable only above a minimum closed-loop gain (THS4021: gains > 10; phase reaches 180 deg by 400 MHz). | noise gain >= G_min (decompensated part) | closed-loop noise gain, G_min | decompensated op-amps | calc | p.328-329 §5.10.10A, Fig. 5.42 | high |
| AOE-2046 | components | High fT carries costs; check them: LT6200 (165 MHz GBW, 0.95 nV/sqrt(Hz), RRIO) has 1.0 mV offset, 50 V/us slew (m = 1), 140 ns settling, 10 V (+/-5 V) max supply, 16.5 mA supply current, 40 uA max bias current, and ~1 mV offset shifts within 1.5 V of the rail. | n/a | Vos, Ib, Iq, supply range | high-speed op-amp selection | review | p.329 §5.10.10B, fn 43 | high |
| AOE-2047 | components | Fast op-amps that keep precision-ish inputs: OPA656 (230 MHz, 290 V/us, 20 ns settling, 2 pA JFET inputs, <3 pF input capacitance, 1.8 mV Vos, 7 nV/sqrt(Hz), <0.1% distortion to 10 MHz) for photodiode TIAs; OPA637 (4.5 nV/sqrt(Hz), 7 pF, 80 MHz); LT1468 (90 MHz, 75 uV, +/-15 V, 0.7 ppm distortion at 10 V, 0.8 us settling) for ADC drive; LMP7717 CMOS (88 MHz, 1 mA, 1.8 V min supply, 1 pA, 6 nV/sqrt(Hz), 150 uV). | n/a | GBW, SR, ts, Cin, Vos, en | TIA and ADC-driver selection | review | p.329 §5.10.10B | high |
| AOE-2048 | test | Normalize test conditions before comparing op-amp distortion curves: vendors use different levels (2 Vpp, 3 Vrms, 10 V peak, 20 Vpp), loads (100 ohm, 600 ohm, 2k, 10k, open), common-mode voltages, analyzer filters, gains, and THD vs THD+N vs single harmonics. A flat distortion region continuing past the op-amp's primary pole indicates the instrument noise floor, not the part. | n/a | level, load, Vcm, gain, filter, metric | distortion-critical selection | review | p.330 §5.10.11A, fn 44 | high |
| AOE-2049 | components | Low-voltage RRO op-amps must run at lower signal levels, so their THD+N suffers from noise: OPA1641 (HV JFET, RRO) winner at 0.5 ppm tested with 8.5 Vpp; OPA376 low-voltage winner at 3 ppm tested with 2.8 Vpp (both Monticelli output stages). | n/a | signal level (Vpp), THD+N | low-distortion selection | review | p.330 §5.10.11; p.318 fn 28 | medium |
| AOE-2050 | components | JFET op-amp input capacitance varies with common-mode voltage, adding distortion when driven from a source resistance in non-inverting mode: OPA1641 (8 pF, <1 ppm at 1 kHz) reaches just over 20 ppm at 100 kHz non-inverting and 100 ppm with Rs = 600 ohm. Match impedances at both inputs, or better use the inverting configuration. | THD(100 kHz): ~20 ppm (Rs ~ 0) -> 100 ppm (Rs = 600 ohm) | Rs (ohm), f (Hz), Cin(Vcm) | JFET op-amps, non-inverting, high frequency | measure | p.330 §5.10.11A, Fig. 5.45 | high |
| AOE-2051 | test | To measure sub-100 ppm op-amp distortion, reduce loop gain by x100 with a resistor network that keeps signal gain = 1 (Fig. 5.46), then divide the measured distortion by 100; beware the artificially low source impedance of this fixture and any claim near 1 ppm ("If you think you've measured something to 1 ppm, you're probably wrong"). | THD_reported = THD_measured / 100 | THD_measured | ppm-level distortion testing | measure | p.330-332 §5.10.11A, Fig. 5.46 | medium |
| AOE-2052 | control-loop | Build a composite amplifier when no single op-amp has both the needed input and output specs: one overall feedback path sets gain; a robust scheme lets the second amp's gain fall to unity well above the first op-amp's fT (§4x.5). Very high overall gain (e.g. G = 10,000) may need no special compensation. | f_unity(A2 local) >> fT(A1) | fT(A1), A2 loop | precision + power / high-voltage outputs | sim | p.332 §5.10.12 | medium |
| AOE-2053 | components | Size driver current for capacitive loads from the required slew rate: 1 V/us into a 1.5 uF piezo pair needs 1.5 A. | I = C * dV/dt | C (F), dV/dt (V/s) | capacitive loads (piezos, cables) | calc | p.332 §5.10.12A | high |
| AOE-2054 | requirements | Keep op-amp input errors below 1 LSB of the driving DAC: Ib * Rout_DAC < V_LSB and Vos < V_LSB. DAC8831 16-bit, 5 V full scale: LSB = 76 uV; with Rout = 6.25 kohm the op-amp needs Ib < 12 nA; target Vos < 75 uV. | V_LSB = V_FS / 2^N; Ib,max = V_LSB / Rout | V_FS (V), N (bits), Rout (ohm) | DAC buffer / precision drive | calc | p.332 §5.10.12A | high |
| AOE-2055 | control-loop | Limit a piezo-positioner loop bandwidth to about 20% of the piezo's mechanical self-resonant frequency (25 kHz chosen in the example). | f_BW <= 0.2 * f_res,mech | f_res,mech (Hz) | piezo actuator drivers | calc | p.332 §5.10.12A | high |
| AOE-2056 | derating | Check the power driver's safe-operating-area against the load: Apex PB51 (300 V, 1.5 A) is limited to 130 V drop when driving 2 A for 100 ms. | AOE-2057 | control-loop | Composite driver for a capacitive load (Fig. 5.47): isolate the precision input op-amp from the output amplifier above the loop bandwidth with a feedback capacitor (it acts as a follower at high speed); slow the DAC step with an RC (10 us time constant) so the band-limited amp is not jolted; add a series RC across the output to cut HF open-loop gain and add lossy damping (as in audio power amps). | tau_input = 10 us; f_iso ~ loop BW (25 kHz) | load C, loop BW | capacitive-load composite amplifiers | sim | p.333 §5.10.12A, Fig. 5.47 | medium |

| AOE-2059 | components | Piezo positioners driven by voltage show nonlinearity and hysteresis; charge (current) drive ameliorates this. Example stack moves 6 um per 100 V, 0.75 uF per element, 1.5 uF pair, 100 V full scale. | n/a | drive mode | precision piezo positioning | review | p.332-333 §5.10.12A, fn 46, Fig. 5.47 | low |

| AOE-2062 | components | Auto-zero clock charge injection produces output spikes that datasheet noise specs hide (they quote Rs = 100 ohm and dc-1 Hz, e.g. 0.3 uVpp): with zero input the output may be a train of 5 us wide, 10 mV spikes of alternating polarity. Evaluate AZ amps over the full circuit bandwidth in the time domain. | spike ~10 mV x 5 us at output (G = 100 test) | circuit BW, gain | auto-zero op-amps in wideband or sampled systems | measure | p.334 §5.11.1, Fig. 5.49 | medium |
| AOE-2063 | components | Auto-zero switching also injects input-current spikes, so input-referred spikes grow with source resistance (compare Rs = 100 ohm vs 1 Mohm, G = 100); keep source impedance low or filter. | spike_in ~ i_spike * Rs | Rs (ohm) | AZ op-amps with high-Z sources | measure | p.334 §5.11.1, Figs. 5.49-5.50 | medium |
| AOE-2064 | components | Measured auto-zero input-current spikes are nA-scale regardless of fA-level datasheet specs: LTC1150 8 nA p-p spikes; MCP6V06 1 nA p-p noise (spec 0.6 fA/sqrt(Hz) at 10 Hz; measured in ~400x the spec); LMP2021 0.2 nA p-p "rumble" (spec 0.35 pA/sqrt(Hz)). Datasheet in values computed as dc-current shot noise can be wrong by x10 to x100. | e_spike,in = i_pp * Rs (V p-p) | i_pp (A), Rs (ohm) | AZ op-amps with source resistance | measure | p.336 fn 49, fn 51, Fig. 5.52 | high |
| AOE-2065 | components | Classic auto-zero architecture (Fig. 5.48: LTC1150, MCP6V06) shows more clock feedthrough than newer designs intended to reduce it (AD8628A ping-pong auto-zero + chopping with a 15 kHz clock; LMP2021). Fixed-clock AZ parts produce sharp spectral lines (AD8551 ~4 kHz; MCP6V06 9 kHz and harmonics) that can intermodulate with nearby signals; spread-spectrum clocks (AD8571) suppress the lines. | keep signal band and its IM products away from f_clk and harmonics | f_clk (Hz), signal band (Hz) | AZ op-amps in ac/narrowband systems | review | p.336 §5.11.1, fn 50, Figs. 5.51-5.52 | medium |
| AOE-2066 | filter | For low-frequency auto-zero applications, RC-lowpass the output to a few hundred hertz to suppress the clock spikes; spiky input-current noise is unimportant with low source impedance, in integrating applications (integrating ADCs) or with intrinsically slow outputs (thermocouple with meter). | f_c(output RC) ~ a few hundred Hz | f_c (Hz) | AZ op-amps, dc/slow signals | inspect | p.337 §5.11.1 | high |
| AOE-2067 | components | Choose an auto-zero op-amp over a conventional low-noise op-amp only when the output bandwidth is very low (below ~1 Hz): AZ parts have ~50 nV/sqrt(Hz) wideband noise at 1 kHz (vs a few nV/sqrt(Hz)) but stay flat at low frequency, whereas 1/f noise diverges (LT1007: 2.5 nV/sqrt(Hz) at 1 kHz, 2 Hz corner -> ~100 nV/sqrt(Hz) at 0.001 Hz; AD8551 flat 42 nV/sqrt(Hz)). Compare integrated noise over the actual band (Fig. 5.54). | en(0.001 Hz) ~ en_w * sqrt(fc/0.001 Hz); AZ integrated noise ~ en * sqrt(BW) | en_w, fc, BW | dc/very-low-frequency measurement | calc | p.337 §5.11.1, Figs. 5.53-5.54 | high |
| AOE-2068 | components | Auto-zero op-amps recover slowly from output saturation (the nulling loop charges its correction capacitors to a large voltage): up to several milliseconds. Prevent saturation (sense near-saturation and clamp the input, or bridge the feedback network with back-to-back zeners, best in the inverting configuration), or choose fast-recovery parts: OPA378 tr = 4 us, OPA734 tr = 8 us (vs LTC1050 3000 us, LTC2054 4000 us, LTC1049 6000 us, LTC1150 20 ms). | tr (overload recovery) from Table 5.6; clamp Vout < Vsat with zeners | tr (us), expected overload events | AZ op-amps in circuits that can saturate | review | p.337-338 §5.11.1, Table 5.6 | high |

| AOE-2070 | components | Auto-zero op-amps are almost all low-voltage (5.5-6 V max, many down to 2 V or less); only five of those tabulated run from +/-5 V and one (LTC1150) from +/-15 V. | Vs,max 5.5-6 V typical | supply (V) | AZ selection | review | p.338 §5.11.3, Table 5.6 | high |
| AOE-2071 | components | RRIO input stages can have large offset in the input-pair crossover region even when the headline Vos is small: AD8616 Vos is 60 uV max only at Vcm = 0.5 V and 3.0 V (Vs = 5.5 V) but as much as 400 uV near Vcm = 2 V. Keep Vcm out of the crossover region or use charge-pump / auto-zero parts. | Vos(Vcm near crossover) up to 400 uV (AD8616) | Vcm (V), Vs (V) | RRIO CMOS op-amps | calc | p.335 Table 5.6 note (f) | high |

At high temperature AZ parts usually beat JFETs: LMP2021 Ib stays below 75 pA at 125 degC (any Vcm) vs OPA124 0.5 nA and LF412 10 nA. | Ib,max(AZ) up to 0.5 nA @25 degC | Ib(T) (A), T (degC) | AZ selection, hot environments | review | p.339 §5.11.3 | high |
| AOE-2073 | components | With gigaohm source impedance, AZ bias current can depend on input shunt capacitance: LMP2021 with Rs = 1 Gohm varies from -25 to +25 pA as Cs goes 2-500 pF, crossing zero at Cs = 22 pF; 25 pA into 1 Gohm is 25 mV of offset. In a high-Rf transimpedance amp a large feedback capacitor Cf dramatically reduces the bias-current error. | V_err = Ib(Cs) * Rs | Rs (ohm), Cs (F) | AZ op-amps with Gohm sources / TIAs | measure | p.339 fn 52 | high |

Use symmetrical wiring and component layout, pair junctions isothermally, and shield from drafts and gradients. | V_thermal = S_pair * dT (S_pair per Table §2) | junction metals, dT across pair (degC) | sub-uV dc circuits | inspect | p.339 §5.11.3; p.340-341 §5.11.4B | high |

Avoid Kovar leads and oxidized copper at low-level junctions. | S(Cu-X) per table (uV/degC) | junction metal pairs | sub-uV dc measurement, relays, connectors | review | p.341 §5.11.4B | high |

Very-long-term extrapolation is eventually limited by other drift sources (impurity diffusion). | Vn ~ en * sqrt(f_LP) | en, f_LP (Hz) | AZ noise budgeting | calc | p.339-340 §5.11.3 | medium |

Current-noise voltage = in * Rs * sqrt(BW): 0.6 fA/sqrt(Hz) through 1 Gohm in 10 Hz -> 2 uV, about equal to a 1.7 uV voltage noise. | Vn,i = in * Rs * sqrt(BW); in >= sqrt(2*q*Ib) | in (A/sqrt(Hz)), Rs (ohm), BW (Hz) | AZ with high-Z sources | calc | p.339-340 §5.11.3 | high |

Headline specs may hold only over restricted Vcm: OPA335 130 dB CMRR for (V-) - 0.1 V < Vcm < (V+) - 1.5 V, and its 1 uV Vos at Vcm = Vs/2. | check Vcm window of each spec | Vcm range (V) | AZ selection | review | p.340 §5.11.3 | high |

| AOE-2085 | control-loop | External auto-zero (Fig. 5.56): use an AZ op-amp integrator to sense the summing-junction error of a high-voltage/high-power/high-speed op-amp and feed an attenuated (x100) correction to its non-inverting input. Use a long time constant (offsets drift slowly; also needed to prevent loop oscillation). Example: 10 uV error -> -100 uV/s integrator slope -> -1 uV/s correction; divider gives +/-10 mV range for +/-1 V integrator swing, cutting LM675's +/-10 mV max offset by ~1000x (also THS4011: 200 MHz, 300 V/us, +/-6 mV). Add an RnCn filter at the AZ output with low-noise parts (THS4011 7.5 nV/sqrt(Hz)); watch the main amp's bias current; most natural in the inverting configuration. | dV/dt = -dV_err/(R*C); correction range = V_int,max / attenuation (+/-1 V / 100 = +/-10 mV) | Vos,max of main amp, R, C, attenuation | offset trim of power/fast op-amps | sim | p.341-342 §5.11.4E, Fig. 5.56 | high |

| AOE-2088 | requirements | When firmware can take zero and full-scale calibration readings, specify and design the front end for offset/gain stability over the measurement interval rather than low absolute offset; average many calibrate-measure cycles (34420A: up to 2 minutes) to beat down scatter. | requirement on drift during t_meas, not on Vos | t_meas (s), drift (V/s) | microcontroller-calibrated instruments | review | p.342-343 §5.12.2, fn 57 | low |
| AOE-2089 | components | JFET-enhanced op-amp: put a discrete dual-JFET stage ahead of a low-noise BJT op-amp. OP-27 alone: 120 dB gain, 0.2 uV/degC, 3 nV/sqrt(Hz), but +/-15 nA input current and 1.7 pA/sqrt(Hz) current noise; a JFET source-follower pair cures Ib and in at the cost of 40 uV/degC offset drift and 10 nV/sqrt(Hz) added noise (34401A: Rin > 10 Gohm at gains x100/x10/x1); a JFET common-source differential pair adds loop gain and lowers noise (34420A). | Rin > 10 Gohm (100 mV-10 V ranges); 10 Mohm with 100 V/1000 V attenuator | en, in, Ib, drift of each option | Gohm-input precision amplifiers | review | p.343 §5.12.4, Fig. 5.58 | high |
| AOE-2090 | components | Bootstrap the drains of a JFET input pair so VDS (about 1-2 V) and ID stay constant as the input swings: the pair's poor offset (Vos up to 40 mV) and gate leakage then do not vary with signal over the full +/-15 V input range. Screen parts so Q1's VGS at 0.7 mA is below Q2's VGS at 1.4 mA (that difference is Q1's VDS). | VDS = const ~1-2 V; VGS(Q1 @0.7 mA) < VGS(Q2 @1.4 mA) | VGS spread, input range | discrete JFET front ends | inspect | p.343-344 §5.12.4, Fig. 5.59, fn 59 | high |
| AOE-2091 | components | In low-distortion precision discrete stages, keep every transistor's operating point (VDS or VCE, and ID) unchanged as the signal changes (bootstrap drains, pin op-amp inputs, cascode), and run JFETs at low VDS to cut gate leakage and self-heating. | dVDS/dVin = 0; dID/dVin = 0 | topology | discrete precision front ends | sim | p.346 §5.12.5B | low |
| AOE-2092 | grounding | At nV resolution, return currents through shared ground resistance must be cancelled: 1 mA (offset DAC, 5 V across 5 kohm) through 0.1 mohm shifts "ground" by 100 nV; injecting an equal and opposite balancing current into the same ground node, matched to 1%, reduces the error to 1 nV. | V_err = I_return * R_gnd * mismatch_fraction | I_return (A), R_gnd (ohm), current match (%) | sub-uV measurement grounds | calc | p.345 §5.12.5A, Fig. 5.62 | high |
| AOE-2093 | components | Split a gain-setting attenuator into two cascaded sections with a precision buffer between them: the buffer's drift is divided by the first section's attenuation (x100 on the most sensitive 34420A range), and its offset can be read and cancelled in the measurement cycle. | drift_referred = drift_buffer / attenuation_1 | attenuation (x), buffer drift (V/degC) | precision range-switching feedback networks | calc | p.345 §5.12.5A, Fig. 5.62 | medium |
| AOE-2094 | control-loop | Compensating a JFET differential input stage with Cc: fT = gm_diff / (2*pi*Cc), gm_diff = gm/2 undegenerated. IF3602 at ID = 2 mA (subthreshold, IDSS typ 300 mA): gm ~ 60 mS (a BJT would give 80 mS) -> fT = 1.5 MHz, open-loop gain ~10^6 at 1 Hz. For low closed-loop gains switch in source degeneration (500 ohm): gm_diff = 1/(2*Rs) = 1 mS -> fT = 50 kHz for stability. | fT = gm_diff/(2*pi*Cc); gm_diff = 1/(2*Rs) with degeneration | gm (S), Cc (F), Rs (ohm) | discrete-front-end composite amplifiers | calc | p.346 §5.12.5C, Fig. 5.63 | high |
| AOE-2095 | test | Averaging N independent readings reduces random (white) noise by sqrt(N): 100 PLC integration (1.67 s, ~0.6 Hz cutoff) gives ~3 nV rms with IF3602 JFETs (en = 0.4 nV/sqrt(Hz) each, x1.4 for the pair); averaging 64 readings over 2 minutes gives 8x lower, ~0.4 nV (Agilent specifies 1.3 nV allowing for non-random errors). 1/f noise limits the benefit. | sigma_avg = sigma / sqrt(N); f_c ~ 1/T_int | sigma (V rms), N, T_int (s) | dc precision measurement | calc | p.346 §5.12.5D | high |
| AOE-2096 | components | Tail/sink current-source noise: a single-ended stage needs in(source) < en(amp)*gm, i.e. allowable reference noise en(ref) <= en(amp)*gm*Rs (ratio 37 -> 11 nV/sqrt(Hz) against a 0.3 nV/sqrt(Hz) JFET); a differential pair cancels sink noise only to the matching of its halves (better than 5% with 1% mirror resistors), and a 10 nF capacitor defeats the cancellation above ~10 Hz. | en(ref)/en(amp) = gm*Rs | en(amp), gm (S), Rs (ohm) | low-noise discrete front ends | calc | p.346-347 §5.12.5D, fn 60 | medium |
| AOE-2097 | process | When worst-case specs cannot meet the product spec (JFET pair worst-case gate leakage 500 pA at 25 degC vs 50 pA instrument input-current spec), screen parts (have the supplier screen or do it in-house), with no guarantee of process continuity or continued availability; consider a lifetime buy of the critical part. | screened limit <= product limit (50 pA) vs datasheet 500 pA | datasheet max, product spec | critical-parameter parts | review | p.347 §5.12.5E | high |
| AOE-2098 | components | Choose the differential topology by requirement: difference amplifier (op-amp + two matched resistor pairs): CMRR 90-100 dB, Gv = 0.1-10, input impedance 25-100 kohm, needs a low-impedance source, inputs can typically exceed the rails; instrumentation amplifier: 10 Mohm-10 Gohm input, Gv = 1-1000, CMRR 110-140 dB at Gv = 100; fully differential amplifier: differential output, low voltage, fast settling, wideband (twisted-pair drivers, fast differential ADCs). | see §2 table | Zs, gain, CMRR, output type | differential signal chains | review | p.347 §5.13 | high |
| AOE-2099 | requirements | Size the required CMRR as common-mode level over allowed error: strain-gauge bridge (~350 ohm arms, +/-2 mV/V -> +/-10 mV full scale at 5 V excitation, riding on +2.5 V): 0.1% FS error = +/-0.02 mV on 2500 mV -> 62,500:1 = 96 dB; with a zero calibration only bias-voltage variations must be rejected, so ~60 dB suffices. | CMRR_req = 20*log10(Vcm / V_err,allowed) | Vcm (V), V_err (V), zero-cal yes/no | bridge sensors, RTDs | calc | p.347 §5.13 | high |
| AOE-2100 | cables | Long balanced audio runs (100 m or more; signals from ~1 V peak down to 1 mV, pickup needed 40 dB lower still = 100,000:1, <10 uV) are solved with balanced differential signaling at a standard 150 ohm or 600 ohm impedance on well-shielded twisted pair with 3-pin latching XLR connectors, and a balanced driver and receiver (quality audio transformer or differential-output driver / differential-input amplifier). | pickup suppression >= 100 dB (1e5:1) | cable length (m), signal range (V) | professional audio and long analog links | review | p.348 §5.13, Fig. 5.64B | high |
| AOE-2101 | grounding | Receive signals between instruments whose local grounds differ with a difference amplifier whose REF pin is tied to the receiving ground, to avoid ground loops; difference amps also serve accurate low-side current sensing. | n/a | topology | inter-instrument analog links | review | p.348 §5.13, Figs. 5.67, 5.68 | low |
| AOE-2102 | components | Classic difference amplifier: Gdiff = Rf/Ri with matched pairs; with an ideal op-amp the CMRR is limited by the match of the Rf/Ri ratios in the two paths: 1% discrete resistors give only ~40 dB at low frequency. Better: trim, 0.1% parts ($0.10-0.20 in 100s), Susumu RG 0.05% / 10 ppm/degC (~$1), matched pairs (Vishay MPM ratio tolerance to 0.01%, ratio tempco to 2 ppm/degC; LT5400 quad ratio tempco 1 ppm/degC max), or an integrated difference amp (AD8278B worst case: gain +/-0.02% at G = 0.5 or 2, 1 ppm/degC gain tempco, 100 uV and 1 uV/degC offset, CMRR 80 dB, ~$3). | Gdiff = Rf/Ri; CMRR ~40 dB with 1% resistors | resistor tolerance/ratio match (%), ratio tempco (ppm/degC) | discrete vs integrated difference amps | calc | p.348-349 §5.14.1, Fig. 5.65 | high |
| AOE-2103 | current-carrying | Low-side current sensing at high current needs a 4-wire (Kelvin) sense resistor read differentially: at 10 A a 0.01 ohm shunt keeps dissipation below 1 W, but just 1 mohm of connection resistance in a single-ended hookup would add 10% error. CMRR demands are modest because the low side stays near ground. | P = I^2 * Rs; error = R_conn / Rs | I (A), Rs (ohm), R_conn (ohm) | shunt current measurement | calc | p.350 §5.14.2C, Fig. 5.68A | high |
| AOE-2104 | requirements | High-side sensing with a difference amp converts CMRR into a large current error: book example (HV unity-gain difference amp with 20:1 input divider, +/-200 V CM, 86 dB min CMRR = 1:20,000, supply 0-200 V) treats this as equivalent to a 100 mV differential input variation, so 1% accuracy would need a 10 V drop at full load (100 W at 10 A). Use only for low-current supplies; otherwise float the amplifier at the high side and send the result to ground as a current or digitized through an opto-isolator. | V_err,in = dVcm / 10^(CMRR_dB/20) (arithmetic: 200 V / 20,000 = 10 mV; book states 100 mV); Rs*I_FS >= V_err,in / accuracy | dVcm (V), CMRR (dB), I_FS (A), accuracy | high-side shunt sensing | calc | p.350 §5.14.2C, Fig. 5.68B | medium |
| AOE-2105 | components | Difference-amp current source (Fig. 5.69): IL = Gdiff*(VA - VB)/Rs, either polarity, load may return anywhere. Choose Rs for ~1 V drop at IL(max) (less for high current or low supply), then check Rs*IL(min) >= ~100 uV; buffer U1 needs Ib << IL(min) and Vos << Rs*IL(min); for IL(max)/IL(min) > 10^4 use low-offset amps for both U1 and U2. Above ~5 mA add a power buffer (wideband unity-gain IC buffer such as LT1010, a MOSFET/BJT follower for one polarity, or an LM317 driven at ADJ with OUT = ADJ + 1.25 V for built-in thermal/overcurrent protection); U2 can be omitted if Rs < ~0.2 ohm. A G = 10 difference amp used backwards (G = 0.1: INA106, INA143) saves compliance. | IL = Gdiff*(VA - VB)/Rs; Rs*IL(max) ~ 1 V; Rs*IL(min) >= 100 uV | IL(max), IL(min) (A), Vs (V), Vos, Ib | precision bipolar current sources | calc | p.350-351 §5.14.2D, Fig. 5.69 | high |
| AOE-2106 | components | Op-amp-based current sources become voltage-source-like at high frequency (compensation and slew limits); characterize with an effective output capacitance; for better HF use an instrumentation-amp source with an inherently high-impedance output (Fig. 5.87). | C_eff = IL(max) / S (S = output slew rate, V/s) | IL(max) (A), S (V/s) | fast-changing current sources | calc | p.351 §5.14.2D, fn 62 | high |
| AOE-2107 | requirements | Pro-audio level math: 0 dBu = 0.775 Vrms (1 mW into 600 ohm); pro "0 dB" = +4 dBu = 1.23 Vrms; home audio -10 dBu = 0.25 Vrms; with 16-20 dB headroom, +20 dB = 12.3 Vrms = 17.4 V differential amplitude (34.8 Vpp). Balanced lines terminate in a nominal 600 ohm bridging resistance; op-amps on +/-18 V swing ~+/-15 V per line, above the +/-9 V needed. | V_dBu = 0.775 * 10^(dBu/20) Vrms | level (dBu), headroom (dB) | balanced line drivers | calc | p.351 §5.14.2E, fn 63, Fig. 5.70 | high |
| AOE-2108 | transmission-line | Analog over unshielded twisted pair (Cat-5e/Cat-6): pairs have differing twist pitch (low normal-mode coupling) but plenty of common-mode coupling, so drive differentially and receive with a difference amp; terminate in the 100 ohm characteristic impedance for anything longer than a few inches. For 300 m, equalize the HF loss with peaking (AD8130: G = 3 at LF, RaC1 zero near 1.6 MHz -> flat +/-1 dB dc-9 MHz); the x3 gain covers the termination-vs-cable divider (50.5 ohm round trip, factor 1.5) and doubles for a back-terminated video output. | Z_term = 100 ohm; f_peak = 1/(2*pi*Ra*C1) ~ 1.6 MHz | cable length (m), R_loop (ohm), bandwidth (Hz) | wideband analog links over UTP | sim | p.352 §5.14.2F, Fig. 5.71 | high |
| AOE-2109 | grounding | For single-ended cable links with minor powerline ground loops, float the driver's output reference (or the receiver's input common) through a small resistor with a bypass capacitor, using a difference amplifier's REF pin (Fig. 5.67); this is no substitute for fully isolated or balanced connections in demanding (pro audio, video) systems. | small R + bypass C at the floating reference | topology | inter-instrument single-ended links | review | p.349 §5.14.2B, Fig. 5.67 | low |
| AOE-2110 | components | A difference amplifier's source resistance adds in series with its input resistor R1 and reduces gain by R1/(R1 + Rs); integrated parts have worst-case gain errors of 0.1% or better, so Rs must be below R1 by at least that factor to keep the gain accuracy. | G_eff = G * R1/(R1 + Rs); require Rs/R1 <= gain-error budget (e.g. Rs <= 0.001*R1 for 0.1%) | Rs (ohm), R1 (ohm), gain-error budget | difference amps driven from non-zero source impedance | calc | p.352 §5.14.3A, Table 5.7 | high |
| AOE-2111 | components | Integrated difference-amp CMRR is specified at Rs = 0 and degrades rapidly with source impedance even when both sources are matched, because on-chip resistors are laser-trimmed for ratio match at the expense of absolute value (absolute scaling typically only +/-20%). Drive them from op-amp outputs or very-low-Rs sources (e.g. low-value shunts). High gain makes it worse: G = 1000 would need R1 = 100 ohm, Rf = 100 k, ~100 ohm input impedance and source impedances matched to 0.001 ohm; use an instrumentation amplifier instead for high-gain differential inputs (difference amps rarely exceed G = 10). | CMRR spec valid at Rs = 0; R1 absolute tolerance ~ +/-20% | Rs, source match, G | difference amps | review | p.352-354 §5.14.3A, fn 65 | high |
| AOE-2112 | components | Raising all difference-amp resistor values to raise input impedance costs Johnson noise and bandwidth (distributed stray capacitance): en = 0.13*sqrt(R) nV/sqrt(Hz), i.e. ~130 nV/sqrt(Hz) at ~1 Mohm. Buffer the inputs with precision followers or use an in-amp instead. Keep sources low-Z for low-en parts: even 100 ohm gives 1.3 nV/sqrt(Hz). | en_R = 0.13 * sqrt(R) nV/sqrt(Hz) (R in ohm, room temperature) | R (ohm) | high-impedance difference-amp designs | calc | p.353-354 §5.14.3A, fn 64 | high |
| AOE-2113 | components | Know which input impedance matters: common-mode Zin(each) = (G+1)*R1 (both inputs driven together; combined = (G+1)*R1/2, which some datasheets quote); single-ended drive: inverting input Rin = R1, non-inverting Rin = R1 + Rf (a factor of 11 apart at G = 10); datasheet "differential" values are often based on net input-current change without explanation (Fig. 5.72). | Zcm(each) = (G+1)*R1; Zcm(both) = (G+1)*R1/2; Rin(-) = R1; Rin(+) = R1 + Rf | R1, Rf (ohm), G = Rf/R1 | loading calculations for difference amps | calc | p.354 §5.14.3A, Fig. 5.72, fn 66 | high |
| AOE-2114 | components | The R1/Rf divider lets a difference amp accept inputs beyond the rails, up to about +/-Vs*(G+1)/G: AD8278 (R1 = 40k, Rf = 20k) specifies -3(Vs + 0.1) to +3(Vs - 1.5) at G = 0.5 (~+/-40 V on +/-15 V) and -1.5(Vs + 0.1) to +1.5(Vs - 1.5) at G = 2 (~+/-20 V). INA117 reaches +/-200 V at unity gain with 200:1 input dividers (op-amp CM range +/-10 V), paying in offset and noise: 120 uV and 550 nV/sqrt(Hz) typ vs 25 uV and 20 nV/sqrt(Hz) for the AMP03. Check each datasheet. | Vcm,max ~ +/-Vs*(G+1)/G | Vs (V), G, datasheet CM range | high-CM difference-amp inputs | calc | p.354 §5.14.3B, Fig. 5.75C | high |
| AOE-2115 | requirements | With large common-mode inputs, finite CMRR dominates the error budget; use the minimum CMRR at the signal frequency: AD629B (96 dB typ, 86 dB min dc) with 200 V CM -> 10 mV input-referred error, swamping its 0.5 mV max Vos (CMRR error exceeds Vos for abs(Vcm) > 10 V); INA117 CMRR falls to 66 dB min at 60 Hz, so a 160 V peak powerline gives 80 mV error. | V_err,in = Vcm / 10^(CMRR_min,dB(f)/20) | Vcm (V), CMRR_min(f) (dB), Vos,max | HV difference amps, line monitoring | calc | p.354-355 §5.14.3B | high |
| AOE-2116 | components | Difference-amp CMRR falls with frequency as loop gain falls; wider-bandwidth parts hold CMRR higher. Compare CMRR-vs-frequency plots (Fig. 5.73), not the tabulated dc value: INA105, AD829, LT1991 are good at dc but poor at HF; AD8271 and MAX4198 look better at HF; AD8273 (datasheet plot 100 dB below 40 kHz, but tabulated 86 dB typ, 77 dB worst case) and AD8275 are good dc to HF; AD8129 very good but limited to small inputs and high gains. | CMRR(f) from plot at f_signal | f_signal (Hz) | ac common-mode rejection | review | p.355 §5.14.3C, Fig. 5.73, fn 68 | medium |
| AOE-2117 | emc | High-frequency CMRR is also lost to stray inductance and asymmetric capacitive loading: balance circuit capacitances with mirror-image component placement; as input shunt reactance falls with frequency, sensitivity to source-impedance imbalance rises ("it is the balance of common-mode impedances that defines a balanced system", Whitlock and Floru, AES). | symmetric layout; matched Zcm on both legs | layout, source Z balance | differential inputs at audio and RF frequencies | inspect | p.355 §5.14.3C, fn 69 | low |
| AOE-2118 | components | Integrated difference amps are factory trimmed to 25-100 uV typical offset (worst case about 10x larger). External offset trim (Fig. 5.74A): divide a +/-15 V trimmer down to +/-1 mV at REF, and balance the ~10 ohm added REF resistance to preserve CMRR; the added resistance cuts gain by about R_add/Rf (10 ohm vs typical Rf = 25k -> 0.04%, comparable to the gain spec); use a smaller resistor if that matters. | trim range = +/-1 mV at REF; gain error ~ R_add/Rf (10/25k = 0.04%) | Rf (ohm), R_add (ohm), trim range (V) | difference-amp offset trimming | calc | p.355 §5.14.4B, Fig. 5.74A | high |
| AOE-2119 | components | CMRR trim (Fig. 5.74B): make the REF-to-ground resistance adjustable around R1 with standard resistors plus a 100 ohm trimmer (20-30 ohm range, +/-5 ohm symmetrical); with 25k resistors this nulls an initial CMRR of 75 dB, beating the 80 dB worst-case spec. Limit the trim range for an accurate, stable trim and avoid trimmers below 100 ohm. | trim span / R1 = +/-5 ohm / 25 kohm = +/-0.02%; null range ~ 75 dB initial CMRR | R1 (ohm), trimmer (ohm) | discrete CMRR trimming | calc | p.355-356 §5.14.4C, Fig. 5.74B | high |
| AOE-2120 | components | Single-supply level translation (split REF resistor, Fig. 5.75D): on +5 V with REF2 driven from a clean reference of the same voltage, zero differential input gives +2.5 V out, and G = 0.2 maps a +/-10 V differential input to 0-5 V; use Vref = 4.096 V when driving an ADC so steps are round numbers (1 mV/step at 12 bits). Difference amps are limited to G < 10, CMRR < 85 dB min, 20-50 nV/sqrt(Hz) noise and ~10k-100k input resistance; beyond that use an in-amp (>1e9 ohm, G 1-1000, ~1 nV/sqrt(Hz), 100-120 dB worst-case CMRR). | Vout = Vref/2 + G*Vdiff; LSB = 4.096 V / 2^12 = 1 mV | Vref (V), G, N bits | ADC front ends on single supply | calc | p.356 §5.14.4D, §5.15 intro | high |

Use lossy ferrite beads to attenuate RF and de-Q resonances, plus filtering. | qualitative | symptoms | precision/high-gain inputs | measure | p.366 §5.16.4 | low |

Split supplies fix the input level but add power-sequencing issues and the risk of negative current into the ADC clamp diodes. | V(pins) >= V- + 1 V and <= V+ - 1 V (AD8139) ; AD8139: en = 2.2 nV/rtHz, ~15 MHz at G = 20, RRO | Vin(CM), G, rails | single-supply ADC drivers (Fig. 5.102A) | calc | p.381 §5.17.3A, fn105 | high |

Noise comparable to 1 LSB is acceptable; lower is nicer; reduce bandwidth if not needed. Wideband FDAs with en = 25-45 nV/rtHz give 175-700 uV rms over 50-250 MHz, far above the 30 uV LSB of a 16-bit ADC with 2 Vpp differential FS (p.386-387 §5.17.5J) | FS_in (V), N (bits), en (V/rtHz), BW_eff (Hz) | ADC front ends | calc | p.382 §5.17.3B, fn107 | high |

| AOE-2211 | termination | Terminated single-ended-to-differential FDA (config D) design: pick Rt so the source sees Rs, add the source's Thevenin resistance to the grounded-side gain resistor for balance, then raise Rf to restore gain; gain is defined relative to the loaded (terminated) input amplitude. | Rt par Rin = Rs (terminate) ; RG2 = RG1 + par(Rs,Rt) (balance) ; RF = G*RG2*(Rs + Rt)/(2*Rt) (gain) ; G = (Vout+ - Vout-)/Vin = (2*Rt/(Rt + Rs))*(RF/RG2). Example: Rs = 50 ohm, G = 2, RG1 = 200 ohm -> Rt = 60.4, RG2 = 226, RF = 412 ohm (1% values) | Rs, G, RG1 (ohm) | wideband 50 ohm inputs to FDAs (Fig. 5.104) | calc | p.382-383 §5.17.4, Fig. 5.104 | high |
| AOE-2212 | termination | At low frequencies (e.g. audio) do not terminate the source: omit Rt, balance with RG2 = RG1 + Rs, and gain is referred to the open-circuit source amplitude. | RG2 = RG1 + Rs ; G = RF/RG2 (OCR of printed formula partly garbled) | Rs, RG1, RF | audio-band FDA inputs | calc | p.383 §5.17.4 | medium |
| AOE-2213 | components | Read FDA swing specs correctly: "Vout diff'l max (Vpp)" is twice the p-p swing of either output; RR output means differential swing = 2 x supply. HV FDAs give > 50 Vpp differential (each output +/-12.5 V) on +/-15 V, more on +/-18 V, suiting line drivers. | Vout,diff,pp = (Va+ - Vb-) + (Vb+ - Va-) = 2*Vpp(single output) ; RRO: Vout,diff,max = 2*Vsupply | Vpp per output, supply | FDA swing budgeting | calc | p.383 §5.17.5A, Table 5.10 note b | high |
| AOE-2214 | components | High-voltage FDAs (+/-12 to +/-15 V, some to +/-5 V) usually run split but have Vocm to drive single-supply ADCs; their internal rail-splitting divider needs a bypass capacitor and can be overridden by the ADC's mid-span output (check both datasheets; an op-amp buffer may be needed). Pro-audio FDAs (THAT1606, OPA1632, LME49724, DRV134) give lowest distortion when driven from a low-impedance source such as an op-amp output. | Vocm override from ADC ref (buffer if ADC ref is weak) | supplies, ADC ref drive | HV FDAs, pro audio | review | p.383 §5.17.5A | medium |
| AOE-2215 | components | Low-voltage high-frequency FDAs are typically limited to +/-5 V (many to 5 V or even 3.3 V total); some run from a single +2.7 V or +3.3 V, others need at least +5 V. Check absolute supply limits against the system rails. | Vsupply,total <= 5 V or 3.3 V for many parts | supply rails | LV FDAs | review | p.383 §5.17.5A | medium |
| AOE-2216 | components | High-frequency rail-to-rail-output FDAs lose HF linearity near the rails: e.g. LTC6404 (600 MHz) distortion at 10 MHz climbs dramatically within 400 mV of the rails. Keep outputs >= 400 mV from rails, or use an output-clamping FDA (LMH6553; CFB, good BW at high gain, worse noise) to protect the ADC. | output headroom >= 0.4 V from each rail (LTC6404 example) | output swing, rails, f | HF RRO FDAs driving ADCs | calc | p.383-384 §5.17.5A | high |
| AOE-2217 | protection | FDA summing junctions: most need >= ~1 V above the negative rail (THS4521 and other NRI parts excepted); even NRI parts must not be driven more than the specified 0.2 V below -Vee or output polarity reversal can occur (as with LM324/358). | V(sum junction) >= -Vee + ~1 V (typical) ; NRI: >= -Vee - 0.2 V | Vin(CM), G, Vocm | single-supply FDAs | calc | p.384 §5.17.5B | high |
| AOE-2218 | matching | FDA input impedance is roughly Rf/G (low at high gain; exceptions LTC6416 and the EL5170 family); higher-Zin FDAs are usually noisier from resistor Johnson noise (AD8352, configuration F, excepted). Source impedance matching matters from ~30-100 MHz up, even with short PCB traces, and low Zin also shifts gain (use the §5.17.4 formulas). | Zin ~ Rf/G ; matching needed for f >= ~30-100 MHz | Rf, G, f | FDA inputs | calc | p.384 §5.17.5C | high |
| AOE-2219 | components | Fixed-gain FDAs with internal resistors (e.g. D3) have poor offsets because resistor mismatch acts on the (high) output common-mode voltage; bare FDAs with 1% external resistors suffer the same, and mismatched external source resistance also degrades dc accuracy. | Vos,eff = mismatch * Vocm ; 1% on Vocm = 1.5 V -> 15 mV | resistor mismatch (fraction), Vocm (V) | FDA dc accuracy | calc | p.384 §5.17.5D | high |
| AOE-2220 | components | Bare (external-resistor) FDAs have far better CMRR than their fixed-gain versions; if CMRR matters use 0.1% resistors or matched arrays. | ADA4932 100 dB typ vs fixed-gain ADA4950 64 dB ; LTC1992 90 dB vs LTC1992-10 60 dB | resistor tolerance | FDA CMRR | review | p.384 §5.17.5D | high |
| AOE-2221 | components | HF CMRR of resistor-set FDAs is limited by board-capacitance matching at the gain resistors; CMRR degrades 20 dB/decade with frequency. | dC_max = 10^(-CMRR_dB/20) / (2*pi*f*R) ; -80 dB at 1 MHz with 500 ohm resistors -> 0.03 pF match | CMRR target (dB), f (Hz), R (ohm) | FDA layout for HF CMRR | calc | p.384 §5.17.5D | high |
| AOE-2222 | components | When CMRR must survive unbalanced input resistances at HF, prefer configuration E or F FDAs over configuration D. | EL5170-series: 80 dB at 1 MHz (but 25 mV offset) ; AD8352: 60 dB at 100 MHz, 6 mV offset | f_cm, source imbalance | FDA topology selection | review | p.384 §5.17.5D | high |
| AOE-2223 | components | Fixed-gain FDAs can beat discrete-resistor gain accuracy, but most fixed-gain FDAs have poor absolute gain accuracy (ADL5561 excepted), rarely allow band-limiting capacitors, and usually restrict you to low gains (PGA870, LT1993-10 excepted, at high power). Some external-resistor FDAs draw much less supply current. | worst-case gain error: LMP7312 +/-0.04%, PGA280 +/-0.15%, AD8270 +/-0.08% ; THS4520: G = 10 with 120 MHz BW | gain accuracy, Iq | fixed vs resistor-set FDAs | review | p.384-385 §5.17.5E | high |
| AOE-2224 | control-loop | Datasheet FDA "bandwidth" is measured at unity gain with peaking and is typically >= 1.5 x the true GBW; for G > 4 estimate f3dB = GBW/G using the true GBW read from the response plots. | f3dB(G > 4) = GBW/G ; BW(table, G = 1) >= 1.5*GBW | GBW (Hz), G | VFB FDAs | calc | p.385 §5.17.5F | high |
| AOE-2225 | filter | VFB FDAs stay stable with capacitors across the feedback resistors: raise Rf (which also raises Zin) and add a small parallel Cf to control peaking, or a larger Cf to set the signal bandwidth. Some fixed-gain parts integrate filters (LT6600-X: five fixed 4th-order responses from 2.5 MHz to 20 MHz). | f_pole = 1/(2*pi*Rf*Cf) | Rf, Cf | FDA band-limiting / anti-alias | sim | p.385 §5.17.5F | medium |
| AOE-2226 | components | CFB FDAs are specified at a particular Rf and keep bandwidth as gain rises. For higher gain raise Rf rather than cutting Rg much, but a larger Rf reduces slew rate proportionally and adds noise. | LMH6553/LMH6552: Rf = 274/357 ohm -> 900/1500 MHz, 2300/3800 V/us at G = 1 ; LMH6552 800 MHz at G = 4 | Rf, G | CFB FDAs | review | p.385 fn111 | high |
| AOE-2227 | components | Gain peaking destroys 0.1 dB flatness at low gain: the -0.1 dB bandwidth can be a small fraction of the -3 dB bandwidth and improves at higher gain. Specify and verify flatness bandwidth separately; dual versions give matched delays. | ADL5561: G = 2: -3 dB 2900 MHz, -0.1 dB 200 MHz (7%) ; G = 6: -3 dB 1800 MHz, -0.1 dB 600 MHz (33%) | G, flatness spec | wideband FDAs (Fig. 5.105) | measure | p.385 §5.17.5G, Fig. 5.105 | high |
| AOE-2228 | components | Large-signal bandwidth is slew-limited: datasheet small-signal (~100 mV) bandwidth overstates what a ~2 V swing achieves. | f_max = S/(2*pi*A) ; ADA4932: S = 2800 V/us -> 445 MHz at A = 1 V (datasheet: 560 MHz at 100 mV, 360 MHz at 2 Vpp) ; ADL5561 10 kV/us -> 2 Vpp to 1.5 GHz ; AD8351 13 V/ns -> 2 Vpp to ~2 GHz | S (V/s), A (V peak) | FDA/op-amp large-signal drive | calc | p.385-386 §5.17.5H, fn112 | high |
| AOE-2229 | components | HF distortion correlates with speed (GBW, slew rate); above ~10 MHz FDAs beat single-ended op-amps. Datasheet distortion conditions are not standardized, so compare 2nd/3rd-harmonic plots at your gain, load, amplitude and supply; headline figures can collapse well below the bandwidth. | best FDAs < -100 dB to 20 MHz vs 7 MHz for the best op-amp (AD8045) ; ADA4932 -90 dB at 20 MHz degrades x10 by 50 MHz ; THS4521 (145 MHz, 1.1 mA, RRO) poor below 5 MHz vs THS4511 (1.6 GHz, 39 mA, no RRO) | f, HD2/HD3 plots | high-speed ADC drivers (Fig. 5.106) | review | p.386 §5.17.5I, Fig. 5.106, fn113 | high |
| AOE-2230 | requirements | ADC-driver linearity must match the converter: a 16-bit ADC sampling to 250 Msps (e.g. AD9467) needs better than 0.01% linearity (-80 dB distortion) at frequencies approaching 100 MHz; multi-GHz-sample ADCs (e.g. ADC12D1800, 12-bit 3600 Msps) will likely need transformer drive. | THD <= -80 dB near 100 MHz for 16-bit 250 Msps | ADC bits, f_in | high-speed ADC chains | review | p.386 §5.17.5I, fn114 | high |
| AOE-2231 | components | Estimate amplifier current noise from input bias current when it is not tabulated: it is at least the shot noise of Ib; CFB amplifiers (very high input currents) have much more current noise, up to ~10x that of VFB parts. | in >= sqrt(2*q*Ib) ; q = 1.602e-19 C ; in(CFB) up to ~10 x in(VFB) | Ib (A) | FDA/op-amp noise estimates | calc | p.386 §5.17.5J | high |
| AOE-2232 | components | FDA en specs (1.1-5 nV/rtHz) are bare summing-junction figures; add the feedback-resistor noise. With the usual Rf = 350-500 ohm, Rg's Johnson noise at G = 1 (2.4-2.8 nV/rtHz) dominates quiet parts; at G = 10 (Rg = 35-50 ohm) it is under 1 nV/rtHz. Gains of 5 or 10 are generally assumed. | en(Rg) = 0.13*sqrt(Rg) nV/rtHz ; Rg = Rf/G | Rf, G, en | low-noise FDA design | calc | p.387 §5.17.5J | high |
| AOE-2233 | components | Check noise-vs-frequency plots for high 1/f corners, especially of current noise (some near 1 MHz or beyond); current noise times the feedback resistances can dominate en. | e_in = in*R ; THS4508 (pnp inputs): in = 4.7 pA/rtHz at 1 MHz -> 1.6 nV/rtHz across 349 ohm (OK vs en = 2.3 nV/rtHz) but 4.7 nV/rtHz with 1 kohm | in (A/rtHz) at f, R (ohm), en | FDA/op-amp noise | calc | p.387 §5.17.5J | high |
| AOE-2234 | requirements | Error budget policy: strict worst-case design keeps every part within datasheet limits and adds all worst-case errors as unsigned magnitudes. Pragmatic design discounts over-conservative limits (e.g. input leakage) and estimates unspecified parameters, then shows each unspecified effect is insignificant, or adds incoming-part testing, or validates the assembled circuit/instrument at final test. | worst case: E_tot = sum(abs(e_i)) ; tally in an error budget to find the dominant terms | error terms, spec status (max/typ/unspecified) | precision circuit design | review | p.388 Review of Ch.5 ¶B-¶C (§5.3, §5.5) | medium |
| AOE-2235 | components | Difference-amplifier CMRR is set by resistor-ratio matching; gain accuracy depends on the ratio match and ratio tempco, not absolute values, so use matched arrays for precision. | CMRR(dB) ~= 20*log10(100/p), p = ratio mismatch in percent. Metal film 0.05-1%, 25-100 ppm/C ; precision arrays 0.01-0.05% accuracy, tempco to 1 ppm/C, ratio match 0.01-0.001%, tracking 1-0.1 ppm/C | p (%) | difference amps, gain networks | calc | p.388 Review of Ch.5 ¶D | high |
| AOE-2236 | requirements | Precision-input error terms referred to input: Vos plus Ib*Rs; Vos appears at the output multiplied by the noise gain; an integrator converts Vos into an input-current error. Bipolar precision op-amps (Ib ~10 nA) suffer once Rs exceeds a few kohm; then use FET/MOSFET inputs, or bias-cancelled bipolar (Ib ~1 nA) for moderate Rs. FET Ib rises dramatically with temperature and can exceed bias-cancelled bipolar. Also budget Ib vs Vcm, CMRR, PSRR and en. | V_err,RTI = Vos + Ib*Rs ; dVout = Gv*Vos ; integrator dIin = Vos/Rin ; typical precision op-amp Vos = 10-50 uV, TCVos = 0.1-0.5 uV/C (auto-zero ~10x better) | Vos, Ib, Rs, Gv, Rin, T range | precision op-amp stages | calc | p.388-389 Review of Ch.5 ¶F (§5.7, §5.10) | high |
| AOE-2237 | control-loop | Closed-loop dynamic accuracy of an op-amp stage: gain error from finite loop gain, bandwidth from fT, settling from the closed-loop time constant (real settling can be longer; use measured data), phase error below fc. | dG = (G_ideal - G_actual)/G_ideal = 1/(1 + A*B) ; f3dB = fT/G_CL ; tau ~= G_CL/(2*pi*fT) ; t_settle ~ 5-10*tau (well compensated, good phase margin) ; phase error phi ~= f/fc (rad), fc = fT/G_CL | A (open-loop gain at f), B (feedback fraction), fT (Hz), G_CL | op-amp gain stages | calc | p.389 Review of Ch.5 ¶G (§5.8.2, §5.8.5, §5.10.10) | high |
| AOE-2238 | components | RRI / RRO / auto-zero op-amp caveats. RRI parts step Ib and Vos at the input-stage crossover (avoid, use a charge-pump RRIO such as OPA360, or pick a CM range reaching only the needed rail). RRO parts (common-source output) have more distortion and higher output impedance. Auto-zero parts give Vos ~1 uV and ~10 nV/C with no 1/f noise, but have noise peaks at the switching frequency, a higher LF noise floor, higher input current noise, usually < 7 V total supply (LTC2057HV 4.75-60 V excepted) and saturation recovery as long as several ms. | AZ: Vos ~1 uV, TC ~10 nV/C ; supply < 7 V total ; overload recovery up to several ms | Vcm range, load, supply, overload events | op-amp selection | review | p.389-390 Review of Ch.5 ¶H-¶I (§5.9, §5.11) | high |

### Ch.6 Filters + Review (pp. 391-424; lines 23446-25110)
| id | domain | rule statement | formula / limit (units) | inputs | applicability & conditions | verify by | source | conf |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| AOE-2239 | filter | Cascading n identical, perfectly buffered RC lowpass sections pulls the overall -3 dB point down ("shrinkage factor"); re-scale each section's RC if the overall corner must stay put | f3dB(n)/f3dB(1) = sqrt(2^(1/n) - 1) (dimensionless) | n = number of identical buffered RC sections | Buffered (non-loading) sections; footnote formula is OCR-garbled, expression derived by setting (1+(f/f1)^2)^(-n/2) = 1/sqrt(2) | calc | p.391 §6.2.1 fn 1, Fig. 6.2 | medium |
| AOE-2240 | filter | Cascading RC sections steepens the ultimate slope but does not sharpen the knee ("many soft knees do not a hard knee make"); do not use an RC cascade where a flat passband plus sharp transition is required | lag phase -> n x 90 deg asymptotically; phase at the -3 dB point grows with n | n sections | Passive RC cascades, normalized to same overall -3 dB point | sim | p.391-392 §6.2.1, Figs. 6.2, 6.3 | high |
| AOE-2241 | filter | A real RC filter stops rolling off where the capacitor ESR becomes comparable to its reactance 1/(omega*C); ultimate attenuation is set by ESR, not by the RC slope | stops rolling off when ESR ~ 1/(2*pi*f*C) (ohm); per-section floor ~ ESR/(R+ESR) (derived) | ESR (ohm), C (F), R (ohm), f (Hz) | Electrolytics especially; e.g. ESR = 1 ohm for a 5 uF 100 V electrolytic (datasheet value used in the book's SPICE run) | sim | p.392-393 §6.2.1A, Fig. 6.4 | medium |
| AOE-2242 | decoupling | When RC-filtering a dc rail that feeds a low-level stage, split the total series R and total C into several sections rather than one big RC; multi-section wins even with realistic ESR | example budget: 100 ohm total series R (load current a few mA), 20 uF total C: 1 x (100 ohm, 20 uF) vs 2 x (50 ohm, 10 uF) vs 4 x (25 ohm, 5 uF) | total R (ohm), total C (F), sections n | Suppressing HF switching noise / coupled signals on a rail for a low-level stage | sim | p.392-393 §6.2.1A, Fig. 6.4 | high |
| AOE-2243 | filter | Square-to-sine with a series LC: resonate at the fundamental and make X_L at the 3rd harmonic (square waves have only odd harmonics) large compared with the load | f0 = 1/(2*pi*sqrt(L*C)); X_L(3*f0) = 2*pi*(3*f0)*L >> R_load; example f0 = 1.0 MHz, L ~ 100 uH -> X_L(3 MHz) ~ 2 kohm vs 50 ohm load | L (H), C (F), f0 (Hz), R_load (ohm) | Fixed-frequency square wave driving a 50 ohm load (Fig. 6.6/6.7, 3 x 74HC04 driver) | calc | p.394 §6.2.3A, Figs. 6.6-6.7 | high |
| AOE-2244 | emc | Slow the square-wave edges with a simple RC prefilter ahead of a series-LC sine filter: very fast edges couple through the inductor's parasitic shunt capacitance and put small notches on the sine output | RC prefilter before the LC | edge rate, inductor self-capacitance | Square-to-sine LC filters driven by fast logic | inspect | p.394 §6.2.3A | high |
| AOE-2245 | timing | Suppress PLL reference spurs with a series LC trap tuned to the phase-comparison frequency, placed across the VCO analog tuning voltage | f_trap = f_comparison = f_ref/N_ref; example 10 MHz/50 = 200 kHz = f_VCO/390 at 78.0 MHz; spurs at 78.0 MHz +/- 0.2 MHz | f_ref (Hz), N_ref, N_VCO | Integer-N PLL with a clean VCO whose dominant residual is the comparison-frequency spur; trap is in addition to the classic loop filter R1 R2 C1 | measure | p.394-395 §6.2.3B, Fig. 6.8 | high |
| AOE-2246 | filter | A signal to be digitized must not contain components above half the sampling rate (Nyquist criterion); precede the ADC with an anti-alias lowpass that is flat over the signal band and thoroughly attenuates above fs/2 | f_signal_content < fs/2 | fs (Hz), signal bandwidth (Hz) | Any sampled system; violation creates non-existent in-band components (aliasing, §13.5.1B) | calc | p.395 §6.2.3C, fn 6 | high |
| AOE-2247 | filter | A sharp LC Chebyshev anti-alias lowpass beats an RC cascade of equal component count by tens of dB just above cutoff | 7-section LC Chebyshev, fc = 1.0 MHz, 0.1 dB p-p ripple, 378 ohm terminations, fs = 2.5 Msps: -20 dB at Nyquist 1.25 MHz; worst-case aliased signals (1.5 MHz) down an additional 16 dB; 7-section RC with same fc: only 1.6 dB more attenuation at 1.25*fc than at fc | order, ripple, fc, fs | Radio-astronomy baseband (dc-1 MHz) digitizer; calculated and measured agree | sim | p.395-396 §6.2.3C, Figs. 6.9-6.11 | high |
| AOE-2248 | filter | Placing the anti-alias corner well below Nyquist is normal practice for high-speed ADC front ends | example: fs = 25 MHz -> steep-rolloff input filter starting at 10 MHz (fc/fs = 0.4) | fs, fc | Filter between driver amplifier and ADC; convert single-ended designs to differential per TI SLWA053B | review | p.396 §6.2.3C | high |
| AOE-2249 | filter | Differential high-speed ADC inputs usually want a low source impedance terminated in a differential capacitor: a passive differential RC lowpass between driver and ADC | example for AD9225 (25 Msps): two 50 ohm series resistors + 100 pF differential capacitor (Fig. 5.102) | R_series (ohm), C_diff (F) | Differential-input ADCs requiring low source impedance (§13.6.2) | inspect | p.396 §6.2.3D | high |
| AOE-2250 | filter | Negative-impedance converter makes Z_in = -Z (a capacitor becomes a "backward inductor"); a gyrator inverts an impedance, turning a capacitor into a true inductor | NIC: Z_in = -Z, Z_C = 1/(j*omega*C) -> Z_in = j/(omega*C) (Eq. 6.1); gyrator: Z_in = R^2/Z, Z_C -> Z_in = j*omega*C*R^2, i.e. L = C*R^2 (H; C in F, R in ohm) (Eq. 6.2) | C (F), R (ohm) | Op-amp NIC/gyrator circuits (Figs. 6.12-6.13) | calc | p.397 §6.2.4B-C, Eqs. 6.1-6.2 | high |
