| AOE-2263 | filter | Chebyshev (equiripple passband) amplitude response | Vout/Vin = 1/[1 + eps^2 * Cn^2(f/fc)]^(1/2); Cn = Chebyshev polynomial of first kind, degree n; eps sets ripple | f, fc, n, eps | Phase/transient response far from ideal (worse than Butterworth) | calc | p.402 Eq. 6.4 | high |
| AOE-2264 | filter | Allowing a small passband ripple cuts the required order drastically; size the order from the spec, not by habit | spec 0.1 dB passband flatness and 20 dB attenuation at 1.25 x (top of passband): Butterworth needs 19 poles, Chebyshev 8 poles | ripple, f_stop/fc, A_min | Lowpass order selection | calc | p.402 §6.2.6A | high |
| AOE-2265 | filter | An elliptic (Cauer) filter trades passband and stopband ripple for the steepest transition and fewest sections, with better phase than the equivalent Chebyshev, but its stopband holds a floor instead of continuing to fall | Fig. 6.24 spec: 6-pole elliptic = 11-pole Chebyshev = 32-pole Butterworth | spec (ripple, fc, f_stop, A_min) | Use when a minimum stopband attenuation (not continuing falloff) is acceptable | sim | p.402-403 §6.2.6A, Fig. 6.24 | high |
| AOE-2266 | components | Finite R and C tolerances make any real active filter deviate from its textbook response (a "Butterworth" will show passband ripple); check worst-case component corners | worst-case R, C tolerance sweep (graph) | R, C tolerances (%) | Active filters; Fig. 6.22 (graph only) | sim | p.402 Fig. 6.22 | medium |
| AOE-2267 | filter | Bessel (Thomson) filter = maximally flat time delay; raising its order barely steepens the transition but improves delay flatness and ultimate slope; choose it when time-domain (step/pulse) fidelity matters; Chebyshev has the worst time-domain behavior, Butterworth in between | ultimate slope still 6n dB/octave (Fig. 6.30) | n | Pulse, step, waveform-shape-critical signals | sim | p.403-405 §6.2.6B-C, Figs. 6.25-6.27 | high |
| AOE-2268 | filter | Alternatives for time-domain performance: Gaussian (phase nearly as good as Bessel, better step response); equiripple-delay "linear phase" filters specified by phase ripple (e.g. 0.5 deg); allpass delay equalizers to flatten the delay of any filter (incl. Butterworth, Chebyshev) | phase ripple spec e.g. 0.5 deg | type | Time-delay-critical paths | review | p.404-405 §6.2.6B | high |
| AOE-2269 | filter | Time-domain figures in Table 6.1 are for fc = 1 Hz; scale all times inversely with the actual cutoff | t(fc) = t_table / fc (s; fc in Hz) (derived normalization) | table time (s), fc (Hz) | Table 6.1 Bessel, Butterworth, Chebyshev 0.5 dB and 2 dB, 2-8 poles | calc | p.406 Table 6.1 | medium |
| AOE-2270 | filter | Above roughly 100 kHz prefer passive LC filters to active ones; for a few units use slug-tuned inductors (set with an inductance meter or bridge) and 1% capacitors or hand-trimmed paralleled capacitor pairs; bought filters use lumped L-C below ~100 MHz and coaxial/cavity above | f > ~100 kHz -> LC; custom filters >= ~$100 and weeks lead time, stock catalog units cheap | f (Hz), quantity | Build-vs-buy of passive filters (design tables in Appendix E) | review | p.405-406 §6.2.7 | high |
| AOE-2271 | filter | Switched-capacitor filters are clock-tunable, stable and cheap to integrate, but are noisier (smaller dynamic range), have more distortion, and add aliasing and clock feedthrough; continuous-time active filters avoid these | qualitative | application | Choosing continuous-time vs switched-capacitor | review | p.406 §6.3 | high |
| AOE-2272 | components | Judge an active-filter circuit by: parts count, adjustability, spread of component (esp. capacitor) values, demands on op-amp slew rate/bandwidth/output impedance, ability to reach high Q, electrical tunability, and above all sensitivity to component values and op-amp GBW (fT) | checklist | topology | VCVS: few parts but high sensitivity; ladder/state-variable: lower sensitivity | review | p.406-407 §6.3 | high |
| AOE-2273 | filter | VCVS (Sallen-Key) table design: an n-pole filter (n even) is n/2 cascaded 2-pole sections with R1 = R2 = R and C1 = C2 = C in each section; set each section's gain K from Table 6.2; choose R in 10k-100k | sections = n/2; 10 kohm <= R <= 100 kohm | n, fc | Avoid small R: the op-amp's rising open-loop output impedance at HF adds to R and upsets the design | calc | p.407-408 §6.3.1-6.3.2 | high |
| AOE-2274 | filter | Butterworth VCVS: every section uses the same RC; cascade sections in order of increasing gain to avoid dynamic-range (clipping) problems | R*C = 1/(2*pi*fc); 6-pole: K = 1.07, 1.59, 2.48 in that order | fc (Hz) | fc = -3 dB point of the whole filter | calc | p.408 §6.3.2A | high |
| AOE-2275 | filter | Bessel and Chebyshev VCVS: each section has its own RC product scaled by the Table 6.2 factor cn | R*C = 1/(2*pi*cn*fc) (s); fc = -3 dB (Bessel) or end of ripple band (Chebyshev); e.g. 0.5 dB Chebyshev with fc = 100 Hz is flat +0/-0.5 dB from dc to 100 Hz | cn, K (Table 6.2), fc | Lowpass; 0.5 dB and 2 dB Chebyshev tables given | calc | p.408 §6.3.2B, Table 6.2 | high |
| AOE-2276 | filter | VCVS highpass: interchange the R's and C's; Butterworth keeps the same R, C, K; Bessel and Chebyshev keep K but use the reciprocal normalizing factor | cn(HP) = 1/cn(Table 6.2) | cn, K | Highpass from the lowpass table | calc | p.408-409 §6.3.2C | high |
| AOE-2277 | filter | Passband gain of an equal-component VCVS cascade is the product of the section gains; plan headroom accordingly | G_total = product(K_i); e.g. 4-pole Butterworth 1.152 x 2.235 = 2.57; Bessel 1.084 x 1.759 = 1.91; Chebyshev 0.5 dB 1.582 x 2.660 = 4.21 | K_i | Fig. 6.29 "Gain" column (derived check) | calc | p.408 Fig. 6.29, Table 6.2 | medium |
| AOE-2278 | filter | Build moderate-Q bandpass by cascading overlapping LP and HP sections and band-reject by summing non-overlapping LP and HP outputs; for high Q use a single high-Q stage instead (VCVS bandpass, state-variable, biquad), because uncoupled cascades are too component-sensitive | high Q -> single-stage BP | Q target | Bandpass/band-reject | review | p.409 §6.3.2C | high |
| AOE-2279 | filter | Unequal-component Sallen-Key lowpass section (any gain K) | m = R1/R2, n = C1/C2, tau = R2*C2: fc = 1/(2*pi*tau*sqrt(m*n)) (Eq. 6.5); Q = sqrt(m*n)/(1 + m + m*n*(1 - K)) (Eq. 6.6) | R1,R2 (ohm), C1,C2 (F), K | Labels per original Sallen-Key paper (C1 = the bootstrapped capacitor to the output); many references (incl. TI Karki SLOA024B/SLOA049B) swap C1 and C2; OCR of eqs. garbled, forms consistent with Eq. 6.7 | calc | p.410 §6.3.2D, Eqs. 6.5-6.6, fn 28 | medium |
| AOE-2280 | control-loop | Equal-component Sallen-Key: Q depends only on gain; the section oscillates at K = 3; a unity-gain equal-component section gives only Q = 0.5 | fc = 1/(2*pi*R*C); Q = 1/(3 - K); require K < 3 | K | R1 = R2, C1 = C2 | calc | p.410 Eq. 6.7 | high |
| AOE-2281 | filter | Unity-gain (K = 1) Sallen-Key with a target Q: pick the capacitor ratio n, then solve the resistor ratio | a = n/(2*Q^2) - 1; m = a + sqrt(a^2 - 1); real solution needs a >= 1, i.e. n >= 4*Q^2 (derived) | Q, n | Follower (unity-gain buffer or discrete follower) sections | calc | p.410 fn 29 | medium |
| AOE-2282 | components | A VCVS section needs an op-amp much faster than the filter; rule of thumb from several authorities | fT >= 50*Q^2*fc with Q = 1/(3 - K); alternatives fT > 50*fc or fT > 50*K*fc ("all in the same ballpark", approximate) | fT (Hz), fc (Hz), K | Sallen-Key/VCVS sections | calc | p.410 fn 30 | high |
| AOE-2283 | filter | VCVS strengths: 2 poles per op-amp, noninverting gain, low output impedance, small value spread, easy gain adjustment, high gain or Q possible; weaknesses: sensitivity to component values and amplifier gain, poor for stable tunable filters | qualitative | - | Choose state-variable/biquad for tunable, stable filters | review | p.410 §6.3.2E | high |
| AOE-2284 | filter | State-variable filter (UAF42, MAX274/275) gives LP, HP and BP outputs at once, tunes at constant Q (or constant bandwidth), and has low component sensitivity with modest op-amp bandwidth demands; frequency goes as 1/R_F, so use a linear tuning scheme (Fig. 6.34) if tuning must be linear | f0 proportional to 1/R_F | R_F | Good choice for sharp (high-Q) bandpass | review | p.410-411 §6.3.3 | high |
| AOE-2285 | filter | State-variable bandpass (Fig. 6.31, IC with internal C and 10k resistors) design equations | R_F = 5.03e7/f0 (ohm); R_Q = 1e5/(3.48*Q + G - 1) (ohm); R_G = 3.16e4*Q/G (ohm) | f0 (Hz), Q, G (band-center gain) | UAF42-class module; two R_F set f0, R_Q and R_G set Q and gain; OCR dropped exponent/Q symbol in Eq. 6.9 | calc | p.410-411 Eqs. 6.8-6.10 | medium |
| AOE-2286 | filter | Four-op-amp state-variable bandpass variant with independent f0, Q and gain, each set by one resistor | f0 = 1/(2*pi*R_F*C) (Hz); Q = R1/R_Q; G = R1/R_G; R ~ 10k (noncritical, matched) | R_F, R_Q, R_G, R1 (ohm), C (F) | Fig. 6.32 (equation symbols partly OCR-garbled) | calc | p.411 Eqs. 6.11-6.14 | medium |
| AOE-2287 | filter | Biquad bandpass tunes frequency at constant bandwidth (Q rises proportionally with f0) | f0 = 1/(2*pi*R_F*C); BW = 1/(2*pi*R_B*C); G = R_B/R_G; Q = f0/BW = R_B/R_F | R_F, R_B, R_G (ohm), C (F) | Fig. 6.33 three-op-amp biquad | calc | p.411 Eqs. 6.15-6.17 | high |

| AOE-2306 | filter | SC filters alias input energy lying within a passband-width of the clock frequency straight into the passband, unattenuated, and nothing at the output can remove it: pre-filter the input (a simple RC usually suffices) and prefer a high clock-to-corner ratio | aliased band = f_clk +/- passband; e.g. MAX7400 as 1 kHz LP (f_clk = 100 kHz): 99-101 kHz input lands in dc-1 kHz; prefer 100:1 over 25:1 or 50:1 (1000:1 available, MSHN series) | f_clk, fc, input spectrum | High ratio also reduces the staircase output | calc | p.417-418 §6.3.6D | high |

| AOE-2308 | filter | For zero dc error in a lowpass, put the filter outside the dc path (LTC1062/MAX280 dc-accurate filter): low frequencies couple passively, the SC filter only shunts HF; SC noise appears only near rolloff | f3dB = f_CLK/100; example input R = 25.8k with 10 nF; external clock must swing rail-to-rail (small series resistor to protect input) or cap from CLK to ground for internal oscillator | f_CLK | Low-level dc-accurate lowpass; cascade two for higher order | review | p.418 §6.3.6D, Figs. 6.46-6.47 | high |

| AOE-2311 | filter | DSP filters: FIR output is a weighted sum of input samples (coefficients ~ inverse Fourier transform of the desired response) and may use "future" samples at the cost of overall delay; IIR (recursive) also feeds back outputs; one-pole IIR mimics an RC lowpass | FIR: y_i = sum_k a_k*x_(i-k); IIR: y_i = b*y_(i-1) + (1-b)*x_i with b = exp(-t_s/(R*C)) (t_s = sample interval, s) | a_k, b, t_s | Discrete-time filtering (§6.3.7B, Fig. 6.48) | calc | p.419 §6.3.7B | high |

| AOE-2313 | filter | One-pole IIR lowpass coefficient for a time constant of 20 sample intervals; the recursive filter approximates an RC lowpass better as tau >> t_s | b = exp(-t_s/tau) = exp(-1/20) = 0.95123; y_i = 0.95123*y_(i-1) + 0.04877*x_i | tau/t_s | Text also calls this a 3 dB point of "1/20 fs" (OCR); a true RC with tau = 20*t_s has f3dB = fs/(40*pi); treat the stated f3dB as unreliable | calc | p.420 §6.3.7C | medium |

| AOE-2316 | filter | Hamming-windowed half-band recipe: multiply sinc by the window, then normalize so the coefficients sum to 1 (unit dc gain); coefficients are symmetric | w(k) = 0.54 + 0.46*cos(2*pi*k/N) (Eq. 6.20); N = 8 result: a0 = +0.497374, a(+/-1) = +0.273977, a(+/-2) = 0, a(+/-3) = -0.022664, a(+/-4) = 0 | N | Stopband ~55-60 dB with Hamming | calc | p.421 §6.3.7D, Eq. 6.20 | high |
| AOE-2317 | filter | Window choice trades stopband depth for transition steepness; stated attenuations are realized only with adequate arithmetic precision and ADC linearity | Hamming ~55 dB (also quoted ~60 dB); Blackman-Harris w(k) = a0 + a1*cos(2*pi*k/N) + a2*cos(4*pi*k/N) + a3*cos(6*pi*k/N): 3-term [0.42323, 0.49755, 0.07922, 0] ~85 dB; 4-term [0.35875, 0.48829, 0.14128, 0.01168] ~105 dB | window, N | N = 32 half-band comparison (Fig. 6.51); windows ref. F. J. Harris, Proc. IEEE 66, 51-83 (1978) | sim | p.421 §6.3.7D, Fig. 6.51 | high |

| AOE-2319 | components | Filters that must stay linear (e.g. high-quality audio): low-distortion op-amps with adequate bandwidth, slew rate and loop gain (e.g. LT1115, OPA627, AD8599; Table 5.4), and linear passives: no high-K ceramics (large capacitance change with voltage) or electrolytics (dielectric-absorption memory); use film (ideally polypropylene) or NP0/C0G ceramic; LC-filter inductors on cores of good linearity (air-core practical up to ~1 mH) | capacitor dielectric in {polypropylene film, NP0/C0G}; L_air <= ~1 mH | dielectric, inductor core | Amplitude-linear filtering | inspect | p.422 §6.3.8A | high |

| AOE-2324 | timing | Classic op-amp/comparator Schmitt-trigger RC relaxation oscillator (threshold at one-half the output swing, symmetric supplies): period is set by RC only and is independent of supply voltage | T = 2.2*R*C (s); R in ohm, C in F | R, C | Fig 7.1A: rail-to-rail comparator output, +/-5 V supplies, positive-feedback divider to half-scale; careful RC relaxation designs reach better than 1% frequency stability | calc | p.425 §7.1.2A Fig. 7.1 | high |
| AOE-2325 | components | In a relaxation oscillator use a comparator with clean rail-to-rail CMOS output (e.g., TLC3702, LMC6762) above a few kHz; an op-amp must slew rail-to-rail every half cycle (even LM6152, fT 75 MHz, 45 V/us, is good only to ~100 kHz); an uncompensated op-amp is acceptable because there is no linear negative feedback | op-amp version f_max ~100 kHz at fT=75 MHz, SR=45 V/us; TLV3501 (2.7-5.5 V, t_pd 3 ns) reaches tens of MHz (10 kHz in Fig 7.1B); micropower LPV7215 < 1 uA supply, t_pd ~10 us, use R ~10 Mohm (input current < 1 pA) | f_osc, comparator t_pd, op-amp SR and fT | Fig 7.1A/B; single-supply variant needs one extra resistor plus speed-up capacitor | review | p.425-426 §7.1.2A fn.1-3 Fig. 7.1 | high |
| AOE-2326 | timing | The textbook CMOS-inverter RC relaxation oscillator (Fig 7.2A) oscillates parasitically (~100 MHz) at every transition because of the slow input edge; add a small speed-up capacitor across the feedback path | C_speedup = 47 pF (tested value); parasitic ~90 MHz measured on a 1 kHz 74HC04 oscillator at 5 V | edge waveform at 40 ns/div | 74HC04 at +5 V; 47 pF was also needed in the Fig 7.4A/B variants | measure | p.426-427 §7.1.2B Fig. 7.2, 7.3, 7.4 | high |
| AOE-2327 | protection | In the CMOS-inverter RC oscillator the timing capacitor drives the input clamp diodes into conduction every cycle; the series input resistor (R2) must limit that current, or use a 2:1 divider so the capacitor swing stays inside the rails (Fig 7.4A) | I_clamp limited by R2 (qualitative; no number given) | R2, V_swing | CMOS logic relaxation oscillators | inspect | p.426-427 Fig. 7.2B, 7.4A | low |
| AOE-2328 | timing | Do not rely on a Schmitt-trigger-inverter RC oscillator (74HC14, Fig 7.5) for frequency accuracy: logic hysteresis is loosely specified, so expect >=50% unit-to-unit frequency spread with identical R and C, f roughly proportional to supply voltage, a few % edge jitter, and sensitivity to digital supply noise | 74HC14 hysteresis 0.5-1.5 V; rising threshold 1.8-3.5 V; falling threshold 1.0-2.5 V; spread >= 50% | V_DD, R, C | guaranteed to oscillate with clean full-swing output; accuracy not guaranteed | review | p.427 §7.1.2B fn.5 Fig. 7.5 | high |
| AOE-2329 | timing | 555 astable: TRIGGER acts below 1/3 Vcc, THRESHOLD above 2/3 Vcc, so the capacitor cycles between 1/3 and 2/3 Vcc and the period is independent of supply voltage | T = 0.693*(RA + 2*RB)*C (s) | RA, RB (ohm), C (F) | Fig 7.9 example: Vcc = +15 V, threshold at +10 V | calc | p.428-429 §7.1.3 Fig. 7.8, 7.9 | high |
| AOE-2330 | timing | Classic 555 astable duty cycle (fraction HIGH) is always > 50% because C charges through RA+RB but discharges through RB only; use the Fig 7.10A/B circuits for 50% or fully variable duty | t_high = 0.693*(RA+RB)*C; t_low = 0.693*RB*C; D = (RA+RB)/(RA+2*RB) > 0.5 | RA, RB, C | derived from the stated charge/discharge paths | calc | p.429 §7.1.3B fn.6 | medium |
| AOE-2331 | components | Bipolar 555: single supply 4.5-16 V, frequency stability approaching 1%, good immunity to supply variation because thresholds track Vcc | Vcc = 4.5-16 V (Table 7.1 lists 4.5-18 V max); stability ~1% | Vcc | original bipolar 555 | review | p.429 §7.1.3 Table 7.1 | high |

| AOE-2338 | timing | Ratiometric design: when a timer's thresholds track the supply (555: V+/3 and 2V+/3) a constant charging current makes f vary with V+ (e.g., a 9 V battery); make the charging current proportional to V+ (Vbe-cancelled current source, Fig 7.13B) so f is supply-independent | I_charge proportional to V+ => f independent of V+ | V+ range, current-source topology | a plain pnp source is off by the ~0.6 V Vbe; Fig 7.13A diode compensation is imperfect; 7.13B cancels Vbe except for mismatch | calc | p.431 §7.1.3D Fig. 7.13 | medium |

| AOE-2348 | filter | Sine from square wave by lowpass filtering: put the corner above the fundamental but below the first harmonic present (2*f0, or 3*f0 for a 50% square wave which has only odd harmonics) | f0 < f_c < 2*f0 (3*f0 for 50% duty) | f0, duty, f_c | measured: 1 kHz 555 + 8-pole Butterworth at 1.5 kHz = 0.6% distortion; at 1.2 kHz = 0.1% (-60 dBc); a fixed filter tolerates only ~+/-25% frequency change | calc | p.435-436 §7.1.5A fn.22-23 | high |
| AOE-2349 | filter | Frequency-agile sine from square: tracking switched-capacitor lowpass clocked from the same source; output amplitude equals the square wave's fundamental | MAX294 8-pole elliptic, f_c = f_clk/100, f_clk = 128*f_in => f_c = 1.28*f_in; measured 0.03% distortion, <= 0.1% over 100 Hz-10 kHz; A_pp = (4/pi)*Vcc (2.25 Vrms at Vcc = 5 V) | f_in, f_clk, Vcc | Fig 7.18/7.19; LTC1799 datasheet variant adds a notch at 3*f0 | calc | p.436 §7.1.5A fn.24-25 Fig. 7.18, 7.19 | high |
| AOE-2350 | control-loop | Wien-bridge oscillator (equal R's and C's): the RC network's phase shifts cancel at f = 1/(2*pi*R*C) where its attenuation is exactly 1/3, so the noninverting amplifier gain must be exactly +3.00; below it oscillation dies, above it the output saturates; keep amplitude in the linear region with a gain-control element | f = 1/(2*pi*R*C) (Hz); A_v = 3.00 | R, C, gain network | Fig 7.20 | calc | p.436 §7.1.5B Fig. 7.20; p.470 Review §D | high |
| AOE-2351 | control-loop | Wien-bridge amplitude-control loop must be slow compared with one oscillation period (example: 2 s time constant); fast control distorts by correcting amplitude within a cycle | tau_AGC >> 1/f_osc (2 s used in Fig 7.20B) | tau_AGC, f_osc | JFET, lamp, or photoresistor gain control | calc | p.437 §7.1.5B Fig. 7.20B | high |
| AOE-2352 | components | Wien-bridge gain-control element capability: incandescent lamp gives < 0.003% harmonic distortion for audio frequencies above 1 kHz; LED-photoresistor optocoupler resistance is linear to < 0.1% distortion for < 1 Vrms applied and responds in tens of ms (slowness helps) | lamp THD < 0.003% (f > 1 kHz); photoresistor distortion < 0.1% at V < 1 Vrms | element type, f, amplitude | LTC AN5, AN43 | review | p.437 §7.1.5B Fig. 7.20A, 7.21 | high |
| AOE-2353 | control-loop | Two-op-amp inverting Wien (Fig 7.22) sustains oscillation at stage gain -2.00: make the fixed gain resistor ~5% below critical and let a series JFET add the rest; guarantee start-up by requiring JFET R_on(Vgs=0) below the added resistance | R_on(Vgs=0) < 1 k => g_m,min >= 1 mS (2N5458 g_m,min = 1.5 mS); control-loop unity-gain ~50 Hz; OPA627 at +/-15 V, 1 kHz, 10 V out | JFET g_m,min, R5 | inverting configuration avoids common-mode distortion | calc | p.437-438 §7.1.5B fn.28 Fig. 7.22 | high |

| AOE-2356 | components | Achievable Wien-bridge purity with conventional parts: 0.0002% THD (-114 dB, 2 ppm) after trimming R3/R4; photoresistive control plus filtered integrator < 3 ppm; treat "parts-per-billion" linearity claims for analog circuits skeptically | THD 2 ppm (trimmed), < 3 ppm (AN132) | THD target | lower amplitude reduces JFET distortion but raises relative op-amp noise | measure | p.437-438 §7.1.5B fn.27, fn.31 | high |
| AOE-2357 | timing | RC phase-shift oscillator (3 RC sections + inverting amplifier): the loaded ladder reaches 180 deg at about w = 2.4/(R*C) with a loss factor of 26 (ideal isolated sections: w = sqrt(3)/(R*C), loss 8); amplifier gain magnitude must exceed the loss; take the output from the last RC section, not the clipped amplifier output | w_180 ~ 2.4/(R*C) rad/s; abs(Gv) > 26 (Gv = -36 used, conservative) | R, C, Gv | Fig 7.23 at 1 kHz: amplifier output clips rail-to-rail (+/-5 V); last-section output after x5 gain = 1 V amplitude with 0.9% distortion | calc | p.439 §7.1.5C fn.32 Fig. 7.23 | high |
| AOE-2358 | timing | Integrator (270 deg lag including inversion) plus two RC sections forms a phase-shift oscillator; back-to-back diode clamps limit amplitude; take output from the last RC section; putting the diodes across the first phase-shift capacitor instead yields a low-distortion 90 deg (quadrature) wave at the integrator | measured 1 kHz, 1 V amplitude, 1% distortion | R, C (39 nF in Fig 7.24) | Fig 7.24 | measure | p.439 §7.1.5C Fig. 7.24 | high |
| AOE-2359 | timing | Above ~1 MHz generate sinewaves with a resonator (LC, crystal) oscillator; LC oscillators are self-starting; Colpitts (JFET, 20 MHz) distortion typically < -60 dB; the ECL MC1648 emitter-coupled VCO runs to 200 MHz but its spectral purity is mediocre compared with a single-JFET Clapp oscillator | f0 = 1/(2*pi*sqrt(L*C)) (Hz) | L (H), C (F) | Fig 7.25 (Colpitts, Hartley, link-coupled output), Fig 7.26, Fig 7.30 | calc | p.439-440 §7.1.5D Fig. 7.25, 7.26 | high |
| AOE-2360 | components | Varactor-tuned LC oscillator: varactor Cmax few pF to few hundred pF with ~3:1 capacitance ratio (hyperabrupt up to 15:1) gives up to 4:1 frequency range, typically +/-25%; for stability restrict the tuning range with a large fixed capacitor, feed the dc bias through a large resistor (no tank loading) and a dc-blocking capacitor | f proportional to 1/sqrt(C_total); Fig 7.28: 100 pF fixed + 15 pF max varactor => +/-1% tuning | C_fixed, C_var range | Armstrong-type JFET oscillator, Fig 7.27 tuning curves | calc | p.440-441 §7.1.5D Fig. 7.27, 7.28 | high |
| AOE-2361 | timing | The oscillation voltage across a varactor causes waveform distortion and amplitude-dependent frequency: keep oscillation amplitude small (amplify in later stages), keep the dc varactor bias above ~1 V, and use a back-to-back series varactor pair so signal-induced capacitance changes cancel | V_bias,dc > ~1 V | V_osc, V_bias | Clapp oscillator Fig 7.29 (60 MHz LO in a PLL) measured ~10 dB better signal purity than an HP 3325A synthesizer | review | p.441 §7.1.5D Fig. 7.29, 7.30 | high |
| AOE-2362 | filter | VCO embedded in a PLL: add an LC trap on the tuning path at the phase-detector reference frequency to suppress reference spurs and keep the free-running spectral purity | trap at f_ref (200 kHz in the example) | f_ref | Fig 7.29 with Fig 6.8 | inspect | p.441 fn.35 | high |
| AOE-2363 | control-loop | Parasitic oscillations (unintended Hartley/Colpitts from lead inductance and interelectrode capacitance) show up as fuzz on part of a waveform, erratic current sources, unexplained op-amp offsets, or behavior that changes when a probe/finger touches the circuit; a small resistor in series with the transistor base often cures it by lowering HF common-base gain | op-amp parasitics typically near fT (~1 MHz); discrete small-signal transistors 10s-100s of MHz; Fig 7.31 current source varied 5%-10% within its compliance range | symptoms | any active circuit with gain | measure | p.442 §7.1.5E Fig. 7.31 | high |
| AOE-2364 | emc | Distinguish pickup from oscillation by its frequency: 60 or 120 Hz (50/100 Hz outside the Americas) = powerline coupling (capacitive to high-Z nodes, magnetic into loop area, or ground loops, e.g., instruments on different outlets); 20 kHz-1 MHz = switching power supplies; broadcast RF (US): AM 0.5-1.7 MHz, FM 88-108 MHz, TV 55-700 MHz; beware digital-scope aliasing | frequency bands as listed | observed interference frequency | lab debug | measure | p.442-443 §7.1.5E fn.36-37 | high |
| AOE-2365 | requirements | Choose oscillator class by stability need: RC/current-source relaxation ~0.1% stability with only 5%-10% initial predictability (fine for e.g. a ~100 Hz multiplexed-display refresh; RC-based oscillators no better than ~1% overall per the chapter review); LC ~0.01% over reasonable periods (review: 10-100 ppm); quartz crystal (Q typically 1e4-1e5) for real stability | RC 0.1%-1%; LC 1e-4 to 1e-5; crystal ppm class | required stability | see Table 7.2 and XO/TCXO/OCXO data in §2 | review | p.443 §7.1.6; p.471 Review §E | high |
| AOE-2366 | components | Quartz crystal model: C0 (electrode + lead capacitance) in parallel with a motional series R1-L1-C1; the series (fr = fs) and antiresonant (fa) frequencies lie within ~0.1%; at fs the impedance is minimum and equals R1 | fs = 1/(2*pi*sqrt(L1*C1)); typical 1 MHz crystal: C0 = 4 pF, L1 = 1 H, C1 = 0.02 pF, R1 ~75 ohm (Q = 1e5) | C0, L1, C1, R1 | Fig 7.33, 7.34; OCR shows R1 as "750" - Q = 1e5 at 1 MHz implies ~75 ohm | calc | p.444 §7.1.6A Fig. 7.33 | medium |
| AOE-2367 | components | Parallel-mode crystals are calibrated for a stated external load capacitance CL (typically 10-35 pF): the oscillator must present that CL including wiring and chip capacitance; match the driving IC's specified mode (series/parallel), max Rs and CL, preferably using its listed known-good crystals | CL = 10-35 pF typical | CL_circuit, CL_spec, Rs_max | - | inspect | p.444 §7.1.6A | high |
| AOE-2368 | timing | Load capacitance pulls the parallel resonance strongly while the series resonance is nearly immune | fp = fs*(1 + C1/(2*(C0 + CL))); CL_required = fs*C1/(2*(fp - fs)) - C0 | fs, C0, C1, CL | measured 10 MHz crystal: CL = 30 pF pulled the parallel resonance ~2000 ppm down; series resonance moved < 1 ppm for CL 0-30 pF | calc | p.445 fn.41 Fig. 7.35-7.37 | high |
| AOE-2369 | timing | Parallel-mode stability budget: drift of the external circuit's capacitance becomes frequency drift | 0.1 ppm stability requires external capacitance change <= 0.002 pF (10 MHz crystal, CL = 30 pF) | dCL | assumes the crystal itself is that stable; hard for the loop amplifier | calc | p.445 §7.1.6B | high |
| AOE-2370 | components | Design the crystal oscillator for the worst-case motional resistance R1, not a good sample: higher R1 flattens the phase slope so loop phase variations must be ~10x smaller for the same stability, and a marginal circuit may not start or may oscillate at an unrelated frequency | 10 MHz crystal: R1 = 4.7 ohm gives ~13 deg/ppm; worst-case R1 = 50 ohm gives ~1.3 deg/ppm; for 0.1 ppm the loop phase must hold within 0.13 deg (worst case) vs 1.3 deg | R1_max, loop phase variation | CTS MP100 10.0 MHz +/-45 ppm series-mode crystal | calc | p.445-446 §7.1.6B Fig. 7.36, 7.37 | high |
| AOE-2371 | test | Extract crystal motional parameters from impedance-analyzer data: measure fs, fa (C0 only) and fp with a known CL | C1 = 2*(1 - fp/fa)/(1/C0 - 1/(C0 + CL)); L1 = 1/(C1*(2*pi*fs)^2) | fs, fa, fp, C0, CL | example: fs = 10.000086 MHz (+8.6 ppm), R1 = 4.736 ohm, C0 = 5.5 pF, fa = 10.02245 MHz, fp = 10.00355 MHz at CL = 30 pF => L1 = 10.3324 mH, C1 = 0.024515 pF (HP4192A analyzer) | measure | p.444-445 fn.40 | medium |
| AOE-2372 | components | VCXO (varactor pulling of a parallel-mode crystal) gives only small deviation; buy a commercial VCXO rather than design one; for wide or digital frequency control synthesize (PLL or DDS) from a fixed crystal reference, which keeps the crystal's stability | VCXO max deviation typically +/-10 to +/-100 ppm (wide-deviation units to +/-1000 ppm) | required pull range | - | review | p.446 §7.1.6C | high |
| AOE-2373 | timing | Inverter (Pierce-type) crystal oscillator: bias an unbuffered CMOS inverter linear with a high-value feedback resistor; C1 and C2 to ground (in series, midpoint grounded) form the load capacitance and provide the 180 deg that the inverter completes to 360 deg; choose series resistor R2 comparable to the reactance of C2 at f_osc | R2 ~ 1/(2*pi*f_osc*C2): ~330 kohm at 32 kHz, ~1 kohm at 5 MHz; CL_eff = C1*C2/(C1 + C2) + C_stray (derived) | f_osc, C1, C2 | unbuffered inverter (e.g., LVC1404; AUP1GU04 down to 0.8 V); trim by varying one load capacitor (e.g., 32 pF); Fig 7.38D | calc | p.446-447 §7.1.6D fn.45 Fig. 7.38 | medium |
| AOE-2374 | requirements | Convert timekeeping requirements into fractional frequency stability: a day is 86,400 s, so 1 part in 1e4 drifts about a minute per week; a quartz wristwatch runs on ~1 uW (1 uA at 1.5 V, 3 years on a 28 mAh cell) | drift (s/day) = 86400*(df/f) | df/f | - | calc | p.448 §7.1.6D Fig. 7.39 | high |
| AOE-2375 | power | Micropower CMOS crystal oscillator: inverter "class-A" shoot-through makes supply current climb steeply with V_DD; add resistors (10 k) in the inverter's supply leads, run the oscillator at ~1 V, and drive a separately biased full-voltage output inverter through a blocking capacitor and large feedback resistor | 32.768 kHz, 74AUP (0.8-3.3 V): supply resistors cut current 20-50x; 1.0 V oscillator + 1.8 V output stage = 2.4 uA total vs 12.8 uA for the 2-stage oscillator at 1.8 V (13.8 uA with 2.5 V output); dedicated EM7604 <= 0.3 uA typ at 3-5 V (1.2-5.5 V) | V_DD, R3 = R4, output voltage | Fig 7.40A-C, Fig 7.41 | measure | p.448-449 §7.1.6D fn.46 Fig. 7.40, 7.41 | high |
| AOE-2376 | timing | Crystal oscillator start-up criteria (Barkhausen): loop gain A*B > 1 and total loop phase = integer * 360 deg at the intended frequency; excessive crystal loss (Rs) can prevent oscillation while the shunt capacitance alone lets the loop oscillate at a frequency unrelated to the crystal; use a crystal cut for the mode (series or parallel) the circuit needs | A*B > 1; loop phase = n*360 deg | A, crystal Rs, C0, mode | discrete and IC-internal oscillators (RCA ICAN-6539) | calc | p.449 §7.1.6E fn.47-48 | high |
| AOE-2377 | timing | Crystal-to-IC implementation: mount the crystal as close to the IC as possible (XTAL pins are sensitive to board parasitics); crystals with high shunt capacitance on a series-resonant input may need a resistor across the terminals to suppress 3rd-harmonic oscillation; qualify the actual crystal vendor/part and load capacitors over the full V_DD and temperature range (vendors' datasheet capacitor values are "not optimized"; one maker's crystal may fail where another's with similar specs works, due to poorly specified ESR and mount capacitance) | qualitative | layout distance, crystal vendor, Vdd/T range | MPC9230 (series-resonant), PIC16F7x (parallel-cut) datasheet guidance | measure | p.449 §7.1.6E | high |
| AOE-2378 | components | For bulletproof clocking prefer a complete crystal oscillator module (guaranteed to oscillate, logic-level output; any IC that takes a crystal also accepts a square-wave clock) over a bare crystal plus IC oscillator | module ~$1.50 vs bare crystal ~$0.30 (100 pcs); typical accuracy over temperature, supply and time 0.01% (100 ppm); stock 1-100 MHz (1, 2, 4, 5, 6, 8, 10, 16, 20 MHz; 14.31818 MHz video; 22.118 MHz 8051 serial) | required accuracy, frequency | programmable (PLL-based) modules 1-125 MHz ~$5, one-time programmable (field programmer ~$500); ECS-300C pin-selects 1/2-1/256 | review | p.449-450 §7.1.6F fn.49-50 | high |
| AOE-2379 | components | Ceramic resonators sit between LC and quartz: drop-in for crystal circuits, cheap, pullable over a few parts per thousand, but only +/-0.3% initial accuracy and 0.2%-1% stability over temperature and time; do not use where UART/USB-class accuracy or ppm timing is required | 200 kHz-50 MHz; +/-0.3%; stability 0.2%-1%; $0.15-0.25 ($0.25-0.50 with built-in capacitors) | required accuracy | Abracon, AVX, ECS, Murata, Panasonic, TDK | review | p.450 §7.1.6G | high |
| AOE-2380 | components | SAW oscillators cover frequencies above crystal range with crystal-module size and comparable stability, but few standard frequencies and high price; bare SAW resonators (e.g., 433 MHz) plus one transistor make a cheap oscillator/transmitter | SAW modules 100 MHz-1 GHz, ~50 ppm over temperature, > $50; bare SAW resonator ~$1 | frequency, stability | - | review | p.450 §7.1.6H | high |
| AOE-2381 | timing | Stability of crystal oscillator grades over temperature: plain crystal oscillator gives a few ppm over normal temperature ranges "without great care"; TCXO 1 ppm over 0-50 degC (inexpensive) down to 0.1 ppm (expensive); OCXO uses a crystal with zero tempco at 80-90 degC held there by an oven | XO few ppm; TCXO 1 to 0.1 ppm (0-50 degC); OCXO few parts in 1e11 (seconds to hours, Symmetricom 1000B, 10 MHz) | required stability, temperature range | modules from many vendors (SMT and DIP) | review | p.450-451 §7.1.7 | high |
| AOE-2382 | timing | Once temperature is controlled, crystal aging (frequency tends to increase continuously), supply-voltage variations, and shock/vibration dominate; budget aging and allow break-in | 1000B OCXO aging <= 1e-10 per day (max, after a month of break-in); well-aged units typically "parts in 1e11 per day"; aging settles after a few months | aging rate, operating life | miniature OCXO: ~1.3 cm^3, 0.15 W heater (resistive plating on crystal) | calc | p.451 §7.1.7A fn.51 | high |
| AOE-2383 | timing | Beyond OCXO use atomic or GPS-disciplined references | Rb: few parts in 1e11; Cs beam: few parts in 1e13; H maser: approaching few parts in 1e14; GPS-disciplined receiver: 10 MHz stable to 1e-12 averaged over a day, 1 pps accurate to 20 ns (~$4,500); consumer GPS receivers do not regenerate a reference frequency | stability requirement | GPS L1/L2 at 1.575/1.228 GHz; Cs defines the second | review | p.451 §7.1.7B | high |
| AOE-2384 | timing | Direct digital synthesis (phase accumulator + sine ROM + DAC): output frequency and resolution are set by the tuning word; the output is a staircase so a reconstruction lowpass must pass f_out yet block the clock/step frequency, which limits the usable span | f_out = FTW*f_clk/2^n; phase step per clock = (360 deg/2^n)*FTW; resolution = f_clk/2^n | FTW, f_clk, n | AD9852: 150 MHz out, 48-bit tuning (microhertz), up to 100 million frequency updates/s, ~$15; AD9912: 1 GHz clock, f_out(max) = 400 MHz, 14-bit DAC, ~$37; phase-synchronized parts give precision quadrature | calc | p.451-452 §7.1.8A Fig. 7.42 | high |
| AOE-2385 | timing | PLL synthesis produces f_out as a rational multiple of a crystal reference with the reference's stability; the step size is f_ref/r | f_out = (n/r)*f_ref; e.g., 16 MHz crystal, r = 16 => 1 MHz steps, typical output range 25-500 MHz | n, r, f_ref | Table 7.2: PLL settling ~0-100 ms (OCR), DDS immediate; programmable oscillator modules (Epson SG8002, ECS ECSP, Citizen CSX-750P, CTS CP7, Cardinal CPP) | calc | p.452-453 §7.1.8B Table 7.2 | high |
| AOE-2386 | timing | A single integrator (or differentiator) does not make a quadrature oscillator: phase is 90 deg but amplitude scales with frequency; a two-integrator loop (each -90 deg) closed by a unity-gain inverter oscillates where each integrator has unity gain | f_osc = 1/(2*pi*R*C) (Xc = R); diode limiting to ~300 mV amplitude | R, C | Fig 7.43 tested: LMC6482 at +/-5 V, R = 15.8 k, C = 10 nF => 1 kHz | calc | p.453 §7.1.9A Fig. 7.43 | high |
| AOE-2387 | timing | Quadrature-oscillator amplitude limiting: a bare diode limiter clamps at ~300 mV with poor control; bias the diodes toward the rails with dividers for larger amplitude, or use a zener inside a diode bridge; below ~5 V replace soft-knee low-voltage zeners with a 2-terminal reference, and put a capacitor across the zener/reference to hold it through zero crossings | measured Fig 7.43A: 997 Hz, THD 0.006% (sine) and 0.02% (cosine); biased limiter (Fig 7.43B) 3.3 V amplitude; references LM385-1.2/-2.5 (1.24/2.50 V), AD1580 (1.22 V), ADR510 (1.0 V) | limiter type, target amplitude | Fig 7.43B, C | measure | p.454 §7.1.9A Fig. 7.43, 7.44 | high |
| AOE-2388 | timing | Switched-capacitor resonator quadrature oscillator: a comparator squares the output of a narrow SC bandpass (Q = 10) and feeds it back; frequency is set by the clock; output is a staircase approximation; amplitude is set by the square wave's fundamental | f_osc = f_clk/100; amplitude = (4/pi)*G*V_sq (G = R2/R1 = 0.2, V_sq = +/-5 V => 4/pi = 1.27 V) | f_clk, G, V_sq | usable from a few Hz to > 10 kHz; equal-amplitude sine/cosine; 50:1 clock ratio gives coarser steps | calc | p.454-455 §7.1.9B Fig. 7.45, 7.46 | high |
| AOE-2389 | timing | Wideband quadrature from one sinewave: drive a phase-sequence network (cyclic sections of equal R and geometrically decreasing C) with the signal and its inverse; simple Weaver-type RC phasing networks work only over a narrow range and need precision parts | 6 sections: +/-0.7 deg over 40:1; 8 sections: 150:1; f_LOW ~ 0.2/(R*C); f_HIGH/f_LOW ~ C_first/C_last (caption partly garbled in OCR) | R, C_first, C_last, sections | SPICE 8-section, R = 10 k, C = 40 nF: accurate quadrature 400 Hz-50 kHz; it is the phase difference of output pairs that is 90 deg | sim | p.455-456 §7.1.9D Fig. 7.48, 7.49 | medium |
| AOE-2390 | timing | Quadrature square waves: generate twice the needed frequency and divide by 2 with a pair of type-D flip-flops; at RF use quadrature hybrids (core-wound transformers from a few MHz to ~1 GHz, stripline or waveguide above) | flip-flop method essentially perfect from dc to at least 100 MHz; hybrids typically an octave (2:1) bandwidth | f_out | Fig 7.50 | review | p.455-456 §7.1.9E-F Fig. 7.50 | high |
| AOE-2391 | timing | Arbitrary phase from an I/Q pair: a resistive (pot) mix of I and Q gives 0-90 deg independent of frequency, but amplitude dips 3 dB at 45 deg; add inverted I', Q' for full 360 deg; with DDS use the phase-offset word | DDS phase step = 360 deg/2^m (AD9951, m = 14 => 0.02 deg); AD9854 quadrature DDS: 300 MHz clock, 12-bit DACs to 150 MHz, 48-bit (1 uHz) tuning | m, required phase resolution | 256-QAM carries 8 bits/symbol | calc | p.455-457 §7.1.9C, G Fig. 7.47 | high |
| AOE-2392 | timing | Timing jitter at a threshold crossing equals the total noise voltage divided by the signal slew rate: square up slow waveforms and make the crossing edge as fast as possible before any timing decision | dt = v_n/S, v_n = v_n(sig) + v_n(th) (V), S = slew rate (V/s); sinewave S = 2*pi*f*V0 (peak amplitude V0, threshold at midpoint); pulse S ~ V_step/t_r | v_n(sig), v_n(th), f, V0, V_step, t_r | applies to RC relaxation and crystal oscillators, sampling clocks, data links; long-term stability characterized by Allan variance; example tempco spec 1 ppm/degC | calc | p.457 §7.1.10 Fig. 7.51 fn.54 | high |
| AOE-2393 | timing | Pulse from a step with an RC differentiator driving a MOSFET/BJT switch gives a width of order RC that depends on the switch threshold relative to the step, so it is somewhat unpredictable: use only for noncritical timing; for BJTs use a gold-doped fast switch and a series base resistor to limit peak base current, and keep the falling-edge input step <= ~6 V to avoid base-emitter reverse breakdown | width ~ R*C (MOSFET); PN2369 storage time 13 ns max vs 200 ns for 2N3904; gold-doped parts (MMBT2369, 2N5771, PN3640 at 20 ns) rated only 12-20 V, low beta, higher leakage | R, C, V_step, V_th | Fig 7.52A-C | review | p.458 §7.2.1A fn.55 Fig. 7.52 | high |
| AOE-2394 | test | For fast pulse circuits include scope-probe capacitance in the SPICE model; with it simulation matched bench waveforms (delay, Vce(sat), base voltage, ~40 mV overshoot above +5 V) | probe capacitance 8 pF at base and collector; Fig 7.53: R1 = 220 ohm, C1 = 47 pF, R2 = 2.2 k, Rc = 470 ohm, 5 V step with 5 ns fall, 20 ns/div | probe C | falling-edge BJT circuit of Fig 7.52C | sim | p.458-459 Fig. 7.53 | high |

| AOE-2397 | timing | 555 monostable (capacitor on TH only, TR' as trigger): the trigger must end before the output pulse ends; CMOS 555 covers ~1 us to 100 s; very large R is allowed because TH input current < 10 nA; minimum R is set by the maximum DIS current and chip speed | t = 1.1*R*C (s); R up to 10-100 Mohm; I_DIS,max ~15 mA at 5 V => R_min ~ V+/15 mA (derived) | R, C, V+ | Fig 7.57: input RC differentiator turns a falling step into a trigger (~100 ms output example); OCR shows "1A RC" | calc | p.460 §7.2.1E Fig. 7.57 | medium |
| AOE-2398 | timing | Use a packaged monostable (one-shot) IC instead of RC plus discrete transistors or gates: DIY one-shots push gate inputs beyond the supply rails and depend on input-circuit details; one-shots are edge triggered by logic levels and give Q and Q' of width set by external R and C | family supply V+ from +2 V to +15 V | logic family | §7.2.2 | review | p.461 §7.2.2 | high |
| AOE-2399 | timing | Monostable timing interval: after the trigger the capacitor is dumped to the lower threshold and recharges through R to the upper threshold | t = R*C*ln((V+ - VL)/(V+ - VH)) (Eq. 7.1); VL, VH = lower/upper thresholds; discharge current typically 30-80 mA, so 0.01 uF takes ~600 ns to fall from 5 V to 1.6 V (folded into the K factor) | R, C, V+, VL, VH | Fig 7.58 internal scheme | calc | p.461 §7.2.2A Eq. 7.1 fn.58 | high |
| AOE-2400 | timing | Give one-shots at least their minimum trigger width; a too-short trigger yields a substandard or jittery output pulse | typical minimum 25-100 ns; 4538: 140 ns at +5 V, 60 ns at +15 V; 'HCT423: 25 ns at +5 V; 'LVC123: 3 ns at +3.3 V | trigger width, V+ | trigger may be shorter or longer than the output pulse | calc | p.461, p.464 §7.2.2B-C | high |
| AOE-2401 | timing | Check each one-shot's input gating truth table: in the OR-gated '4538 the unused input must be disabled, in the AND-gated '123 unused inputs must be enabled; the '123 fires when RESET is released if both trigger inputs are already asserted (usually unwanted; the '423 omits this); ensure the signals that enable the inputs cannot themselves trigger an extra pulse at the ends of a pulse train | qualitative (truth-table review) | gating type, reset sequencing | Table 7.3, Fig 7.59; RESET can suppress power-up pulses except for the '123 caveat | review | p.462, p.464-465 §7.2.2B-C Fig. 7.59 | high |
| AOE-2402 | timing | Know whether a monostable is retriggerable: retriggerable parts ('123, '423, '4538) restart timing on a new trigger and end one pulse width after the last trigger; the '221 is non-retriggerable; allow the minimum retrigger recovery time, which grows with timing capacitance | 74LVC1G123: minimum wait 1 us with a 10 nF timing capacitor (1% of the 100 us pulse with 10 k) | retrigger interval, C | Table 7.3 | calc | p.462, p.464 §7.2.2B-C | high |
| AOE-2403 | components | Predict one-shot width with the manufacturer's K factor plus a fixed minimum: K clusters at 0.7 (all '4538 parts) or ~0.45 or ~1.0 (other part numbers, depending on manufacturer), so same-numbered parts from different vendors are NOT interchangeable; include t_min for short pulses (datasheets omit it) and lock the vendor in the BOM | t_w = K*R*C + t_min; 74HC123 at R = 10 k, C = 100 nF: FSC/Toshiba 0.9-1.1 ms (1.0 typ), NXP 0.45 ms typ (measured FSC 1.05 ms, TI 0.42 ms); t_min ~45-390 ns (Table 7.4); K varies with supply voltage (Fig 7.60) | K, R, C, t_min, vendor | if K is not stated, read the width at R = 10 k, C = 100 nF | calc | p.463 §7.2.2C fn.59-61 Table 7.4 Fig. 7.60 | high |
| AOE-2404 | timing | Monostable accuracy budget: expect a few % width change over 0-50 degC and +/-5% supply plus +/-10% unit-to-unit; self-heating and supply glitches during the pulse also shift width; specs are quoted in the favorable middle of the width range and degrade at its extremes | 4538: few % (0-50 degC, +/-5% V+); +/-10% unit-to-unit; widths 40 ns to ms (or seconds) | required width tolerance | for tighter or longer timing use digital counters (§7.2.4) | review | p.462-464 §7.2.2B-C | high |
| AOE-2405 | timing | Small timing capacitors (~1000 pF or less) make the fast discharge overshoot the lower threshold, enlarging and destabilizing the pulse width; prefer one-shots whose lower threshold sits near ground | SN74HC4538: VL ~4.3% of V+ (+0.2 V at 5 V); another admirable part uses VL ~10% of V+ | C_timing, VL | threshold ratios (R1-R3) vary by manufacturer and are rarely specified | review | p.463-464 §7.2.2C | high |
| AOE-2406 | timing | Long one-shot pulses: CMOS monostables accept timing resistors up to 10 Mohm (even if the datasheet shows only ~200 k); microfarad-class timing capacitors bring electrolytic leakage and high-k ceramic C variations (50% or more with temperature and voltage); add a diode across R so the charged timing capacitor cannot drive reverse current into the RCx pin when V+ drops abruptly | R_timing <= 10 Mohm; high-k ceramic dC >= 50% | R, C type, power-down behavior | Fig 7.61A | inspect | p.463-464 §7.2.2C fn.62 Fig. 7.61A | high |
| AOE-2407 | decoupling | One-shots have poorer noise immunity than ordinary logic: keep the timing R and C away from capacitively coupled signals and feed the monostable from a private RC-filtered V+ (or a small separate linear regulator) to prevent false triggering and width shifts from supply/ground glitches | qualitative | supply filtering | Fig 7.61B | inspect | p.464 §7.2.2C Fig. 7.61B | high |
| AOE-2408 | timing | Verify one-shot width at the maximum duty cycle: some parts shorten or become erratic near 100% duty | 74LV123 at 3.3 V, 10 k/100 nF: constant to 95% duty, ~1.5% shorter near 100%; '221 erratic at high duty; MM74HC123A exact to 99.98% duty and jitter-free | max duty cycle | - | measure | p.464 §7.2.2C | high |
| AOE-2409 | cables | Buffer outputs of one-shots and other flip-flop-containing devices (gate, inverter, or line driver) before they drive cables or equipment outside the instrument; load capacitance and cable reflections can cause erratic operation | qualitative | output net leaves board (Y/N) | - | inspect | p.464 §7.2.2C | high |
| AOE-2410 | timing | Do not overuse one-shots (a circuit full of them is the mark of a neophyte and cannot tolerate clock-rate changes); prefer synchronous delays made from D flip-flops clocked by the system clock (or a divided master clock) for better noise immunity, accepting up to one clock period of timing uncertainty | delay quantized to clock; jitter <= 1 clock period | clock period, delay | Fig 7.62 | review | p.465 §7.2.2C Fig. 7.62 | high |
| AOE-2411 | protection | Hardware pulse-width and duty limiter for software-driven high-current pulses (LEDs, solenoids): one-shot T1 gates the drive off after T1, and a second one-shot blocks retriggering until T2 after the last trigger, so a software bug or MCU crash cannot produce a destructive long pulse | T_out <= T1; duty <= T1/T2 (derived); example T1 = 100 us, T2 = 5 ms | T1, T2, load pulse rating | example: 60-LED ring driven 80 us every 8 ms (120 frame/s, 1% duty) at 1 A = 10x the 100 mA continuous rating, no heatsink; a single long pulse destroyed the array; Fig 7.63 (+22 V, 10 stacks of 6 LEDs) | calc | p.465 §7.2.3 Fig. 7.63 | high |
| AOE-2412 | timing | Use a fixed (crystal) oscillator plus digital counters instead of analog RC timing when delays are long (minutes to hours or days) or when real accuracy, stability, or predictability is required | qualitative | delay length, accuracy need | §7.2.4 | review | p.465 §7.2.4 | high |

| AOE-2422 | components | Total noise of several independent sources: sum the squares of each noise density, multiply by bandwidth, take the square root; output noise = RTI noise x gain. | vn_total = sqrt(sum(en_i^2) * B) (V rms); vout_n = G * vn_total | en_i (V/sqrt(Hz)), B (Hz), G | white, mutually uncorrelated sources | calc | p.473 §8 intro "quicker guide" | high |

| AOE-2426 | components | Johnson noise short-circuit current (Norton form). | inoise(rms) = sqrt(4kTB/R) A (eq 8.3); density in = sqrt(4kT/R) A/sqrt(Hz) (eq 8.5); voltage density en = sqrt(4kTR) V/sqrt(Hz) (eq 8.4); vn = en*sqrt(B) | R, T, B | white noise | calc | p.474 §8.1 eqs 8.3-8.5 | high |

| AOE-2428 | components | Johnson noise amplitude is Gaussian; use for crest-factor/peak estimates. | p(V)dV = (1/(Vn*sqrt(2*pi))) * exp(-V^2/(2*Vn^2)) dV; normalizing factor 0.4/Vn (exactly 1/sqrt(2*pi) = 0.3989) | Vn rms (V) | Johnson and shot noise (both white, Gaussian) | calc | p.475 §8.1.1 Fig. 8.2 | high |

| AOE-2432 | components | Do not apply the shot-noise formula to current in metallic conductors (long-range carrier correlation gives far less noise) or to the standard transistor current source (see §8.3.5). | in << sqrt(2qI) for metallic resistive circuits | circuit type | exception list to AOE-2430 | review | p.476 §8.1.2 | high |

Penalties of small Re: bias divider acts as a current source so the Q-point depends on beta, and the stiff bias divider (3.2k) gives low input impedance. | Re = 15 ohm -> en = 0.5 nV/sqrt(Hz); Gv = 30 dB; 5 parallel transistors (15 ohm ballast each) -> en / sqrt(5); result ~1 nV/sqrt(Hz) | Re, Rf, N | ac-coupled moving-coil phono preamp (NA323-like), +10 to +25 V | calc | p.495-496 §8.5.5A Fig. 8.34 | high |
| AOE-2484 | requirements | Worst-case specs of hard-to-measure noise parameters tend to be overly pessimistic; a max-NF spec can imply a large en. Convert before rejecting, and consider screening/selection. | ZTX384C: NF = 4 dB max (30 Hz-15 kHz, Rs = 2k, Ic = 0.2 mA) -> en = 6.9 nV/sqrt(Hz) equivalent; 2SC3324: 3 dB max vs 0.2 dB typ | NF max, Rs | datasheet interpretation | calc | p.495 §8.5.5A | high |
| AOE-2485 | components | DC-coupled differential low-noise preamp (2-stage op-amp style, current-source active loads) removes electrolytic blocking capacitors at the input and in the feedback divider, minimizes Early-effect nonlinearity, gives higher loop gain and predictable bias; cost is complexity and +3 dB noise (second input transistor). Match the input pair Vbe to ~10 mV, or use parallel matched arrays / larger-die low-rbb' transistors. | noise penalty +3 dB vs single-ended; pair Vbe match ~10 mV; e.g. 2SD2653 (2 A continuous, beta ~500 at Ic = 1 mA) | topology | audio preamp; noise dominated by rbb' Johnson noise at these Ic | review | p.496 §8.5.5B Fig. 8.35, footnote 35 | high |
| AOE-2486 | components | Device choice by source impedance: BJTs give very good noise for Rs ~200 ohm to 1 Mohm (optimum Ic from several mA down to ~1 uA; low Rs -> minimize en, high Ic; high Rs -> minimize in, low Ic). Above ~100k current noise dominates -> use a FET. Very low Rs (~50 ohm, say < 100 ohm) -> en dominates, poor NF -> consider a signal transformer (drawbacks: ac only, few decades bandwidth, LF units bulky/expensive/nonlinear, magnetic pickup). | BJT: 200 ohm < Rs < 1 M; FET: Rs > ~100 k; transformer: Rs < ~100 ohm | Rs | low-noise front-end technology selection | review | p.496-497 §8.5.6 | high |
| AOE-2487 | requirements | Photodiode "lightning detector" preamp requirements example: 9 V battery (6 V at end of life), input noise a few nV/sqrt(Hz), response a few us, cost $0.40 in 1000s. Back-bias the PIN photodiode to reduce capacitance (lower noise, faster); ac-couple into the transimpedance summing junction to reject ambient/leakage dc; gain G = -Rf V/A. | ground strikes: rise-time signatures < 5 us; ~90 % of lightning is cloud-to-cloud (slow) | supply, BW, noise target | discrete low-cost TIA | review | p.497 §8.5.7A Fig. 8.36, footnote 36 | high |
| AOE-2488 | components | At low collector currents (~0.1 mA, high node impedances) add a cascode on the gain stage; without it Miller effect multiplies load capacitance by the voltage gain (~1000) and kills bandwidth. Use an inverted (pnp) cascode when headroom is tight (battery sag to 6 V). | Miller C = C*(1 + Gv), Gv ~ 1000 | Ic, Gv, supply | low-power discrete transimpedance amps | review | p.497-499 §8.5.7C-D | high |
| AOE-2489 | components | TIA input noise budget (lightning preamp, input follower Q4 at 1 uA, beta ~350): base shot noise sqrt(2qIb) = 30 fA/sqrt(Hz) (Ib = 3 nA); 10M feedback + bias resistors sqrt(4kT/R) = 57 fA/sqrt(Hz); total 65 fA/sqrt(Hz). Voltage noise: Q4 re shot 14.3 + rbb'(300 ohm) 2.2 -> 14.5 nV/sqrt(Hz); Q1 at 40 uA: 2.3 + 2.2 -> ~3.2 nV/sqrt(Hz); total en = 14.8 nV/sqrt(Hz); with Cin ~10 pF (5 pF photodiode + 2.5 pF Q4 Ccb + 2.5 pF wiring) -> in = en*omega*Cin = 90 fA/sqrt(Hz) at 100 kHz. | in_total = sqrt(in_shot^2 + in_R^2); in(enC) = en*2*pi*f*Cin | Ib, R, en, Cin, f | measured noise at these low currents ~25 % above simple theory | calc | p.499 §8.5.7E | high |
| AOE-2490 | control-loop | Transimpedance-amplifier stability: the open-loop unity-gain frequency must exceed the square of the feedback network rolloff divided by the Rf*Cin rolloff; i.e. the feedback network's -3 dB frequency must be below the geometric mean of fT(open-loop) and f(Rf*Cin). | fT(open-loop) > f(Rf*Cc)^2 / f(Rf*Cin) (eq 8.33); example: (106 kHz)^2/1.6 kHz = 7 MHz required; estimated open-loop: G = gm*Rload (Rload ~1.3 Mohm) = 1000-3000 for Q1 Ic 20-60 uA (battery 6-9 V), pole at 25 kHz (Ccb ~2.5 pF each Q2, Q3) -> unity gain ~25 MHz: good margin | Rf, Cc, Cin, open-loop gain and pole | shunt input capacitance + large Rf add lagging phase; details §4x.3, §8.11 | calc | p.499 §8.5.7 eq 8.33 | high |
| AOE-2491 | components | Integrate TIA noise over the passband: resistor/base current noise (65 fA/sqrt(Hz), flat) and en*omega*Cin noise (90 fA/sqrt(Hz), rising with f) contribute comparably -> ~30 pA rms input (0.3 mV rms at output with 10M). Biggest share: the 10M gain/bias resistors; raise them, but the photodiode bias resistor must not let the diode saturate under night ambient light (alternative: quiet servoed bias current source). | in_rms = sqrt(integral(in^2(f)) df over passband) | in(f), BW = 100 kHz | photodiode TIA | calc | p.499 §8.5.7 Optimization | high |
| AOE-2492 | components | Replacing the BJT input follower with a low-capacitance JFET cuts transistor noise contribution ~3x (en ~5 nV/sqrt(Hz), negligible in); worthwhile only if resistor Johnson contributions are cut at least 4x; stop optimizing when amplifier noise reaches the photodiode leakage shot noise. | 2N5484 (Cin ~2.2 pF) at 50 uA: en ~5 nV/sqrt(Hz) (from 100 uA data); total ~10 pA rms, comparable to shot noise of 3 nA leakage (typ, -10 V, 25 degC); +5-10 cents | en, Cin, leakage | discrete photodiode preamp | calc | p.500 §8.5.7 Fig. 8.37 | high |
| AOE-2493 | components | Low-noise BJT selection practice: datasheets rarely give en; use measured en, rbb', VA tables. Design with the manufacturer's minimum beta (not typical) unless you screen parts; check beta at your (often low) current because beta falls at microamp currents (recombination) and small-die parts lose beta above ~1 mA; high-current beta also falls at low Vce. | Table 8.1 "Linearity" 1 (large beta falloff at uA) to 5 (flat); measurements at Vce = 5 V, 1 uA-50 mA | beta(Ic, Vce), die size | many classic low-noise through-hole parts discontinued | review | p.500 §8.5.8 Fig. 8.39 | high |
| AOE-2494 | components | Higher-beta grades of the same BJT (binned A/B/C) tend to have worse noise, lower VCEO and lower Early voltage, and are often unavailable at distributors; do not default to the top grade. | BC850 A/B/C typ beta @ 2 mA: 180/290/520; 2N5089 (beta min 450, 25 V, ~10 % higher en) vs 2N5088 (300, 30 V); ZXTN19020 D (beta 300) vs C (200): +10 % en; 2SB1197K Q (beta min 120) VA = 110 V vs R (180) VA = 70 V | grade, beta, VA, en | small-signal BJT selection | review | p.502 §8.5.8 footnote 42 | high |
| AOE-2495 | components | Early voltage sets BJT output conductance and the maximum single-stage gain (infinite load). | goe = 1/ro = Ic/(VA + VCE); Gmax = gm/goe ~ VA/VT (VCE << VA) | Ic, VA, VCE | gain stages with active loads | calc | p.502 §8.5.8 | high |
| AOE-2496 | components | Check beta at the actual (low) collector current: some parts hold beta, others collapse (pnp parts commonly). | BC850 beta drops < 10 % at 1 uA; 2N5962 drops 50 %; 2N3904 steady; 2N3906 falls 3x over four decades; pnp BC860 and ZTX718 drop < 20 % at 1 uA; 2N5087 flat (printed "nA", OCR; plot spans 1 uA-50 mA) | beta(Ic) | Vce = 5 V measurements (Fig. 8.39); in = sqrt(2*q*Ic/beta) at chosen Ic | measure | p.502 §8.5.8 Fig. 8.39 | medium |
| AOE-2497 | components | The pnp member of a complementary pair usually has a dramatically lower Early voltage, which cuts the gain of pnp current-mirror loads on npn differential pairs; fix with emitter degeneration in the mirror, a Wilson mirror, a cascode, or pick relatively high-VA parts. | BC860C VA = 30 V vs BC850C 220 V | VA (pnp), topology | differential stages with mirror loads | review | p.502 §8.5.8 | high |
| AOE-2498 | components | High-voltage "video-output" transistors often combine very low en with low capacitance; high-voltage parts generally have lower beta; superbeta parts trade VCEO for beta. | 2SC3601: en = 0.22 nV/sqrt(Hz), rbb' 1.7 ohm, Cob = 2 pF; MPSA42 (300 V) lowest gain; IT124 VCEO 2 V; LS301 (18 V) beta = 3000 at 1 uA | VCEO, beta, en, Cob | low-noise candidate search | review | p.503 §8.5.8 footnotes 43-44 | high |
| AOE-2499 | components | Watch base-current noise when running a low-en BJT at the high Ic needed for its best en: in*Zs grows linearly with source impedance. ZTX851 at Ic = 10 mA (beta ~220, Ib = 45 uA): in = 3.8 pA/sqrt(Hz) -> +0.19 nV/sqrt(Hz) across 50 ohm (adds 3 dB), 2.3 nV/sqrt(Hz) across 600 ohm. At 1 mA: en x2.3 (0.48 nV/sqrt(Hz)), in /3.2 (1.2 pA/sqrt(Hz)) -> 0.72 nV/sqrt(Hz) at 600 ohm, total 0.86 nV/sqrt(Hz), below the 3.1 nV/sqrt(Hz) Johnson noise of 600 ohm. | ea = sqrt(en^2 + (sqrt(2*q*Ic/beta)*Rs)^2); en = sqrt(4kT*(rbb' + 0.5*VT/Ic)) | Ic, beta, rbb', Rs | ZTX851 en at 10 mA ~0.2 nV/sqrt(Hz) (Table 8.1a note f: 0.21) | calc | p.503 §8.5.8 | high |
| AOE-2500 | components | Source Johnson noise grows as sqrt(Rs) but in*Zs grows linearly with Zs, so current noise always wins at high enough impedance; a reactive (non-resistive) high-impedance source has little or no Johnson noise to hide behind. | in*Zs vs sqrt(4kT*Rs) | Zs, in | high-Z and capacitive sources | calc | p.503 §8.5.8 footnote 45 | high |
| AOE-2501 | test | Parts not specified for noise can vary widely batch to batch; measure or screen low-frequency noise. | MJE15028-33 (8 A power BJTs, low rbb'): > 10 dB variation in LF noise within one batch | lot samples | audio power-transistor-as-low-noise-front-end practice | measure | p.503 §8.5.8 Fig. 8.40 | high |
| AOE-2502 | components | Pick the BJT by source resistance: low rbb' at high Ic for low Rs; high beta at low Ic for high Rs. ZTX851 beats 2N5962 for Rs < 1k at Ic >= 100 uA; 2N5962 beats ZTX851 for Rs > 100k at almost any Ic; 0.5 nV/sqrt(Hz)-class en helps only if the resistive source is below ~10 ohm; at Ic = 10 uA all candidates are comparable for Rs < 10k and high-beta parts shine above 1 Mohm. | crossover Rs from Fig. 8.33/8.41 charts | Rs, Ic, rbb', beta | spreadsheet from measured beta and rbb' | calc | p.503-505 §8.5.8 Fig. 8.41 | high |
| AOE-2503 | components | Noise-vs-Rs charts that include source Johnson noise are invalid for reactive high-impedance sources (e.g. capacitive sensors): there the en and in*Zs traces meet directly with no intervening Rs region. | use ea = sqrt(en^2 + (in*abs(Zs))^2) without 4kTRs for reactive Zs | Zs type | capacitive sensors | calc | p.505 §8.5.8 | high |
| AOE-2504 | components | Low-rbb' (large-die) BJTs have dramatically higher capacitance, which degrades performance with high-impedance inputs; table capacitances are Cob (sometimes Ccb) at VCE = 10 V, with Cob = Ccb + Cce and Cce usually well under 25 %. | Cob = Ccb + Cce; Cce < 25 % of Cob (usual) | Cob, source Z | high-Z sources at low Ic | review | p.505 §8.5.8 footnote 47 | high |
| AOE-2505 | components | "Optimum" (maximum practical) BJT current: throttle Ic down until the re term raises en 50 % above the rbb'-only Johnson value; this bounds Ic from above and en from below, and gives Rn = en/in at that current (in from minimum beta). | en(o) = 1.5*sqrt(4kT*rbb') => re ~ 2.5*rbb', Ic(o) ~ VT/(2.5*rbb') (derived; Table 8.1a values fit within ~10 %) | rbb', beta_min | if Rn(o) << Rs (bottom quarter of Table 8.1a: Rn = 30-125 ohm; next quartile rarely > 400 ohm) the in*Zs term dominates for signal impedances above 100-400 ohm -> seek higher beta, lower Ic, or a JFET | calc | p.505 §8.5.8 Table 8.1a note (o) | medium |
| AOE-2506 | components | Figure of merit for low-noise BJTs: FOM = Rn/rbb' (want both high Rn and low rbb'); BJTs with high beta at low current tend to have high rbb'. | Table 8.1a FOM 20-70; MMBT6429 = 69 (best), then 2N5089, BC850C; 2SD786, 2SB737 = 51; > 50: ZTX718, DSS2020, ZXTN19020, 2SB1690, 2SD2653, 2N5088, 2N5089 | Rn, rbb' | ranking candidates | calc | p.505 §8.5.8 footnote 48 | high |
| AOE-2507 | requirements | Ultra-low-level source requirement example: moving-coil cartridge ~1 mV at max groove modulation -> 0.1 uV floor for 80 dB dynamic range; ribbon mic gives 50-100 uV at 1 Pa (94 dB SPL) and only 5-10 nV rms for quiet sounds 80 dB lower. Lowest-en op-amp (LT1028, 0.85 nV/sqrt(Hz)) integrates ~100 nV rms over 20 Hz-20 kHz, 20-25 dB too noisy, hence 1:30 step-up transformers; a transformerless preamp needs en < 0.1 nV/sqrt(Hz). | vn = en*sqrt(20 kHz - 20 Hz); target en < 0.1 nV/sqrt(Hz) | source level, dynamic range, bandwidth | audio 20 Hz-20 kHz | calc | p.505-506 §8.5.9 | high |
| AOE-2508 | components | Translate an en target into device requirements: en = 0.1 nV/sqrt(Hz) equals the Johnson noise of a 0.6 ohm resistor, so the (effective) rbb' must be well below 0.6 ohm and Ic must reach ~50 mA (re term then equals the Johnson noise of 0.25 ohm). Acceptable only because the ribbon source is < 1 ohm across the audio band (tolerates low input impedance and high input current). | R_equiv = en^2/(4kT); re/2 = VT/(2*Ic) | en target, Rs | ribbon-mic preamp (§8.5.9) | calc | p.506 §8.5.9A | high |
| AOE-2509 | components | Ultra-low-noise microvolt preamp without feedback: a class-A stage of moderate gain (G ~ 100) at microvolt levels is inherently linear. Single-ended CE is 3 dB quieter than differential but needs a huge input blocking capacitor (~150,000 uF to keep sub-ohm source impedance down to a few Hz; long startup settling); the dc-coupled differential version must double the paralleled transistors per side (halve rbb' and re) to recover the doubled noise power. Parallel devices until effective rbb' <= ~0.1 ohm. A feedback (Naim-type) design would need Re of a fraction of an ohm. | differential noise power x2 -> 2x devices per side; effective rbb' ~ 0.1 ohm or less | topology, N devices | Fig. 8.42 | review | p.506-507 §8.5.9A Fig. 8.42 | high |
| AOE-2510 | components | Small-geometry "low-noise" BJTs are optimized for low Ic where re noise dominates and rbb' barely matters; for high-Ic/low-en work use large-geometry (power) transistors, some of which have rbb' < 10 ohm and good en at ~10 mA (re term = Johnson of 1.25 ohm). | 2SC3324 min NF near Ic = 30 uA (re = 830 ohm; rbb' insignificant below ~200 ohm; measured ~40 ohm); 2SD786 rbb' 4 ohm typ spec (2.3 measured), 2SB737 2 ohm spec (1.2 measured) | Ic regime, rbb' | ZTX851 (5 A, 60 V, no low-noise endorsement) performs on a par with 2SD786 | measure | p.507 §8.5.9B | high |
| AOE-2511 | components | Run plenty of current for really low en, but not beyond the sweet spot where base-current flicker noise raises low-frequency noise; the quietest pnp parts measured slightly better than their npn complements. | ZTX851 sweet spot Ic ~ 5 mA; ZTX951 en = 0.20 vs ZTX851 0.21 nV/sqrt(Hz) at Ic = 10 mA (average of six; rbb' 1.2 and 1.5 ohm) | Ic, polarity | measured down to 4 Hz (Fig. 8.43) | measure | p.507-508 §8.5.9B-C Fig. 8.43 | high |
| AOE-2512 | components | Paralleled BJTs normally need emitter-ballast resistors dropping ~50 mV to prevent current hogging; that is too noisy in ultra-low-noise stages (5 ohm at 10 mA per device), but same-batch ZTX851/ZTX951 (and BD437) Vbe matched well enough to omit ballast. JFETs are NOT matched this way (2N5457-59 Idss spread). | ballast R = 50 mV / Ic_per_device; omit only after measuring the batch Vbe histogram | Vbe spread, Ic per device | Fig. 8.44 (batch of 100 each); Fig. 3.17 JFETs | measure | p.508 §8.5.9C Fig. 8.44 | high |
| AOE-2513 | power | Open-loop low-noise stages with resistive collector loads and emitter pulldowns pass supply-rail noise unattenuated to the output: supply them from quiet rails (e.g. a capacitance multiplier, §8.15). | rail noise appears 1:1 at output | rail noise density | class-A open-loop preamps | measure | p.508 §8.5.9C | high |
| AOE-2514 | components | Measured transformerless ribbon-preamp results (differential, paralleled arrays per side, followed by G = 30 LT1128 stage with < 1 nV/sqrt(Hz) down to 10 Hz): en well below 0.1 nV/sqrt(Hz) is achievable with 32 x 2 ZTX951 at 2 x 200 mA. | ZTX951 2x16 @ 2x100 mA: 0.085 (1 kHz) / 0.10 (100 Hz); ZTX951 2x32 @ 2x200 mA: 0.070 / 0.09; BD437 2x24 @ 2x100 mA: 0.095 / 0.17; BD437 2x24 @ 2x200 mA: 0.080 / 0.13; 2SC3601E 2x12 @ 2x200 mA: 0.093 / 0.18 (nV/sqrt(Hz)) | device, N, Ic | source < 1 ohm; grounded-emitter stages at 100 mA; for low input current use JFETs | measure | p.508 §8.5.9C Fig. 8.45 | high |
| AOE-2515 | test | Measuring discrete-transistor en and in: DUT as a grounded-emitter amplifier with settable Ic and VCE and known voltage gain; measure the output noise spectrum with the input bypassed (-> en), then with a series resistor at the input (-> in). | en = vn_out/Gv (input shorted); in from excess with series R | Ic, VCE, Gv | details §8.12.2; commercial HP/Agilent 4470A measured en, in, NF at 11 spot frequencies (two per decade, 10 Hz-1 MHz) | measure | p.508 §8.5.9D | high |
| AOE-2516 | components | JFET voltage noise is essentially Johnson noise of the channel: equal to the Johnson noise of a resistor 2/(3*gm) (BJT analog: 1/(2*gm)). This is a reliable lower bound; measured parts are often 10-30 % worse, some up to 2x. Get gm as high as possible for low en (parallel or larger-die JFETs) but watch capacitance, leakage and 1/f plateaus. | en^2 ~ 4kT*(2/(3*gm)) V^2/Hz | gm (S) | white region well above 1/f corner; >100 measurements, 65 transistors, 50 types, 75 uA-50 mA | calc | p.509 §8.6.1 eq 8.34, Fig. 8.46, footnote 56 | medium |
| AOE-2517 | components | JFET gm rises as sqrt(Id), so en falls only as Id^(-1/4); do not run so hot that other properties degrade (gm falls with heating, offset drift and CMRR worsen, gate leakage rises dramatically and adds flicker to en). At low current density the dependence approaches the BJT-like square root. | en ~ Id^(-1/4) at high current density (above ~Idss/100); IF3601 en rose 2.8x (~sqrt(10)) when Id cut 1 mA -> 0.1 mA | Id, Idss | JFET operating-point choice | calc | p.509-510 §8.6.1 footnote 57 | high |
| AOE-2518 | components | Paralleling JFETs: two JFETs at the same total Id give gm x sqrt(2), en x 2^(-1/4) = 84 %; at twice the Id gm doubles. All capacitances scale with N, so stop paralleling when the input capacitance roughly matches the source capacitance. For HF use high gm and low Crss (figures of merit gm/Crss, gm/Ciss; fT = gm/(2*pi*C)); a cascode removes Miller multiplication of Crss. | large-geometry JFETs: IFN146 0.7 nV/sqrt(Hz), IF3601 0.3 nV/sqrt(Hz) | N, gm, Ciss, Crss, Csource | low-noise JFET front ends | calc | p.510 §8.6.1 footnote 58 | high |
| AOE-2519 | components | Most JFETs show rising low-frequency en; some have a single 1/f corner (use eqs 8.27/8.28), others an elevated "roller-coaster" plateau from carrier trapping (can appear as popcorn/burst noise in time domain). | 1 kHz en: 2SK170B 1 nV/sqrt(Hz); 2N5457 (low C) 2.8; PN4117 (at Idss = 75 uA) 11; LSK389 1.8 nV/sqrt(Hz) at >= 1 kHz rising to 2x near 100 Hz, wideband ~70 nV rms (1 kHz BW) and 180 nV rms (10 kHz BW) | f band | Fig. 8.47, 8.52 | measure | p.510 §8.6.1A footnote 59 | high |
| AOE-2520 | components | The same JFET part number from different manufacturers can differ enormously in LF noise: qualify the source, or measure. | 2N5486 Fairchild en = 3 nV/sqrt(Hz) vs "manufacturer D" 4.5 nV/sqrt(Hz) at 10 kHz rising to 50 nV/sqrt(Hz) at 100 Hz (15x; ~1 uV wideband to 1 kHz); noisiest sample: 210 nV/sqrt(Hz) plateau, ~2.5 uV rms LF integrated; the 2N5486 datasheet specifies NF only at 100 and 400 MHz | manufacturer, lot | LF (audio/instrumentation) use of RF JFETs | measure | p.510-511 §8.6.1A Fig. 8.51 | high |
| AOE-2521 | requirements | JFET datasheet noise specs often dodge LF noise: none at all; en only at 10 kHz, 100 kHz or higher; rms over a stated bandwidth; deliberately conservative max (e.g. 115 nV/sqrt(Hz), or NF = 3 dB at Rs = 1 Mohm, whose own noise is 126 nV/sqrt(Hz)); typical-only at 100 Hz; or RF-only (>= 100 MHz). Check the spec frequency against your band. | spec frequency must lie in the application band | datasheet noise conditions | JFET selection | review | p.511 §8.6.1A | high |
| AOE-2522 | components | JFET current noise is shot noise of the gate leakage (plus a 1/f component in some FETs); it rises with temperature and rapidly for n-channel parts at high VDG and/or high Id. Leakage specs are unrealistically high (1 nA max) vs typical 1-10 pA at normal ambient. | in = sqrt(2*q*Ig) = (3.2e-19*Ig)^(1/2) A/sqrt(Hz); Ig = 10 pA -> 1.8 fA/sqrt(Hz) -> 1.8 nV/sqrt(Hz) across 1 Mohm (whose Johnson noise is 128 nV/sqrt(Hz)); source must reach 5 Gohm before JFET in*Rs equals source Johnson noise | Ig (A), Rs, T, VDG | low-frequency, low-leakage JFETs; cascode to keep VDS low (§3.2.8) | calc | p.511-512 §8.6.2 eq 8.35, Fig. 8.48, footnote 60 | high |
| AOE-2523 | components | JFET noise rises at moderate/high frequency: (a) at a summing junction en drives Cin -> in = en*omega*Cin; (b) in a non-cascoded common-source stage, Miller feedback through Crss with a phase-shifted (capacitively loaded) output creates a resistive input component that falls with frequency. Both rise linearly with f above breakpoints typically 2-100 kHz for low-leakage JFETs; add a cascode to suppress (b) and improve HF gain. | (a) in = en*2*pi*f*Cin; (b) eq 8.36 R = (1 + omega^2*CL^2*RL^2)/(omega^2*Crss*CL*gm*RL^2) ohm (reconstructed from garbled OCR; bench-confirmed within 2x); 2N5486 (in ~5 fA/sqrt(Hz), en 2.5 nV/sqrt(Hz) at Idss, 10 kHz) with 470 ohm + 5 pF load (68 MHz rolloff): in climbs from ~30 kHz, of concern at 10-100 MHz | en, Cin, Crss, CL, RL, gm, f | can greatly lower the Q of an RF tuned input | calc | p.511-512 §8.6.2 eq 8.36, footnote 61 | medium |
| AOE-2524 | components | FETs give good noise performance for input (source) impedances of 10k to 100M. Reference: SR560 NF <= 1 dB for Rs = 5k-20 Mohm over 100 Hz-10 kHz, corresponding to en = 4 nV/sqrt(Hz) and in = 0.013 pA/sqrt(Hz). | JFET range Rs = 10 k-100 M; Rn = en/in = 4 nV/0.013 pA ~ 300 k | Rs | JFET-input preamps | review | p.512 §8.6.2 Fig. 8.27 | high |
| AOE-2525 | components | Hybrid JFET + op-amp instrumentation amp (three-amp INA with discrete JFET pairs): G = 1 + 2Rf/Rg = 100; +/-2 % trim of Ra maximizes CMRR; JFET offset needs a drain balance trim (Q1 20 mV worst case). Noise: LSK389B 0.9 nV/sqrt(Hz) typ at 1 kHz, Id = 2 mA (slightly better at 5 mA) x sqrt(2) per differential pair x sqrt(2) for upper+lower pairs; op-amp en ~12 nV/sqrt(Hz) divided by JFET-pair gain ~20 -> ~2 nV/sqrt(Hz) overall with ~20 MHz BW (Rs < 1k). Paralleling a second JFET pair per input (own 10 mA sink, drain resistors 500 ohm) cuts en by sqrt(2); add cascodes to keep VDS(Q1) < 5 V for minimum input current. | en_total = sqrt(2)*sqrt(2)*en_JFET (+ en_opamp/20) | JFET en, Id, op-amp en, pair gain | Fig. 8.49; related hybrids Fig. 3.34, 3.37 (en ~2 nV/sqrt(Hz)) | calc | p.512-513 §8.6.3 Fig. 8.49 | high |
| AOE-2526 | components | Servo JFET bias to tolerate loose Idss: sense average drain voltage, compare with a reference (+6.2 V), close the loop with an integrator; loop bandwidth vs frequency sets CMRR (integrator unity gain 230 kHz -> bias loop gain 230 at 1 kHz -> CMRR > 90 dB to 1 kHz, falling 6 dB/octave above). Slew must cover full-bandwidth swings (signal integrator 17 V/us -> 5 Vpp at 1 MHz; bias integrator 10 V/us min -> 3 Vpp CM at 1 MHz). | NPD5564 Idss 5-30 mA vs LSK389B 6-12 mA; noise at Id = 2 mA: NPD5564 12 (10 Hz) / 3.8 (1 kHz), LSK389B 2.5 / 0.9 nV/sqrt(Hz) | Idss spread, loop gain(f), slew | SR560: 100 Mohm par 25 pF input, gain x1-x50,000, en < 4 nV/sqrt(Hz), flat +/-0.5 dB to 1 MHz, 0.01 % distortion, 10 Vpp out; JFET pair at 4.6 mA; LT1028 second stage with < 100 ohm source impedance | calc | p.513-515 §8.6.4 Fig. 8.50, footnote 64 | high |
| AOE-2527 | components | Dual JFETs have poor offset (far worse than the poorest op-amps): trim with multi-turn pots (offset and CMRR), expect fussy, non-orthogonal iterative calibration. Keep source/degeneration resistors small: a series pair of 110 ohm resistors adds 2 nV/sqrt(Hz), too much with sub-nV JFETs (cut ~2x). | NPD5564 max offset 5 mV at 2 mA; LSK389B 20 mV at 1 mA; LS840 2 mV typ / 5 mV max; SR560 offset trim range +/-0.13 mA (10-turn), CMRR trims 20-turn | Vos spec, R values | differential JFET stages | calc | p.515 §8.6.4, §8.6.5 | high |
| AOE-2528 | components | Low-level signal switching (ac/dc/gnd, gain, input select): use gold-clad twin-contact relays (stable low resistance without contact wetting current) of the latching type (pulse to switch; no steady coil current, so no coil-noise pickup and no local heating drift). | latching relay, gold-clad bifurcated contacts | switch type | microvolt-level signal paths | inspect | p.515 §8.6.4 Miscellany | high |
| AOE-2529 | components | Low-noise JFETs have high K and therefore high input capacitance; low-capacitance JFETs are noisier: pick by source impedance and frequency. | AOE-2530 | components | JFETs have low "Early voltage": output conductance gos (roughly proportional to gm) can seriously cut stage gain. Use Gmax = gm/gos (maximum common-source gain with a high-impedance drain load; largely independent of Id, varies linearly with drain voltage) when designing; circumvent with a cascode. Some JFETs also lose gm severely at VDS < 2 V, especially at high Id. | Gmax = gm/gos (Table 3.7 lists it); keep VDS >= 2 V | gm, gos, VDS, Id | JFET gain stages | calc | p.517 §8.6.5 footnote 66 | high |
| AOE-2531 | components | BJT vs JFET noise "shootout" (2SD786 vs 2SK170 at 1 kHz, total amplifier noise vs Rs): the JFET wins minimum NF and remains unbeaten at high source impedance; the BJT wins at low source impedance (especially below ~5k) and has more predictable Vbe than a JFET's loosely specified Vgs. | 2SK170: NF(Rn) = 0.0005 dB (Tn ~33 mK), NF < 0.2 dB for Rs = 1k-100M, in ~1 fA/sqrt(Hz) (gate leakage ~3 pA), specified 200 uA-10 mA; 2SD786: en ~0.5 nV/sqrt(Hz) at 1 mA, 1/f corner well below 10 Hz, rbb' 2.3 ohm -> ~0.25 nV/sqrt(Hz) at higher Ic, beta > 200 down to 1 uA, NF 0.2 dB for Rs = 1-10k | Rs, Ic/Id | Fig. 8.53; previous-edition contenders LM394 (1 nV/sqrt(Hz) @ 1 mA) and 2N6483 (4 nV/sqrt(Hz) @ 100 uA) retired | calc | p.517-519 §8.7 Fig. 8.53, footnote 67 | high |
| AOE-2532 | components | Very-low-en JFETs are "muscle-bound": huge capacitance limits HF use; a complementary low-rbb' BJT pair can beat them at moderate source impedances and frequencies. | IF3601: en 0.35 nV/sqrt(Hz) typ even at 30 Hz, Ig 100 pA typ -> in ~6 fA/sqrt(Hz), Rn ~60k, NF 0.001 dB at Rs = 60k; Ciss 650 pF, Crss 80 pF; IFN146: 75 pF / 15 pF with en 0.7 nV/sqrt(Hz); ZTX851/ZTX951 en as low as 0.3 nV/sqrt(Hz); LSK170 almost as good as 2SK170 | Ciss, Crss, en, Rs, f | IF/LS parts bought direct from manufacturer | review | p.519 §8.7 | high |
| AOE-2533 | components | MOSFETs are not a choice for best-in-class low-noise amplifiers below ~1 MHz (much higher en, 1/f knee as high as 10-100 kHz; no small discrete parts); they are fine in RF ICs. Zero-drift (auto-zero/chopper) CMOS op-amps have mediocre white en but no 1/f rise, making them the quietest choice at very low frequencies. | MOSFET 1/f knee 10-100 kHz; zero-drift en typically 25-100 nV/sqrt(Hz), flat to dc; OPA188/OPA2188: 9 nV/sqrt(Hz), noise corner 0.4 Hz (input current noise not vouched for) | f band | Fig. 8.54 (MOSFET vs JFET vs BJT spectra), Fig. 5.54 | review | p.519-520 §8.7.1 footnote 68 | high |
| AOE-2534 | components | Differential-amplifier noise: read en and in from datasheets at each transistor's individual collector current (not the pair total); in at each input equals the single-ended value; en seen at one input with the other grounded is 3 dB larger (x sqrt(2)) than a single transistor. | en_diff = sqrt(2)*en_single; in_diff = in_single (per input) | Ic per device | BJT/JFET differential pairs, op-amp inputs | calc | p.520 §8.8 | high |
| AOE-2535 | components | Feedback amplifier noise: move equivalent sources eA, iA outside the loop, then add the source term. Noninverting: iA^2 = in^2; eA^2 = en^2 + 4kT*R_par + (in*R_par)^2 with R_par = par(R1,R2) and en the differential ("+3 dB") value; follower: R2 = 0 (differential-amp noise only). eA and iA are not fully uncorrelated, so root-sum-square can be off by up to a factor of 1.4. | e^2 = eA^2 + (Rs*iA)^2 V^2/Hz (eq 8.37); eqs 8.38-8.39 | en, in, R1, R2, Rs, T | assumes signal source resistance zero or small vs R_par (else add its Johnson noise) | calc | p.520-521 §8.8 eqs 8.37-8.39, Fig. 8.55 | high |

### Ch.8 §8.9-§8.10: op-amp circuit noise, low-noise op-amp selection, signal transformers (pp. 521-537; lines 30490-33317)
