|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| AOE-2537 | components | Inverting amplifier: refer all noise sources to the signal input; op-amp en is amplified by the noise gain (1+R2/R1) while signal gain is R2/R1, so en counts more heavily than in a noninverting stage of equal gain | e_in^2 = en^2*(1+R1/R2)^2 + 4kT*R1*(1+R1/R2) + (in*R1)^2  [V^2/Hz]; en op-amp voltage noise (V/rtHz), in op-amp current noise (A/rtHz), R1 input resistor, R2 feedback resistor (ohm), k=1.38e-23 J/K, T (K) | en, in, R1, R2, T | Fig. 8.56 inverting amplifier, source resistance zero or small vs R1; derived from the book's en/in/Johnson model because printed eqs. 8.40-8.41 are OCR-garbled; for a follower R2=0 and only the op-amp's own sources remain | calc | p.521 §8.9 Fig. 8.56 eqs. 8.40-8.41 | medium |
| AOE-2538 | components | A series resistor added at the noninverting input to balance bias-current offsets adds its own Johnson noise; shunt it with a capacitor to quiet that noise | e_R = sqrt(4kT*R) (V/rtHz) removed above f = 1/(2*pi*R*C) | R (ohm), C (F) | op-amp stages with bias-current-compensation resistor (Fig. 4.55); example C1 in Fig. 8.78 | inspect | p.521 §8.9 footnote 70 | medium |
| AOE-2539 | components | Current-feedback (CFB) op-amps: noise current at the inverting input is generally much larger than at the noninverting input; do not assume equal in at both inputs | use in(-) for the feedback-node term, in(+) for the source term | datasheet in(+), in(-) (A/rtHz) | CFB op-amps (§4x.6); voltage-feedback op-amps have comparable en and in at both inputs | review | p.521 §8.9 | medium |
| AOE-2540 | components | A low-en BJT op-amp loses its noise advantage when the source resistance exceeds its noise resistance Rn = en/in, because in*Rs then dominates; LT1028 (en = 0.85 nV/rtHz, 1/f corner fc = 3.5 Hz, in ~1 pA/rtHz) loses its advantage for source impedance greater than 1 kohm | Rn = en/in (ohm); prefer the op-amp when Rs <= Rn; LT1028: Rn ~ 1 kohm | en (V/rtHz), in (A/rtHz), Rs (ohm) | BJT-input op-amps; values at 1 kHz; Fig. 8.60 curve A | calc | p.521 §8.9 Fig. 8.60 | high |
| AOE-2541 | components | Lowest en needs a high input-stage current: LT1028 draws 8.5 mA supply per the prose (Tables 5.5 and 8.3 list 7.4 mA typ) with ~1.8 mA in the input stage; an op-amp with total supply current of about 0.1 mA cannot match that en, and its BJT inputs have correspondingly high bias current and noise current | LT1028: Iq = 8.5 mA, input stage ~1.8 mA, en = 0.85 nV/rtHz | Iq (mA), en | trade-off when choosing micropower vs low-noise op-amps; LT1028 cost about $6 in modest quantity | review | p.521 §8.9 footnote 72 | high |
| AOE-2542 | components | "High-voltage" op-amps run from +/-15 V (36-44 V total); "low-voltage" op-amps are limited to about 5-20 V maximum; many high-voltage (especially bipolar) op-amps also work down to 2.2-3 V total, e.g. LT1677 works with 3 V total and swings rail-to-rail in and out | HV: Vs_total up to 36-44 V; LV: Vs_max 5-20 V; LT1677 min 3 V total | Vs_total (V), datasheet supply range | op-amp selection by supply; check the datasheet min supply before ruling out an HV part for a low-voltage design | review | p.525 §8.9.1B Table 8.3 | high |
| AOE-2543 | components | Input noise voltage falls with quiescent current: to minimize en you must run BJTs and JFETs at relatively high quiescent current, and the same trend holds for integrated op-amps; budget supply current accordingly when en is the driving spec | scatterplot en vs Iq (Fig. 8.57); e.g. en ~ 0.85-1 nV/rtHz parts draw 3.9-9 mA; en 185-400 nV/rtHz parts draw ~1 uA (Table 8.3) | en target (nV/rtHz), Iq budget (mA) | all op-amp technologies; graph trend, no closed formula | review | p.525 §8.9.1 Fig. 8.57 Table 8.3 | medium |
| AOE-2544 | components | "Single-supply" op-amp means inputs and outputs can swing to the negative rail, regardless of whether it is run from a single positive supply or from split supplies | Vin_cm and Vout range must include V- | datasheet input/output swing to V- | op-amp selection for ground-referenced signals on a single supply | review | p.525-526 §8.9.1B | high |

| AOE-2575 | power | Supply-rail noise reaches the output attenuated only by PSRR; LF PSRR is roughly the open-loop gain (80-140 dB) but falls as 1/f with frequency and is often much worse on one rail because of the internal compensation capacitor (LT1012: positive-rail PSRR 25 dB worse than negative above 10 Hz; LT6003: negative-rail PSRR < 10 dB at 1 kHz) | Vn_supply_RTI(f) = Vs_noise(f)*10^(-PSRR_rail(f)/20); PSRR(f) ~ PSRR_dc falling 20 dB/decade | supply noise spectrum per rail, PSRR(f) per rail | read both rails' PSRR curves; see §4x.7 | calc | p.533 §8.9.2 | high |
| AOE-2576 | power | Where HF PSRR is inadequate, RC-filter the supply of the sensitive stage or use a capacitance multiplier | filter corner f = 1/(2*pi*R*C) below the noise band of concern | R (ohm), C (F), supply current | low-noise analog stages; §8.15.1 | inspect | p.533 §8.9.2 | medium |
| AOE-2577 | components | Low-noise op-amp selection procedure: first restrict candidates to those meeting accuracy, speed, power dissipation, supply voltage and input/output swing; then choose on noise - low in for high signal impedance, low en for low signal impedance; source Johnson noise sets the floor | vn^2 = 4*k*T*Rsig + en^2 + (in*Rsig)^2 (V^2/Hz) (eq. 8.42) | Rsig (ohm), en, in, T | Fig. 8.58 interpolation at 1 kHz | calc | p.533 §8.9.3 eq. 8.42 | high |
| AOE-2578 | components | In the inverting configuration and at high frequency, the op-amp's en drives a noise current through the input capacitance, which matters with Gohm-scale input impedances; OPA627 has nearly triple the Cin of the somewhat noisier OPA656 | in_C = en*omega*Cin = en*2*pi*f*Cin (A/rtHz) | en (V/rtHz), f (Hz), Cin (F) | TIAs, high-Z inputs (§8.11.3) | calc | p.533 §8.9.3 fn 81, caution (b) | high |
| AOE-2579 | components | For source resistance between 1 Mohm and 10 Gohm even a noisy low-in CMOS op-amp (LMC6482) contributes negligible voltage noise; a good low-noise JFET op-amp (OPA140) nearly combines the best of BJT (low en) and CMOS (low in) | en << sqrt(4kTRs) + in*Rs for Rs = 1e6-1e10 ohm | Rs | Fig. 8.58 at 1 kHz | calc | p.533 §8.9.3 Fig. 8.58 | high |
| AOE-2580 | components | Total-noise-vs-Rs comparisons at 1 kHz understate op-amps with high 1/f corners: re-evaluate at the lowest signal frequency (LMC6482 en is ~170 nV/rtHz at 10 Hz vs 37 nV/rtHz at 1 kHz) | evaluate en_tot at f_min, not 1 kHz | en(f), f_min | Fig. 8.58 caution (a) | calc | p.533 §8.9.3 | high |

| AOE-2582 | components | Discrete-front-end (hybrid) op-amp common-mode range: bias tail current sources for compliance within 2.5 V of the rails and size collector loads to drop only 3 V to get >= +/-10 V CM range (BJT: negative swing to at least -12 V); a JFET cascode front end loses a volt or two, limiting positive CM to ~+10 V; a cascode out of compliance becomes a low-RON switch | tail compliance to within 2.5 V of rail; R_load drop 3 V; CM >= +/-10 V at +/-15 V | rails (V), drops (V) | Figs. 8.66-8.67, +/-15 V supplies | calc | p.534 §8.9.5A fn 83 | high |

| AOE-2584 | components | Do not trim a differential stage's offset by unbalancing its load resistors: that greatly compromises CMRR and the rejection of tail current-source noise (at least x50 with 1% load resistors); balance the currents seen at the load resistors instead (Fig. 8.80) | CS-noise attenuation >= 50 with 1% matched loads | load tolerance (%) | discrete differential front ends | review | p.534 §8.9.5A | high |
| AOE-2585 | control-loop | Hybrid/composite op-amp stability: the discrete front end adds voltage gain (~x120 BJT, ~x12 JFET) on top of the op-amp's ~160 dB LF loop gain; with a unity-gain-stable second stage (OPA277) run the hybrid at closed-loop gain >= x100 (BJT) or >= x10 (JFET); for lower gain add a series R+C across the op-amp inputs to cut HF open-loop gain | Gcl_min ~ front-end gain (100 BJT, 10 JFET) | Gcl, front-end gain | OPA277 phase margin ~60 deg to past its 1 MHz GBW | sim | p.534-535 §8.9.5A fn 84 | high |

| AOE-2587 | components | A very quiet amplifier must see a low source/feedback impedance to keep its en: the BJT hybrid implies a resistor around 10 ohm in the lower leg of the feedback divider | R_fb_lower ~ 10 ohm (its Johnson noise sqrt(4kT*10 ohm) ~ 0.4 nV/rtHz at 300 K, derived) | Rs, R_fb | sub-nV/rtHz amplifiers | calc | p.535 §8.9.5B | high |

| AOE-2596 | magnetics | Benchmark for a well-engineered audio signal transformer (JT-115K-E): +/-0.15 dB over 20 Hz-20 kHz, -3 dB at 2.5 Hz and 90 kHz, distortion < 0.1% even at 20 Hz (0.001% at 1 kHz), common-mode rejection 110 dB at 60 Hz | limits as stated | response, THD, CMR | acceptance criteria for transformer selection | measure | p.536 §8.10 fn 88 | high |
| AOE-2597 | magnetics | For source resistances of 0.8-10 ohm use a high-ratio, very-low-winding-resistance transformer (Signal Recovery 1900: 1:100 and 1:1000, 0.04 ohm) for minimum NF ~0.5 dB, accepting less bandwidth than audio types | NF_min ~0.5 dB for Rs = 0.8-10 ohm | Rs, R_winding | sub-10-ohm sources | review | p.536 §8.10 | high |

### Ch.8 §8.11-§8.16 + Review: transimpedance amplifiers, noise measurement, supply noise, interference/shielding/grounding (pp. 537-593; lines 33318-35540)
| id | domain | rule statement | formula / limit (units) | inputs | applicability & conditions | verify by | source | conf |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| AOE-2605 | control-loop | Transimpedance amplifier (TIA) ideal transfer: output voltage is minus input current times feedback resistor; gain has units of ohms | Vout = -Isig*Rf (V; Isig in A, Rf in ohm) | Isig, Rf | inverting op-amp with feedback resistor Rf, ideal components; inputs such as photodiode, photomultiplier, particle detector, STM tip, patch clamp | calc | p.537 §8.11 Fig. 8.69 | high |
| AOE-2606 | control-loop | Input capacitance with large Rf adds lagging phase in the feedback path and makes a bare TIA unstable; cure with small Cf across Rf. Usable TIA bandwidth is roughly the geometric mean of op-amp GBW (fT) and the input RC rolloff | fc = sqrt(GBW*fRC) (Hz), i.e. GBW = fc^2/fRC; fRC = 1/(2*pi*Rf*Cin) (Hz) | GBW (Hz), Rf (ohm), Cin (F, total at inverting input: sensor + cable + op-amp) | op-amp TIA compensated with Cf; needs a surprisingly fast op-amp for modest bandwidth | calc | p.538 §8.11.1 eq.(8.43) | high |
| AOE-2607 | components | TIA op-amp voltage noise flowing in Rf appears as input noise current en/Rf (use par(Rf,Rs) when the source has finite resistance Rs); usually small vs Rf Johnson current but 1/f can raise en by 10x-50x over its white value at low frequency | in_en = en/par(Rf,Rs) (A/rtHz) | en (V/rtHz) at the frequency of interest, Rf, Rs (ohm) | low-frequency TIA noise budget | calc | p.538 §8.11.2 | high |
| AOE-2608 | components | Amplifier input current noise in adds directly (root-sum-square) to TIA input noise current; check its 1/f rise and temperature rise, not only the 25C mid-band figure: AD743 in 7 fA/rtHz mid-band rises to 100 fA/rtHz at 1 Hz (equivalent to shot noise of 30 nA), to 40 fA/rtHz at 80C (Ib 5 nA) and 400 fA/rtHz at 125C | in_total = sqrt(in_amp^2 + in_R^2 + in_en^2 + in_enC^2) (A/rtHz) | in(f,T), Ib(T) from datasheet plots | FET/CMOS-input TIAs; datasheets often specify in at 25C only | calc | p.538 §8.11.2 | high |
| AOE-2609 | components | At mid frequencies an op-amp's input noise current is the shot noise of its dc input (bias or leakage) current; estimate worst-case in from the Ib-vs-temperature curve at maximum operating temperature (AD743: 0.15 nA -> ~7 fA/rtHz; 5 nA at 80C -> 40 fA/rtHz) | in = sqrt(2*q*Ib) (A/rtHz; q = 1.602e-19 C, Ib in A) | Ib at T_max | FET and BJT input stages, mid-band | calc | p.538 §8.11.2 | medium |
| AOE-2610 | derating | Keep JFET drain-source voltage to no more than a few volts (e.g. cascode the drains) to avoid impact-ionization gate current and its noise; low-noise JFET op-amps show rising input current as the input nears one rail (positive rail: LT1792, ADA4627; negative rail: AD8610) | VDS <= a few V; keep VCM away from the affected rail | VDS, VCM | discrete JFET front ends; JFET op-amps near rails | review | p.538 §8.11.2 (see §3.2.8) | low |
| AOE-2611 | components | Use the low-frequency en, not the white en, in low-frequency noise budgets: AD743 2.9 nV/rtHz @10 kHz rises to 23 nV/rtHz @1 Hz; OPA627 to 33 and ADA4627 to 42 nV/rtHz @1 Hz; OPA656 to 75 nV/rtHz @10 Hz; AD8610/AD8620 from 6 to ~200 nV/rtHz; CMOS op-amps worse | en_LF from datasheet en-vs-f curve | en(f) | JFET/CMOS op-amps below the 1/f corner | review | p.538 §8.11.2 fn.92 | high |
| AOE-2612 | components | enC noise: the op-amp's voltage noise impressed across the total capacitance at the inverting input creates an input noise current rising proportional to frequency; often the dominant TIA noise at high frequency | in_enC = en*omega*Cin = 2*pi*en*Cin*f (A/rtHz) | en (V/rtHz), Cin (F), f (Hz) | TIA or any feedback amplifier whose summing node sees capacitance to ground | calc | p.539 §8.11.3 eq.(8.44) Fig. 8.70 | high |
| AOE-2613 | components | Feedback-resistor Johnson noise current sets the TIA low-frequency floor and favors large Rf; 1 Mohm at 25C gives 0.128 pA/rtHz, at least 100x a FET op-amp's in (which can then be ignored) | inR = sqrt(4*k*T/Rf) (A/rtHz; k = 1.38e-23 J/K, T in K) | Rf, T | TIA noise floor | calc | p.539 §8.11.4 | high |
| AOE-2614 | components | Crossover frequency above which enC noise exceeds the Rf Johnson-noise current | fx = sqrt(4*k*T/Rf)/(2*pi*en*Cin) (Hz); for Rf = 1 Mohm: fx(Hz) = 2e7/(en*Cin) with en in nV/rtHz and Cin in pF | Rf, en, Cin, T | TIA; compare fx with the required signal bandwidth | calc | p.539 §8.11.4 eq.(8.45) fn.94 | high |
| AOE-2615 | control-loop | Choose Cf so the Rf-Cf corner fc equals the geometric-mean frequency fGM: maximum bandwidth with only modest peaking (damping ratio 0.7); above fc the enC noise current falls at -6 dB/octave | fc = 1/(2*pi*Rf*Cf) = fGM = sqrt(fRC*fT); so Cf = 1/(2*pi*Rf*sqrt(fRC*fT)) (F) | Rf, Cin, fT | TIA compensation (stability criterion of §4x.3); otherwise a flat noise region results | calc | p.539 §8.11.4 eq.(8.46) Fig. 8.72 | high |
| AOE-2616 | components | Typical small-area high-speed photodiode TIA values for first-pass budgets: Cin ~10-20 pF (more through shielded cable), Rf ~1-10 Mohm, FET op-amp en ~3-10 nV/rtHz and in ~1-10 fA/rtHz | Cin 10-20 pF; Rf 1-10 Mohm; en 3-10 nV/rtHz; in 1-10 fA/rtHz | - | default assumptions before real data | review | p.539 §8.11.4 | high |
| AOE-2617 | components | For an enC-dominated TIA pick the op-amp by low en and low input capacitance (external + amplifier); a faster op-amp by itself does not lower input-referred noise current, it only extends bandwidth (fast parts help only because they tend to have less input capacitance) | figure of merit en*(Cext+Camp) (smaller is better) | en, Camp, Cext | wideband TIA followed by output lowpass filtering | calc | p.539 §8.11.4 Fig. 8.72 | medium |
| AOE-2618 | control-loop | Size Cf for stability at the largest anticipated input capacitance: OPA656 (fT 230 MHz, en 7 nV/rtHz) with Rf 1 Mohm and conservative Cf 2 pF (fc 76 kHz) keeps ~75 kHz bandwidth and stability for Cin 10 pF to 1000 pF; Cin 10 nF substantially cuts bandwidth (stable only because of the part's limited 65 dB open-loop gain) | Cf >= Cf_required(Cin_max) | Cin_max, fT, Rf | TIA with uncertain or variable source capacitance | sim | p.540 §8.11.5 Fig. 8.73 | high |
| AOE-2619 | components | Lower bound on TIA feedback resistance: Rf Johnson current must stay below the signal's shot noise; they are equal when Iin*Rf = 2kT/q = 50 mV, so choose Rf such that the voltage drop from the input current (including any dc component) is not less than about 100 mV | Iin*Rf >= 100 mV (equality point 2*k*T/q = 50 mV at room temperature) | Iin (A, dc + signal), Rf (ohm) | shot-noise-limited current inputs (photodiodes) | calc | p.541 §8.11.6 | high |
| AOE-2620 | control-loop | In an enC-dominated fast TIA, reduce Rf to gain bandwidth: Rf 1M -> 100k raises the input rolloff 10x and the usable bandwidth by sqrt(10) (~3x); recover the 10x lost gain with a downstream voltage amplifier (low-frequency floor rises but stays below the enC contribution) | BW proportional to 1/sqrt(Rf) (fixed Cin, fT) | Rf, Cin, fT | wideband photodiode TIA | calc | p.542 §8.11.6 Fig. 8.75 | high |

| AOE-2631 | components | Noise of a degenerated current-mirror load referred to the JFET pair input | en_mirror = sqrt(4*k*T/RE)/gm (V/rtHz); 100 ohm emitter resistors with gm 130 mS -> 0.1 nV/rtHz (a 4% contribution in quadrature) | RE (ohm), gm (S) | mirror-loaded differential input stage | calc | p.546 §8.11.8A | high |
| AOE-2632 | power | A balanced differential pair rejects its tail-current-sink noise (e.g. ~30x for 3% balance); when the pair is deliberately unbalanced for offset trim, feed the current sink from a low-noise regulator (< 10 nV/rtHz) with extra RC filtering | rejection ~ 1/(fractional imbalance) | imbalance | differential input stages | review | p.546 §8.11.8A | medium |
| AOE-2633 | power | Derive quiet split rails for an ultra-low-noise front end from a buried-zener reference (LT1027, low 1/f) with a bypass capacitor on its noise-reduction pin, buffered by op-amps (a resistor to ground from the upper op-amp's inverting input sets other voltages, e.g. +-6 V) | - | - | +-5 V low-noise supplies for preamps | review | p.546 §8.11.8A fn.101 Fig. 8.80D | medium |
| AOE-2634 | components | Photodiode operating mode: for lowest light levels use zero bias (photovoltaic), where NEP comes from diffusion-current dark resistance biased by the op-amp offset (datasheets assume 10 mV bias; a 100 uV-offset op-amp gave dark NEP ~4 orders of magnitude better than specified); reverse bias (photoconductive) lowers capacitance and improves speed, linearity and long-wavelength response at the cost of dark-current NEP (optical NEP typically in the fW/rtHz range) | Vos_opamp << 10 mV for photovoltaic mode | Vos, bias mode | photodiode front ends | review | p.537 §8.11 fn.91 | medium |
| AOE-2635 | components | Include cable capacitance in TIA input capacitance: shielded cable adds ~30 pF/ft; large-area photodiodes reach 1000 pF or more | Cin_total = C_detector + 30 pF/ft * L_cable (ft) + C_amp | C_detector, L_cable, C_amp | TIA / charge amplifiers fed by coax (see App. H) | calc | p.547 §8.11.9 | high |
| AOE-2636 | components | Bootstrap the return side of a capacitive detector (and its cable shield) with a low-noise JFET follower of the summing-junction signal: gm ~25 mS gives ~40 ohm output impedance and at least 10x reduction of effective input capacitance; BF862 gate current ~1 pA if VDS < 5 V, feedback capacitance ~2 pF | Zout = 1/gm (~40 ohm at 25 mS); Cin_eff reduced >= 10x | gm, VDS | photodiode TIAs with large detector capacitance | calc | p.547 §8.11.9 Fig. 8.82 | high |
| AOE-2637 | components | In a bootstrapped TIA the follower JFET's en acts on the full (unbootstrapped) detector+cable capacitance while the op-amp's en sees only the reduced capacitance; choose a JFET with en much lower than the op-amp's (BF862 ~0.9 nV/rtHz). Example at 100 kHz, 1000 pF diode: LT1792 4.2 nV/rtHz on ~100 pF -> 0.26 pA/rtHz; JFET 0.9 nV/rtHz on 1000 pF -> 0.57 pA/rtHz; combined 0.63 pA/rtHz vs 2.6 pA/rtHz unbootstrapped | in_enC = 2*pi*f*sqrt((en_amp*Cin_eff)^2 + (en_JFET*C_det)^2) (A/rtHz) | en_amp, en_JFET, Cin_eff, C_det, f | bootstrapped TIA | calc | p.547 §8.11.9 | high |
| AOE-2638 | cost | Bootstrapping lets an inexpensive op-amp replace a premium low-en part: LT1792 $4.85 + BF862 $0.67 vs OPA637 $18 (ADA4637 $12); BF862 ~$0.50 qty 25; BF862s from the same reel can be paralleled without source ballasting (cutoff to Idss in ~400 mV; low tempco near Idss) | - | - | 2015 prices | review | pp.547-548 §8.11.9 fn.103 fn.104 | medium |
| AOE-2639 | control-loop | Reference bootstrapped photodiode amplifier (input capacitance up to 1000 pF): OPA637 (GBW 80 MHz, en 4.5 nV/rtHz), Cf 4 pF, photodiode bias through 10k/0.1 uF, x1/x2/x5/x10 output stage (gain resistor open/3.01k/750/332 ohm), 49.9 ohm series output (to 10 V unterminated, 3 V into 50 ohm); a conservative 10x capacitance reduction improves bandwidth and noise with gain unchanged | - | - | reference design (values read from schematic) | review | p.548 §8.11.9 Fig. 8.83 Fig. 8.84 | medium |
| AOE-2640 | components | A common-base (cascode) input stage isolates detector capacitance (the TIA sees only the cascode's few-pF Ccb), but its emitter input resistance forms a rolloff with Cin; unbiased at 1 uA signal re = 25 kohm, giving 6.4 kHz with 1000 pF; the input node also sits a diode drop below ground | fin = 1/(2*pi*re*Cin); re = 25/Ic(mA) ohm | Ic (mA), Cin (F) | cascode-input TIAs | calc | pp.548-549 §8.11.10A Fig. 8.86A | high |
| AOE-2641 | components | A cascode/common-base transistor needs fT well above the TIA bandwidth at its actual low operating current, because the base diverts about f/fT of the emitter current: 2N5089 fT ~2 MHz at 10 uA diverts ~10% at 200 kHz; MMBT918 fT 13 MHz at 15 uA (~8% at fc) but beta < 40; 2SC4082 fT 20 MHz at 10 uA with flat beta (90 at 100 uA) chosen | diverted fraction ~ f/fT(Ic) | fT vs Ic (datasheet, Fig. 8.85), beta(Ic), f | low-current cascode stages | calc | pp.549-551 §8.11.10 fn.106 Fig. 8.85 | high |
| AOE-2642 | components | Biasing the cascode lowers re and raises the input rolloff (+-15 V, Rc = Re = 60 kohm -> Ic 250 uA, re 100 ohm, 1.6 MHz with 1000 pF) but (a) Rc must be critically chosen so the collector sits at ground or a high-gain TIA (e.g. 1 V/uA) gets large output dc offset, (b) the lower Rc and Re add Johnson current at the summing junction, (c) higher base current adds shot noise | in_R = sqrt(4*k*T/R); in_B = sqrt(2*q*IB) (A/rtHz) | Rc, Re, IB | biased cascode TIA | calc | p.550 §8.11.10A Fig. 8.86B | high |
| AOE-2643 | components | Shot noise applies to base current but not to a current set by voltage across a metallic resistor (charge carriers not independent); base-current shot noise appears at the collector because IC = IE(noiseless) - IB(noisy) | in = sqrt(2*q*IB) for the base current only | IB | BJT stage noise budgets | review | p.550 §8.11.10 fn.107 | high |
| AOE-2644 | control-loop | Cascode-TIA compensation flow: capacitance at the op-amp input = cascode Ccb (2 pF) + op-amp Cin; with Rf 500 kohm (0.5 V/uA) and OPA637 (15 pF, fT 80 MHz): fRfCin = 18.7 kHz, fGM = 1.22 MHz; Cf for fc = 0.7 MHz (conservative damping 1.2) is 0.46 pF, of which the resistor supplies ~0.1 pF; add 0.36 pF with a pair of PCB traces, a 0.5 pF trimmer, or a gimmick (few twists of insulated wire), aiming for ~1 MHz transimpedance bandwidth | fGM = sqrt(fT/(2*pi*Rf*Cnode)); Cf = 1/(2*pi*Rf*fc) | Rf, Cnode, fT | cascode TIA (see §4x.3) | calc | p.550 §8.11.10A Fig. 8.87 | high |
| AOE-2645 | components | At a low-capacitance TIA node (cascode collector) prefer a low-input-capacitance op-amp over a large-area-JFET low-en part: with 4 pF op-amp Cin, fRfCin ~53 kHz and a 1 MHz bandwidth (damping 1, fc 0.7 MHz) needs only fT > 9 MHz (OPA209, fT 18 MHz, $2.27 -> fGM 0.98 MHz); op-amp en need only be well below en(Q1) times (effective input C / C at op-amp node), 7.5 nV/rtHz here | en_amp << en_Q1*(Cin_eff/C_node) | Cnode, fT, en | cascode TIA op-amp selection | calc | p.551 §8.11.10B fn.110 | high |
| AOE-2646 | components | Set the cascode quiescent current so its re*Cin_eff rolloff lies somewhat above the TIA bandwidth: Iq 15 uA -> re 1.7 kohm -> 1.9 MHz with 50 pF (bootstrapped); low Iq allows 1 Mohm collector/emitter resistors whose Johnson current is no larger than Rf's; base shot noise 0.1 pA/rtHz vs 0.26 pA/rtHz combined Rc, Re, Rf | 1/(2*pi*re*Cin_eff) > f_B | Iq, Cin_eff, f_B | bootstrapped cascode TIA (GaP UV photodiode 460 pF at 5 V reverse bias -> ~50 pF bootstrapped) | calc | p.551 §8.11.10B Fig. 8.87 | high |
| AOE-2647 | components | The bootstrap follower must drive the detector low side with output impedance much lower than the cascode input impedance (Zo = 1/gm ~40 ohm vs re 1.7 kohm); add an emitter follower at a few mA to stiffen the guard signal, especially when it drives an external coax shield (AM-radio pickup) | Zo_follower << re | gm, re | bootstrapped/guarded inputs | calc | pp.551-552 §8.11.10B fn.112 | high |
| AOE-2648 | components | The cascode BJT's own input noise voltage generates enC noise through the detector's effective capacitance; choose low rbb' (2SC4082: rbb'Cc ~6 ps with Cob 0.9 pF implies rbb' < 10 ohm) | en = sqrt(4*k*T*(rbb' + 0.5*re)) (V/rtHz) | rbb', re | cascode TIA | calc | p.551 §8.11.10B fn.113 | high |
| AOE-2649 | components | Regulated cascode (RGC): a local-feedback transistor regulating the cascode base divides the input impedance by its voltage gain (raising the Rin*Cin-limited bandwidth) and sets the input noise voltage, letting the cascode run at low current without an en penalty; integrated versions are used in most contemporary fiber-optic receivers | Rin = re/G2 | re, G2 | Gb/s photoreceivers; low-current cascode TIAs | calc | pp.551-552 §8.11.10C Fig. 8.88 | medium |
| AOE-2650 | components | Capacitive-feedback (integrating) TIA removes Rf Johnson noise: Cf-only integrator, output differentiated to recover the current, both reset often enough to prevent saturation (LED optical reset avoids switch leakage); differentiator op-amp en must be well below the integrator op-amp's; with Rf of ~100 Mohm and above the resistor self-capacitance already makes the TIA an integrator, so deliberately raising Cf to ~1 pF is reasonable | reset interval < Vsat*Cf/Iin_max (derived from V = I*t/C) | Cf, Iin_max, Vsat | patch clamp, cooled Ge/Si(Li) x-ray detectors, imaging readout (correlated double sampling) | calc | p.552 §8.11.11 | medium |
| AOE-2651 | components | Capacitive-feedback benchmark: Axon 200B patch clamp claims 0.2 fA/rtHz open-input noise current at 150 Hz (capacitive-feedback mode, thermoelectrically cooled), equivalent to shot noise of 0.1 pA leakage | in = sqrt(2*q*I_leak) | I_leak | benchmark | review | p.552 fn.116 | high |
| AOE-2652 | components | STM preamp requirements: tip current ~1 nA at ~1 V bias and ~1 nm gap (current changes ~10x per 0.1 nm); shielded line to the vacuum flange adds 50-200 pF; preamp is inside the tip-spacing servo loop so needs ~20 kHz bandwidth with careful compensation; Fig. 8.89 reaches ~10 kHz at 0.1 V/nA with a trimmer compensating 100 pF or more (OPA637, 16 pF, 4.5 nV/rtHz); Rf of 1e9 ohm used at very low currents | - | - | STM and similar low-current servo TIAs | review | pp.553-554 §8.11.12 Fig. 8.89 fn.119 | high |
| AOE-2653 | components | Noise on a TIA's noninverting (bias) input adds directly to the op-amp en and is multiplied by the same noise gain: isolate the bias DAC's ground-path noise with a difference amplifier and add RC lowpass sections in the bias path, including one after the bias buffer op-amp | e_bias << en_opamp | e_bias(f) | TIAs applying a bias/tip voltage | review | p.554 §8.11.12 | medium |
| AOE-2654 | assembly | Mount the summing-junction node of very-high-resistance TIAs (both ends of the input resistor, op-amp inverting input) on Teflon standoffs to prevent PCB leakage currents | - | - | Rf ~1e9 ohm, nA-level currents | inspect | p.554 fn.119 | high |
| AOE-2655 | control-loop | Cancel the Cin*dV/dt current pulse produced when a TIA bias voltage is stepped by feeding R5-C5 into the output difference amplifier with R5*C5 = R1*Cin, so the signal current can be read before the bias settles | R5*C5 = R1*Cin | R1, Cin | STM I-V scans; source-measure-unit style TIAs | calc | p.554 §8.11.12 | high |
| AOE-2656 | test | Calibrated nA square-wave source for TIA compensation: a 1 V square wave through 1 Gohm fails because ~0.1 pF shunt capacitance produces spikes (0.01 V/us of slew gives 1 nA); use (A) a tunable series R1C1 lowpass compensating the scaling resistor's Cp with R1*C1 = R2*Cp (0.1 V square wave -> clean 1 nA), optionally via an isolated BNC to suppress ground-path spikes, or (B) a 1 pF series capacitor differentiator: 0.5 Vpp 1 kHz triangle -> +-1 nA square current (needs a clean triangle at turning points) | i = C*dV/dt; R1*C1 = R2*Cp | Cp, C, dV/dt | adjusting TIA input compensation for best step response with the input cable in place | calc | p.555 §8.11.13 Fig. 8.91 | high |
| AOE-2657 | test | Measure amplifier en without a noise source: (1) measure voltage gain Gv at the frequency of interest (signal large enough to override noise, not saturating); (2) short the input and measure rms output es in a known bandwidth B | en = es/(Gv*sqrt(B)) (V/rtHz) | es (V rms), Gv, B (Hz) | high-input-impedance FET/BJT stages at low-moderate frequencies | measure | p.555 §8.12.1 eq.(8.47) | medium |
| AOE-2658 | test | Measure amplifier in: put resistor R across the input (large enough that in*R is significant, small vs amplifier input impedance; or leave open and use the input impedance as R) and measure er | er^2 = (en^2 + 4*k*T*R + (in*R)^2)*B*Gv^2; in = (1/R)*sqrt(er^2/(B*Gv^2) - en^2 - 4*k*T*R) (A/rtHz) | er, R, en, B, Gv, T | as above | measure | pp.555-556 §8.12.1 eq.(8.48)-(8.49) | high |
| AOE-2659 | requirements | SNR of a signal Vs from source resistance Rs amplified by an amplifier with en, in in bandwidth B; bandwidth beyond that needed to pass the signal only lowers SNR (a broadband/noise-like signal's SNR is independent of bandwidth) | SNR(dB) = 10*log10( Vs^2 / ((en^2 + (in*Rs)^2 + 4*k*T*Rs)*B) ) | Vs (V rms), Rs (ohm), en, in, B (Hz), T (K) | signal within B | calc | p.556 §8.12.1 eq.(8.50) | high |
| AOE-2660 | test | Noise-figure measurement with a calibrated noise source: source impedance Rs equal to the intended signal source; note output rms with the source attenuated to zero, then raise the noise source rms Vs until the output rises 3 dB (x1.414); the amplifier's input noise in the measurement bandwidth equals that Vs | NF(dB) = 10*log10( Vs^2/(4*k*T*Rs*B) ); SNR(dB) = 10*log10( vs^2/(4*k*T*Rs*B) ) - NF(Rs) | Vs, Rs, B, T | assumes Rin >> Rs; with matched source (Rs = Zin) omit the factors 4; gives only the combination of en, in for that Rs (repeat at several Rs to infer en and in) | measure | p.556 §8.12.3 eq.(8.51)-(8.52) | medium |
| AOE-2661 | test | Y-factor (hot/cold) noise temperature: switch the amplifier input between matched 50 ohm terminations at 77 K (liquid nitrogen) and room temperature with a high-quality coax relay; measure output noise powers Ph and Pc with an RF power meter at a set center frequency and bandwidth | Y = Ph/Pc; Tn = (Th - Y*Tc)/(Y - 1) (K); NF(dB) = 10*log10(Tn/T + 1) | Th, Tc (K), Ph, Pc (W) | matched-impedance RF amplifiers | measure | p.557 §8.12.3 eq.(8.53)-(8.54) | medium |
| AOE-2662 | test | Inexpensive two-temperature noise calibrator: at low current a BJT's input noise voltage equals the Johnson noise of re/2; base grounded, collector at +5 V, emitter through 10k to -5 V gives a ~50 ohm source at 150 K noise temperature; alternate with a real 50 ohm resistor via a coax relay (not a CMOS switch); a diode such as MBD301 (no high-level injection at 500 uA) is preferable to a BJT | T_noise = T/2 at source impedance re | Ie, re | low-frequency amplifier noise-temperature checks | measure | pp.557-558 §8.12.3 fn.121 | high |
| AOE-2663 | test | Matched-input (50 ohm RF) amplifiers: en and in are irrelevant separately; specify noise figure with matched source, or the input needed for a given SNR (e.g. receiver spec 10 dB SNR with 0.25 uV rms in 2 kHz bandwidth: raise a matched sinewave input until the rms output rises 10 dB, both with 2 kHz bandwidth) | - | - | RF receivers (input level OCR-garbled as '0.25 n V') | measure | p.558 §8.12.3A | medium |
| AOE-2664 | test | Use a true-rms meter whenever noise and signal are measured together | - | - | noise/SNR measurements | inspect | p.558 §8.12.3A | high |
| AOE-2665 | test | Pink noise (equal power per octave, density falling 3 dB/octave) cannot come from a single RC (6 dB/octave); use a parallel set of series RC sections whose characteristic frequencies step by a fixed ratio (x10) with impedances decreasing by the square root of that ratio (sqrt(10)); decade spacing gives +-0.25 dB from ideal -3 dB/octave over 10 Hz-1 MHz (fine-tuned 1% resistors) | f(k+1) = 10*f(k); Z(k+1) = Z(k)/sqrt(10) | - | pink-noise generators, audio equalization | sim | p.559 §8.12.4 Fig. 8.93 Fig. 8.94 | high |
| AOE-2666 | test | PRBS noise source: an n-bit maximal-length shift register cycles 2^n - 1 states before repeating; lowpass well below the clock (50-bit at 10 MHz -> white to ~1 MHz, repeat 3.6 years; 71-bit at 1 MHz -> flat +-0.07 dB to 100 kHz, repeat ~75 million years); use XNOR feedback (not XOR) to make the all-zeros stuck state impossible and guarantee startup | T_repeat = (2^n - 1)/f_clk (s) | n, f_clk | digital pseudorandom noise generators | calc | pp.558-560 §8.12.4A Fig. 8.93 | high |
| AOE-2667 | test | PRBS noise shaping: 'white' = RC lowpass above the band of interest (200 kHz for a 10 Hz-100 kHz source) to remove clock edges; 'red' = RC lowpass below the band (5 Hz) for a 6 dB/octave rolloff | - | - | Fig. 8.93 | review | p.559 §8.12.4A | high |
| AOE-2668 | power | Choose an LDO that is stable with zero-ESR ceramic output capacitors and tolerant of reversed input in battery products (LT1121-5: stable with zero ESR, reverse input to -30 V, overcurrent and overtemperature protection); LP2950 does not tolerate reversed input and LP2950/LM2931 need a minimum output-capacitor ESR | require ESR_min = 0 when all bypass caps are ceramic; V_reverse rating >= battery voltage | regulator datasheet | 9 V battery circuits | review | p.561 §8.12.4A | high |
| AOE-2669 | control-loop | Put a series resistor at an op-amp output that drives cables or capacitive loads: it ensures stability into capacitive loads and provides 50 ohm source termination | R_series = 50 ohm for 50 ohm cable | - | signal/noise source outputs | inspect | p.561 §8.12.4A | medium |
| AOE-2670 | test | Transistor noise test fixture practice: calibrate gain with an input signal, then replace it with a 0 ohm shorting plug; very long RC time constants (capacitance-multiplier supply, blocking networks) suppress excess LF noise (settling takes many seconds); a second pole in the bias loop creates LF bias wander that mimics 1/f noise (C2 raised to 0.15-0.35 F for sub-nV/rtHz LF work); a large base capacitor grounding the base down to 4 Hz lets rbb' be measured from its Johnson noise | - | - | discrete transistor noise characterization | measure | pp.556-557 §8.12.2 Fig. 8.92 fn.120 | high |
| AOE-2671 | filter | Use the equivalent noise bandwidth (ENBW: width of the brick-wall filter passing the same noise) as B in every noise formula; for a single-section RC lowpass it is 1.57x the 3 dB frequency | B = 1.57*f3dB = (pi/2)*f3dB (Hz) | f3dB | band-limiting before rms noise measurement | calc | p.561 §8.13.1 eq.(8.55) Fig. 8.96 | high |
| AOE-2672 | filter | ENBW of common lowpass band limits: two cascaded buffered RC sections 1.22x f3dB; Butterworth 1 pole 1.57x, 2 poles 1.11x, 3 poles 1.05x, 4 poles 1.025x | B = k*f3dB, k = 1.57 / 1.22 / 1.11 / 1.05 / 1.025 | f3dB, filter type/order | lowpass band-limiting of noise | calc | p.561 §8.13.1 | high |
| AOE-2673 | power | LDOs whose loop compensation depends on the output capacitor's ESR zero (e.g. LP2950/LP2951) lose phase margin with ceramic output capacitors (ESR 5-10 mohm, below the stable minimum); the datasheet forbids ceramic > 1000 pF directly from output to ground unless a series resistance of about 0.1-2 ohm is added | ESR_out within datasheet stable range, else add R_series = 0.1-2 ohm | C_out type and ESR, regulator ESR range | ESR-compensated LDOs | review | p.561 fn.124 | high |
| AOE-2674 | filter | RLC bandpass for narrow (high-Q) noise-measurement bands (better than a cascaded RC pair when the passband is narrow vs center frequency): f0 = 1/(2*pi*sqrt(L*C)); parallel RLC (current in, voltage out) Q = 2*pi*f0*R*C and B = 1/(4*R*C); series RLC (voltage in, current out) Q = 2*pi*f0*L/R and B = R/(4*L); an interposed filter (Fig. 8.99) is equivalent to parallel RLC with R = par(R1,R2) | B = pi*f0/(2*Q) (Hz) | L (H), C (F), R (ohm) | narrowband noise measurement | calc | p.562 §8.13.1 Fig. 8.98 Fig. 8.99 | medium |
| AOE-2675 | filter | Averaging a static signal over time T is a lowpass with ENBW 1/(2T) (1 s -> 0.5 Hz) that rolls off only 3 dB/octave (softer than one RC section); a rectangular window of length T on an ac signal (FFT), or lock-in detection over T, gives resolution bandwidth 1/T | B_avg = 1/(2*T); B_window = B_lockin = 1/T (Hz) | T (s) | integrating ADCs/DVMs, FFT windows, synchronous detection | calc | p.562 §8.13.1 fn.125 | high |
| AOE-2676 | test | For spot-noise measurement in a narrow band at a high center frequency (Q = f_in/df > ~1000, e.g. 10 Hz at tens of kHz to MHz) heterodyne the band down with a mixer + LO and lowpass the difference product, or mix directly to baseband with a lock-in amplifier (typical to 100 kHz; SR844 to 200 MHz), rather than building a high-Q filter | Q = f_in/df | f_in, df | spot-noise measurement | measure | pp.562-563 §8.13.1 Fig. 8.100 | high |
| AOE-2677 | filter | Integrated noise from a noise density: white noise vn = en*sqrt(B); through a single RC, vn = en*sqrt(1.57*f3dB); in general integrate the squared density times the squared filter response (same for noise current with in) | vn^2 = integral(f1..f2) en(f)^2*H(f)^2 df (V^2 rms); white: vn^2 = en^2*B | en(f) (V/rtHz), H(f), B | rms noise prediction | calc | p.563 §8.13.2 eq.(8.56)-(8.57) | high |
| AOE-2678 | filter | The high end of the passband dominates integrated noise: the lowpass order at f2 matters (a single RC collects much unwanted high-frequency noise) while the highpass order at f1 hardly matters, even for pink noise and for a 10:1 passband | use >= 2-pole lowpass at f2; 1-pole highpass at f1 acceptable | f1, f2, orders | band-limited noise measurement and design | calc | pp.564-565 §8.13.2A Fig. 8.102 Fig. 8.103 | high |
| AOE-2679 | filter | Pink (1/f) and red (1/f^2) noise integrals diverge at zero frequency, so a nonzero lower band limit is mandatory; hence 1/f noise is specified in a bounded band such as 0.1-10 Hz | f1 > 0 | f1 | low-frequency noise specs/budgets | review | p.563 §8.13.2 | high |
| AOE-2680 | components | Op-amp datasheet 0.1-10 Hz noise is usually measured with an asymmetric filter (1-pole highpass at 0.1 Hz, 2-pole or higher lowpass at 10 Hz) and quoted peak-to-peak from a 10 s scope capture; convert with the rule of thumb | Vn(rms) ~ Vn(pp)/6 | Vn(pp) | comparing datasheet LF noise | calc | p.564 §8.13.3 | high |
| AOE-2681 | components | Integrated noise of an op-amp with white density enH and 1/f corner fc over a brick-wall band f1-f2 (sum of white and pink parts; use when the band straddles fc) | vn^2 = enH^2*(f2 - f1 + fc*ln(f2/f1)) (V^2 rms) | enH (V/rtHz), fc, f1, f2 (Hz) | conventional (non-auto-zero) op-amps | calc | p.565 §8.13.3A eq.(8.59) | high |
| AOE-2682 | components | Integrated noise versus decade of bandwidth: white noise grows sqrt(10) per decade; ideal pink noise is constant per decade; auto-zero op-amps (flat en) keep falling 10 dB per decade as bandwidth is reduced, whereas conventional op-amps flatten at low frequency | white: vn prop. sqrt(B); pink: equal vn per decade | spectrum type | choosing measurement bandwidth, auto-zero vs conventional parts | calc | p.566 §8.13.3A Fig. 8.106 | high |
| AOE-2683 | components | Find the 1/f corner frequency from two tabulated densities (datasheets often give en at 10 Hz and 1 kHz) | fc = fL*((enL/enH)^2 - 1) (Hz); enL = density at fL below the corner, enH = density well above it | enL, fL, enH | 1/f-plus-white spectra | calc | p.566 §8.13.4 eq.(8.60) Fig. 8.105 | high |
| AOE-2684 | components | Pure 1/f noise power diverges only logarithmically: total power from 1 uHz to 10 Hz is 3.5x that of 0.1-10 Hz, and to 1e-12 Hz only 6.5x (about 6x down to 1/(32,000 years)); measured LT1012 current noise stays pink or flatter (f^-1/4 or slower) down to 0.5 mHz | P(fa..fb) prop. ln(fb/fa) | fa, fb | long-term noise/drift estimates | calc | pp.566-567 §8.13.4B Fig. 8.107 | high |
| AOE-2685 | test | Measure op-amp noise spectra with an FFT/dynamic signal analyzer (1 Mohm input, to ~100 kHz; RF analyzers are 50/75 ohm): en with source resistor Rs = 0; in with Rs substantially larger than the amplifier noise resistance Rn = en/in; DUT in a shielded box with filtered dc feedthroughs | Rs >> Rn = en/in | en, in | op-amp and reference noise characterization | measure | pp.567-568 §8.13.5 Fig. 8.108 | high |
| AOE-2686 | test | rms noise readout: a true-rms meter must have response over the full measured band (some reach only a few kHz) and a crest factor of 3-5 for Gaussian noise; an averaging (sine-calibrated) meter reading pure Gaussian noise must be multiplied by 1.13 (+1 dB), not valid for sine plus noise; oscilloscope estimate rms = 1/6 to 1/8 of peak-to-peak | Vrms = 1.13*V_reading (averaging meter, Gaussian); Vrms ~ Vpp/6 to Vpp/8 | meter type, bandwidth, crest factor | noise voltage measurement | measure | pp.568-569 §8.13.5 | high |
| AOE-2687 | test | For in measurement with a source resistor, in*Rs must dominate both the amplifier en and the resistor's own Johnson noise; reference values for 1 Mohm at room temperature: ~128 nV/rtHz open circuit (scales as sqrt(R)) and 127 fA/rtHz short circuit (scales as 1/sqrt(R)); 100 Mohm -> 12.7 fA/rtHz; measuring 1 fA/rtHz needs Rs >= 10 Gohm (1.3 fA/rtHz) | in*Rs > en and in > sqrt(4*k*T/Rs) | in, en, Rs, T | op-amp current-noise measurement (1 Mohm voltage figure OCR-garbled; value computed) | calc | pp.569-570 §8.13.6 | high |
| AOE-2688 | components | Do not trust datasheet input current noise, especially for auto-zero op-amps and bias-cancelled BJT op-amps: it is often computed as sqrt(2*q*Ib) from the net (cancelled) bias current instead of measured; measured values were worse by up to ~50x (e.g. LTC1049 measured 100 fA/rtHz), while datasheet en values were correct | in_actual up to 50x in_datasheet | - | auto-zero and bias-cancelled op-amps | measure | p.569 §8.13.6 Table 8.5 | high |
| AOE-2689 | components | Auto-zero op-amps have flat low-frequency noise (no 1/f rise) but clock-induced spectral peaks at higher frequencies (e.g. AD8628A near 15 kHz); inspect the spectrum around the auto-zero clock before using one in a wideband path | - | - | auto-zero/chopper op-amps | measure | p.569 §8.13.6 Fig. 8.111 | high |
| AOE-2690 | test | Limits of the source-resistor in method at fA levels: bias-current dc drop (10 pA x 10 Gohm = 100 mV, saturating the output at G = 100), oscillation from output-to-input stray capacitance (tame with a shunt C), and bandwidth collapse (10 Gohm with 1 pF -> 16 Hz); instead drive a virtual-ground current amplifier from the DUT input (DL/Ithaco 1211: 1.5 fA/rtHz, 400 Hz on 1e-11 A/V, input held within 0.2 mV of ground; Laser Components DDPCA-300: 1e4-1e13 V/A, down to 0.2 fA/rtHz; Keithley 428: 1e3-1e11 V/A) | f_BW = 1/(2*pi*Rs*C_in) | Rs, C_in, Ib | fA-level current-noise measurement | calc | p.570 §8.13.6A-B Fig. 8.112 | high |
| AOE-2691 | test | Resistorless fA/rtHz current-noise jig: composite integrator (DUT as follower driving an overcompensated inverting stage, ~16 kHz unity-gain bandwidth) with a feedback capacitor only (a resistor would need > 16,000 Mohm to stay below 1 fA/rtHz; a capacitor has no Johnson noise); the output is vn = in/(omega*Cf) (white in gives a 1/f vn); valid up to the frequency where en*omega*C takes over; 7-8 pF Cf, guarded 1 Tohm suppression resistor and LT1677 (3.2 nV/rtHz) reach 0.1 fA/rtHz with ~1 kHz bandwidth | vn = in/(2*pi*f*Cf); f_max = in/(2*pi*en*Cf) (Hz) | Cf, en, in | op-amp input current noise below ~1 fA/rtHz | calc | pp.571-572 §8.13.7 Fig. 8.113 Fig. 8.114 fn.134 | high |
| AOE-2692 | assembly | Femtoamp circuits: use guarded high-value glass resistors (Welwyn glass-sealed with a conductive guard ring tied to ground, so the envelope between guard and summing junction sees only the op-amp offset, <= ~1 mV); moisture (breath) raised leakage 20x until dry; a 1 Tohm bias-suppression resistor itself adds sqrt(4kT/Rs) = 0.13 fA/rtHz (100 Tohm, e.g. Welwyn 3810 series, gives 10x less only if every other insulation path stays higher) | in_Rs = sqrt(4*k*T/Rs) (A/rtHz) | Rs | fA-level current measurement | inspect | p.573 §8.13.7 fn.135 | high |
| AOE-2693 | components | Reset switch for femtoamp integrators: plastic pushbuttons inject static charge (output jumps +1.5 to +2 V on release); a magnetically actuated reed switch inside the shield (OFF ~0.3 pF and > 1e12 ohm, no coil coupling) is better, but triboelectric charge on its glass envelope still injected ~0.35 pC (-50 mV step; 15-100 mV for other models) until wiped along its length with grounded metal cloth (-> ~2 mV) | q = Cf*dV (C) | Cf, dV | fA/pF integrators and electrometers | measure | pp.573-574 §8.13.7A | high |
| AOE-2694 | test | Averaging time an indicating instrument (linear detector) needs so that rectified noise of bandwidth B fluctuates by a standard deviation of sigma percent | tau ~ 600/(B*sigma^2) (s; B in Hz, sigma in %) | B, sigma | reading noise on meters | calc | p.574 §8.13.8 eq.(8.61) | medium |
| AOE-2695 | test | Noise waveform statistics: expected maxima per second of band-limited white noise N = 0.77*f2 for f1 = 0 and ~(f1+f2)/2 for narrowband noise; rms/average (average magnitude) ratio 1.25 (1.96 dB) for Gaussian noise, 1.11 (0.91 dB) for a sinewave, 1 (0 dB) for a square wave (hence the 1.13 correction of sine-calibrated averaging meters) | N = sqrt((3/5)*(f2^5 - f1^5)/(f2^3 - f1^3)) (1/s); rms/avg(Gaussian) = sqrt(pi/2) | f1, f2 | noise statistics and meter corrections (eq. 8.62 OCR-garbled, formula re-derived to match the stated limits) | calc | p.574 §8.13.8 eq.(8.62) | medium |
| AOE-2696 | components | Quantization noise of an ideal quantizer | sigma_q = LSB/sqrt(12) ~ 0.3 LSB (rms) | LSB size | ADC/DAC noise budgets | calc | p.574 §8.13.8 eq.(8.64) | high |
| AOE-2697 | test | False-trigger rate of a threshold on lowpass-filtered Gaussian white noise: positive crossings fall as exp(-Vth^2/2) with Vth in rms units, proportional to the brick-wall bandwidth BW; double the rate for symmetric (magnitude) thresholds; Fig. 8.115 gives the fraction of time a level is exceeded (also sets required meter crest factor) | TCR = (BW/sqrt(3))*exp(-Vth^2/2) crossings/s (prefactor illegible in scan; BW/sqrt(3) is the standard Rice result - verify against printed p.574) | Vth/Vrms, BW (Hz) | comparator/trigger thresholds on noisy signals | calc | p.574 §8.13.8 eq.(8.63) fn.139 Fig. 8.115 | low |
| AOE-2698 | requirements | To dig a weak slow signal out of noise, do not just add a lowpass (1/f noise near dc and drifts defeat it); make the signal periodic by modulating it and detect synchronously (lock-in, signal averaging, boxcar), moving the signal away from 1/f noise | - | - | weak signals buried in noise/interference | review | pp.574-575 §8.14 | medium |
| AOE-2699 | test | Lock-in (phase-sensitive) detection: modulate a source parameter (~100 Hz-1 kHz), demodulate with a gain-reversing switch phase detector (e.g. AD630) or an analog multiplier with sine reference (AD633/AD734, avoids odd-harmonic response), then lowpass with tau = RC >> T; output averages to -(2*Es/pi)*sin(phi); a component offset by d_omega passes only if d_omega < 1/tau; the post-detection lowpass sets the bandwidth (1 Hz rolloff admits noise only within 1 Hz) and limits how fast any slow parameter sweep may run (tau from fractions of a second to tens of seconds) | <Vout> = -(2*Es/pi)*sin(phi); pass if d_omega < 1/tau | Es, phi, tau (s) | weak periodic signals | calc | pp.575-576 §8.14.1A-B Fig. 8.116 Fig. 8.117 | high |
| AOE-2700 | test | Lock-in modulation choice: small sinusoidal modulation outputs the derivative of the line shape, large square-wave modulation outputs the line shape itself (Dicke switch in radio astronomy, rocking secondary mirror in IR astronomy); demo: lock-in with a few-second time constant ignored >50 dB ambient-light swings on an LED/phototransistor link at 6 ft; commercial DSP lock-ins digitize to 20 bits, typically to 100 kHz (SR844 to 200 MHz via heterodyning); Hamamatsu S6809/46/S6986 integrate synchronous ambient-light rejection for ~$6 | - | - | optical/NMR/astronomy style measurements | review | pp.577-578 §8.14.1C Fig. 8.118 Fig. 8.119 | high |
| AOE-2701 | power | Treat supply noise as a design input for low-noise circuits: rails are rarely quieter than 100 nV/rtHz (100x a 1 nV/rtHz input target); a bench-supply spec of 0.2 mV rms / 2 mV pp is 46 dB above a preamp's 1 uV rms (20 kHz) output noise; measured linear supplies spread 100x (40 dB); quietest sources: lead-acid battery (trickle-charged at load current) and an ovenized-zener precision supply; noisiest: switchmode phone charger and unregulated wall-wart | e_supply/PSRR << en_in*Gv (referred to output) | supply noise spectrum, PSRR, Gv | low-noise analog supplies (circuits marked 'quiet' rails) | measure | p.578 §8.15 Fig. 8.123 | high |
| AOE-2702 | power | A quiet linear supply needs plenty of loop gain with low-noise error amplifiers and, critically, a low-noise voltage reference, because low-frequency reference noise cannot be filtered out afterward | - | - | low-noise regulated supplies | review | p.578 §8.15 (see §9.10) | medium |
| AOE-2703 | power | Capacitance multiplier: emitter follower whose base is driven by an RC-smoothed replica of the noisy input; choose R so the drop at maximum load is at most about a volt (0.6-2 V across the stage), and C for an RC long enough to attenuate the band of interest (a 2-stage RC of ~2 s reached the analyzer floor); a ferrite bead or ~100 ohm resistor prevents oscillation; gives no dc regulation (no feedback) | V_drop = 0.6-2 V; RC >> 1/(2*pi*f_min) | R, C, I_load, beta | cleaning noisy dc rails | calc | pp.578-579 §8.15.1 Fig. 8.121 | medium |
| AOE-2704 | power | Put a capacitance multiplier upstream of the regulator pass element (after rectifier and storage capacitor) to get ~100x lower output ripple more easily than by raising loop gain and bandwidth; give Q1 enough drop for the full ripple (100/120 Hz ripple at full load can be a few volts): add a base-to-ground resistor or use a MOSFET for more headroom; measured with 100 ohm load it far outperforms the same 10,000 uF placed directly across the supply terminals | V_CE(Q1)_min > V_ripple_pp at full load | V_ripple_pp, V_drop | line-powered supplies, retrofits of commercial supplies/instruments | measure | p.579 §8.15.1 Fig. 8.122 | high |
| AOE-2705 | emc | Capacitive (electrostatic) coupling hits high-impedance nodes: cure by reducing capacitance to the aggressor (move apart), shielding (a complete metal enclosure or close-knit metal screen eliminates it), routing wires close to a ground plane (swallows fringing fields), and lowering the impedance of susceptible points; op-amp outputs rarely pick up interference, inputs do | - | node impedance, aggressor dV/dt | wiring and layout inside instruments | inspect | p.581 §8.16.1C | medium |
| AOE-2706 | emc | Low-frequency magnetic fields are not significantly reduced by metal enclosures: minimize enclosed loop area (magnetic pickup is independent of impedance level), use twisted pairs, move or exile large power transformers; where needed use mu-metal with high-permeability inner shield and lower-permeability outer shield (iron/low-mu) to keep the inner one out of saturation; prefer shielded (pot-core) inductors and toroidal transformers, adding a single reverse turn to cancel the toroid's one-turn effective area | - | loop area, field strength | low-level amplifiers, microphones, tape heads, inductors, wirewound resistors near transformers | inspect | pp.581-582 §8.16.1D | medium |
| AOE-2707 | emc | Fit ac power inputs with an RF line filter plus transient suppressor: expect 60 dB or better attenuation of interference above a few hundred kilohertz and elimination of damaging spikes | attenuation >= 60 dB above a few hundred kHz | - | line-powered instruments | measure | p.581 §8.16.1B | high |
| AOE-2708 | cables | Always shield low-level signals, especially at high impedance, and shield the instrument cabinet; lowpass-filter inputs and outputs where the signal band allows (e.g. audio amplifiers pick up radio stations via speaker wires acting as antennas) | - | signal level, impedance | instrument I/O | inspect | p.581 §8.16.1B | medium |
| AOE-2709 | crosstalk | Inductive and capacitive crosstalk between PCB traces or cable pairs are generally comparable: far-end (forward) crosstalk is proportional to the aggressor slew rate with capacitive and inductive parts of opposite sign (tending to cancel); near-end (reverse) crosstalk is a flat-topped pulse as wide as the round-trip travel time with the parts reinforcing, and if the driver does not match Z0 it reflects and often dominates the far-end crosstalk | NEXT width = 2*t_pd (s) | t_pd, slew rate, driver impedance vs Z0 | closely spaced lines/ribbons | sim | p.581 §8.16.1E | medium |
| AOE-2710 | decoupling | Several ceramic bypass capacitors plus supply-rail inductance can form a parasitic tuned circuit in the HF-VHF range (tens to hundreds of MHz) that active circuits drive into ringing or oscillation; include some aluminum electrolytic capacitors whose lossy ESR damps the resonance; keep leads short, avoid resonant loops, use ferrite beads for very-high-frequency problems | - | bypass network, rail inductance | supply bypassing | sim | p.582 §8.16.1F fn.148 | medium |
| AOE-2711 | grounding | Tie both the low-level stage and the high-current stage to the supply at the regulator (at its sense leads) so power-stage IR drops never appear on low-level rails, and never let load return current flow through the ground reference of the low-level input (e.g. case ground at the BNC); otherwise supply coupling can cause oscillation; a single ground 'mecca' is a crutch - trace where the big currents flow | - | current paths | mixed low-level/high-current instruments | review | pp.582-583 §8.16.2A Fig. 8.124 | medium |
| AOE-2712 | decoupling | Decouple the dc rail feeding low-level stages with a small series-R shunt-C network, and in stubborn supply-coupling cases give the low-level stage its own zener or 3-terminal regulator | - | - | low-level stages sharing rails with power stages | inspect | p.583 §8.16.2A Fig. 8.125 | medium |
| AOE-2713 | grounding | Expect 100 mV or more at powerline frequency (plus harmonics, RF and spikes) between the local grounds of instruments on different outlets even when their grounds are bridged; signals of several volts or logic swings can simply be tied together, small signals cannot | V_ground_diff >= 100 mV (planning value) | signal amplitude | inter-instrument connections | measure | p.583 §8.16.3A Fig. 8.126 | high |
| AOE-2714 | grounding | Small signals between instruments over coax: ground the shield to case/circuit ground at the driving end, isolate it at the receiving end (insulated BNC, e.g. Bendix 4890-1 or Amphenol 31-010) and receive with a differential amplifier; add a small resistor and bypass capacitor from shield to ground at the receiver to limit ground swing and protect the input | - | - | low-level inter-instrument links | inspect | pp.583-584 §8.16.3B Fig. 8.127A | medium |
| AOE-2715 | grounding | Pseudo-differential input for a single-ended amplifier: put ~10 ohm between the amplifier common and circuit ground so the source's ground sets the reference; ground noise then appears at the output unamplified, giving an effective CMRR equal to the stage voltage gain | CMRR_eff = Gv; R_common ~10 ohm >> source ground impedance | Gv, R_common | high-gain single-ended stages with ground noise, between or within instruments | calc | p.584 §8.16.3B Fig. 8.127A | high |
| AOE-2716 | cables | Shielded twisted pair with the shield tied to the case at both ends (harmless because no signal flows on the shield) plus a differential receiver; send logic signals differentially; for severe ground interference use isolation amplifiers (kilovolts of common mode) or optoisolators; at RF, transformer coupling removes common mode and eases generating a differential drive | - | - | inter-instrument links | inspect | p.584 §8.16.3B Fig. 8.127B | medium |
| AOE-2717 | cables | Very long cable runs (miles): tie the shield to the case through a small inductor so dc shield voltage stays small while large RF shield currents are blocked; receive differentially with protection limiting common-mode excursions to +-10 V | V_CM clamp = +-10 V | - | long-line inputs | inspect | p.584 §8.16.3B Fig. 8.128 | medium |
| AOE-2718 | cables | In a multiwire cable with common-mode pickup, run one extra wire grounded at the sending end, buffer it at the receiver and use it as the comparison input for each of the n differential receivers; effective at low-moderate frequencies only (receiver CMRR is poor at RF) | wires = n + 1 | n | multichannel low-frequency cables | review | p.584 §8.16.3B Fig. 8.129 | medium |
| AOE-2719 | emc | Suppress RF common-mode on a coaxial or multiwire cable by wrapping the whole cable around a ferrite toroid (a 1:1 transmission-line transformer): common-mode impedance rises so small bypass capacitors at the far end can shunt it, while the differential signal is unaffected | - | - | cables carrying RF common-mode interference | measure | pp.584-585 §8.16.3B Fig. 8.130 | medium |
| AOE-2720 | grounding | Floating low-level transducers (tape head, mV sources) on shielded lines: ground the shield at the receiving (amplifier) end only and lift it at the transducer, otherwise ground-potential differences appear as signal (sneak ground currents) | shield grounded at one end (receiver) | - | mV-level floating sources | inspect | p.585 §8.16.3C Fig. 8.131 | high |
| AOE-2721 | protection | Isolation amplifiers are mandatory for medical electronics with electrodes on human subjects and useful wherever grounds differ by up to kilovolts: transformer-coupled (AD215: 1.5 kV, typical 5 kHz bandwidth, 120 kHz for AD215, 0.015% max nonlinearity, ~$80, dc power needed on one side only), optical delta-sigma (ACPL-C79: dc-200 kHz, 0.06%, ~$5), capacitive (ISO124: 1.5 kV, 0.01%, 50 kHz, $18, supplies on both sides); digital optoisolators give >= 10 MHz and several kV for $1-2; iso-amps add clock residue and broadband noise, so lowpass their output | V_iso >= expected ground difference | V_iso, BW, nonlinearity | medical and high-common-mode analog links | review | pp.585-587 §8.16.3D Fig. 8.132-8.137 | high |
| AOE-2722 | components | Guard high-impedance inputs (micro-electrodes, capacitive transducers, sources of hundreds of Mohm; a few pF then gives rolloff at a few Hz; cable insulation leakage degrades sub-pA amplifiers by orders of magnitude): drive an inner shield with a follower of the signal (zero voltage difference -> no leakage or capacitive attenuation), add an outer grounded shield, place the follower close to the source and guard only the short cable to it | f_roll = 1/(2*pi*Rs*Cin) without guard | Rs, Cin, Ib | high-impedance sensor inputs | calc | p.587 §8.16.3E Fig. 8.138 | high |
| AOE-2723 | crosstalk | Precision analog lines near fast logic: op-amp closed-loop output impedance rises to 10-100 ohm or more at 1 MHz, so with 0.5 V/ns logic edges the coupling capacitance must stay below 0.02 pF to keep interference under 0.1 mV; keep analog away from fast logic, add a few hundred pF from output to ground (with a small series resistor for op-amp stability), or use a low-impedance buffer (LT1010) or power op-amp (LM675), plus shielding, twisted pairs and ground planes | Cc_max = V_err/(Zout*dV/dt) (F); e.g. 0.1 mV/(10 ohm*0.5 V/ns) = 0.02 pF | Zout(f), aggressor dV/dt, V_err | precision DAC/op-amp outputs routed near digital logic | calc | pp.587-588 §8.16.3F Fig. 8.139 | high |
| AOE-2724 | components | Noise quick-reference anchors (scale Johnson noise by sqrt(R), shot noise by sqrt(I)): Johnson 1.28 nV/rtHz at 100 ohm, 4 nV/rtHz at 1 kohm, 128 nV/rtHz at 1 Mohm; shot noise 18 pA/rtHz at 1 mA dc; shot noise of the current through a resistor exceeds its Johnson noise when its dc drop exceeds 50 mV; a 10 pA JFET gate leakage gives 1.8 fA/rtHz (1.8 nV/rtHz in 1 Mohm) | en = sqrt(4*k*T*R); in = sqrt(2*q*Idc) | R, Idc | first-pass noise budgets | calc | pp.590-591 Review of Ch.8 E, F, H fn.151 | high |

### Ch.9 §9.1-§9.5: linear regulators, heat and SOA, ac line to unregulated dc (pp. 594-636; lines 35530-38215)
| id | domain | rule statement | formula / limit (units) | inputs | applicability & conditions | verify by | source | conf |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| AOE-2725 | power | Size a resistor+zener shunt regulator so some zener current remains at maximum load and minimum input; the supply then runs at full current all the time, so the zener and R each dissipate the full-load power even at zero load. | I_zener = (V+ - Vz)/R; design: R <= (V+min - Vz)/(I_load,max + I_z,min); P_z(max) = Vz*(V+max - Vz)/R at zero load (derived) | V+min, V+max (V), Vz (V), I_load,max (A), I_z,min (A) | Resistor-biased zener used directly as regulator; book example (+5 V, 0-1 A, unregulated 9-12 V +/-10%, 50 mA min zener current) needs a 10 W zener and ~10 W in each part | calc | p.595-596 §9.1, Fig. 9.2A, Exercise 9.1 | medium |
| AOE-2726 | power | Do not use a bare zener shunt regulator for large or widely varying load currents; use a reference + error amplifier + series pass transistor (can reach >=75% efficiency over most of the load range with an adjustable output). | qualitative; high-power zeners "hard to find and rarely used" | load current range | Linear regulator architecture choice | review | p.596 §9.1 | high |
| AOE-2727 | power | Bias a zener used only as a reference at a few mA; an IC voltage reference needs only 0.1-1 mA. | I_Z(ref) = a few mA (zener); 0.1-1 mA (IC reference) | reference type | Reference feeding an error amplifier (Fig. 9.2C) | review | p.597 §9.1.1A | high |
| AOE-2728 | power | Zener-plus-noninverting-amplifier regulator: output is set by the divider and cannot go below Vz or above the op-amp swing; op-amp output current alone is typically limited to ~20 mA. | Vout = Vz*(1 + R2/R1); Vout > Vz; I_out(max) ~ 20 mA (op-amp alone) | Vz (V), R1, R2 (ohm) | Fig. 9.2C | calc | p.597 §9.1.1A | high |
| AOE-2729 | power | Close the regulator feedback loop around the pass transistor (sense the actual output), rather than compensating the pass transistor's ~0.6 V Vbe drop with the divider; Vbe varies with temperature and load current. | Vbe ~ 0.6 V (uncontrolled if outside the loop) | schematic topology | Series-pass linear regulator with npn follower (Fig. 9.2D) | inspect | p.597 §9.1.1B | high |
| AOE-2730 | power | An emitter-follower pass transistor multiplies the error amplifier's maximum output current by beta; use a Darlington (or n-channel MOSFET) for more current. | I_out(max) = beta * I_opamp(max) | beta, I_opamp(max) (A) | Discrete series-pass regulator | calc | p.597 §9.1.1B | high |
| AOE-2731 | control-loop | Compensate a discrete series regulator with a Miller capacitor Cc around the inverting gain stage; find Cc by increasing it until a load-current step gives a well-damped output, then double it for stability margin. Typical Cc = 100-1000 pF. | Cc = 100-1000 pF typical; Cc(final) = 2 x Cc(just well-damped) | load-step response | Regulator driving capacitive (bypassed) load; IC regulators have internal compensation or datasheet values | measure | p.597 §9.1.1C | high |
| AOE-2732 | control-loop | Treat the bypass capacitance distributed across the powered circuit as the regulator's capacitive load: with the pass transistor's (and sense resistor's) output resistance it adds lagging phase shift and can make the regulator oscillate. | qualitative; include C_bypass(total) in loop analysis | total downstream bypass capacitance (F), regulator output resistance (ohm) | Any linear regulator feeding bypassed circuitry | sim | p.597 §9.1.1C, Fig. 9.2D | high |
| AOE-2733 | protection | Every series-pass regulator needs output current limiting: with a shorted output, feedback forces the op-amp's full 20-40 mA into the pass-transistor base, and beta of 50-250 turns that into 1-10 A. | I_short(unlimited) = I_opamp(20-40 mA) * beta(50-250) = 1-10 A | op-amp I_out(max), beta | Discrete regulator without current limit (Fig. 9.2D) | review | p.597 §9.1.1C | high |
| AOE-2734 | protection | Classic current limit: choose sense resistor Rcl to drop ~0.6 V (one Vbe) at a current somewhat above the maximum rated current; Q2 then steals pass-transistor base drive. Example Rcl = 5 ohm for a 100 mA supply (OCR "5 0"). | Rcl ~ 0.6 V / I_limit; I_limit slightly > I_rated | I_rated (A), Vbe (V) | Fig. 9.2E, npn-follower regulators | calc | p.597 §9.1.1C, Fig. 9.2E | high |
| AOE-2735 | components | The current-limit sense transistor needs no power rating: it sees at most ~2 Vbe collector-emitter, the op-amp's max output current, and their product. | Vce < 1.5 V, Ic < 40 mA, P ~ 60 mW (any small-signal transistor) | op-amp I_out(max) | Fig. 9.2E topology | calc | p.597 §9.1.1C | high |
| AOE-2736 | power | Filter ripple out of the zener-reference bias: split the bias resistor into R1a, R1b and bypass the midpoint with C1, with a time constant long compared with the ripple period (8.3 ms at 120 Hz); or bias the zener from a current source. | tau = par(R1a,R1b)*C1 >> T_ripple (8.3 ms for 120 Hz; 10 ms for 100 Hz) | R1a, R1b (ohm), C1 (F), mains frequency | Reference fed from unregulated (rippled) input; unnecessary if fed from a regulated rail | calc | p.598 §9.1.1C, Fig. 9.2E | high |
| AOE-2737 | protection | Add an overvoltage crowbar (SCR fired by a zener + diode drop, ~100 ohm gate resistor) that shorts the output if it exceeds ~6.2 V on a +5 V rail (pass transistor C-E short, open divider resistor); fuse the supply because the crowbar may sink very large current if the fault also disables current limiting. | V_trip = Vz(D1) + V_diode ~ 6.2 V for a 5 V rail | Vout nominal, Vz | Linear supplies with a possible pass-transistor short | review | p.598 §9.1.1C, Fig. 9.2E; see Fig. 9.48, §9.13.1 | high |
| AOE-2738 | power | The 723 regulator: reference 7.15 V +/-5% (production spread 6.8-7.5 V, so provide an output trim); internal pass transistor 150 mA max; ~0.5 W; no internal thermal/overload shutdown. | Vref = 7.15 V +/-5%; I_pass <= 150 mA; P ~ 0.5 W | - | uA723 | review | p.598-599 §9.2.1, Fig. 9.3 | high |
| AOE-2739 | power | 723 operating limits: dropout 3 V minimum; unregulated input never below +9.5 V (reference supply); output from Vref up to 37 V (Fig. 9.4) or from +2 V to Vref (Fig. 9.5, divider on reference). | V_in - V_out >= 3 V; V_in >= 9.5 V; 2 V <= V_out <= 37 V | V_in(min incl. ripple), V_out | uA723 | calc | p.599 §9.2.1A-B | high |

Still allow a few volts of headroom from an unregulated supply because worst-case design must work at 105 Vac line. | V_in(pass) - V_out >= ~1.5 V (Darlington npn) or ~1 Vbe (pnp); design at low line 105 Vac | line min (Vac), V_out | Discrete/723 regulators | calc | p.600 §9.2.2C, footnote 9 | high |

Internal current-sense resistor 0.2 ohm. | I_out <= 1 A (78xx), 0.1 A (78Lxx) | V_out, I_out | 78xx/LM340/79xx/LM320 families | review | p.601 §9.3.2, Fig. 9.6, Table 9.1 | high |
| AOE-2746 | decoupling | Fit the datasheet minimum input and output bypass capacitors at 3-terminal regulators (needed for stability and transient response); the input or output cap may be omitted only if the source or load is already bypassed close to the regulator. | AOE-2762 | components | Bandgap reference principle (used in the LM317): two transistors at a 10:1 current-density ratio differ in Vbe by (kT/q)*ln(10) ~ 60 mV (PTAT); scaling this PTAT drop to cancel Vbe's ~-2.1 mV/C tempco (Vbe ~ 600 mV) yields zero tempco near 1.2 V (silicon bandgap). | dVbe = (k*T/q)*ln(N) = ~60 mV for N = 10 at room temp; Vbe tempco ~ -2.1 mV/C; V_ref ~ 1.2 V | N (area ratio), T (K) | Bandgap references/regulators | calc | p.604 "Anatomy of a 317", Fig. 9.13 | high |

| AOE-2764 | power | For an LM317 to regulate at zero external load, choose R1 so the divider alone carries the worst-case minimum load current: R1 <= 125 ohm for Vref = 1.25 V and I_out(min) = 10 mA. Calculating with the typical I_adj halves the worst-case error. | V_out = Vref*(1 + R2/R1) + I_adj*R2; fractional error = I_adj*par(R1,R2)/Vref (derived) | Vref, R1, R2, I_adj | LM317-style regulators | calc | p.605-606 §9.3.4E, eq. 9.1, Fig. 9.14E, footnote 17 | high |

The ramp starts at Vref (1.25 V), or at Vref + Vbe (~1.8 V) with the follower; the disable switch only pulls the output down to Vref. | S = dV/dt = I/C; I = Vref/R1 or Vbe/R3 | R1, R3 (ohm), C (F) | Fig. 9.14G-H; always add the protection diode | calc | p.606-607 §9.3.4G-H, Fig. 9.14 | high |

| AOE-2794 | power | Regulator-type selection: no low-dropout need -> conventional 3-terminal fixed (78xx/79xx) or adjustable (317/337), cheaper and stable with small bypass caps; adjust to 0 V -> current-reference (LT3080) type; LDO (V_do < 1 V) with V_in > 10 V -> bipolar (LT1083-85, LM1117, LM350, LM338 for ~1 V; LT1764A for ~0.3 V); V_in < 10 V -> MOSFET LDOs; efficiency, power density, step-up or inversion -> switching regulator; high-current low-voltage -> regulator with separate control (bias) and pass-element input pins. | decision table (V_do, V_in thresholds 1 V, 10 V) | V_in, V_out, V_do needed, I_out | Linear regulator choice | review | p.613 §9.3.11 | high |
| AOE-2795 | thermal | Never assume a negative regulator has the positive twin's pinout or tab potential: the 78xx tab is ground (can be screwed to chassis/ground plane) but the 79xx tab is the input voltage (IC substrate) - grounding it shorts the input. | AOE-2796 | power | LDO regulators typically drop < 100 mV except at their highest currents; the ADJ versions of some parts are less accurate than the laser-trimmed fixed versions. | V_do < 100 mV typ (below max current) | I_out/I_max | LDO selection (Table 9.3) | review | p.615 Table 9.3 note (a) | high |
| AOE-2797 | requirements | Compare regulator noise specs only on the same bandwidth and measure (rms vs peak-to-peak) and with/without noise-reduction capacitor: Table 9.3 bands include 10 Hz-100 kHz (default), 100 Hz- or 200 Hz-100 kHz, 10 Hz-10 kHz, 300 Hz-50 kHz, 10 Hz-1 MHz. | normalize e.g. V_n,rms to the same band before comparing | noise spec, bandwidth, NR cap | Regulator noise selection | review | p.615 Table 9.3 notes (b), (e1)-(e4); p.619 §9.3.12H | high |
| AOE-2798 | protection | Check an LDO's reverse-voltage behavior: some pass reverse current through an input-output back diode (limit that current; reversed supply not allowed; add a Schottky), some block current when V_in < V_out, some also block reversed input polarity. | classes: back-diode conducts / reverse-block / reverse-polarity protected | datasheet reverse behavior | LDO selection (Table 9.3 "reverse block" column) | review | p.615 Table 9.3 notes (x), A, square; p.616 §9.3.12C | high |
| AOE-2799 | protection | Automotive load dump can drive a 13.8 V rail to 50 V or more; use regulators specified for load dump (withstand input transients or continuous inputs to 60 V, or 40 V for some parts, without passing the spike to the output). | V_in(abs max) >= load-dump peak (>= 50 V); load-dump-rated parts: 60 V (some 40 V) | V_in transient amplitude (V) | Vehicle-powered electronics | review | p.615 Table 9.3 note (o); p.618 §9.3.12G | high |
| AOE-2800 | protection | With split supplies, a single fault (e.g. C-E short in a push-pull transistor across the rails) or an asymmetric load at power-off can reverse one rail; connect a power rectifier (preferably Schottky) reverse-biased from each regulated output to ground. For single supplies, a reverse high-current rectifier across the circuit guards against backward wiring. | reverse Schottky per rail | rails, polarity | Any multi-rail circuit | inspect | p.616 §9.3.12C, Fig. 9.8 | high |
| AOE-2801 | power | Bipolar (pnp-output) LDO ground-pin current rises sharply near dropout (pnp saturates, beta collapses): LT1764A-3.3 at 100 mA load goes from ~5 mA to ~50 mA; its no-load quiescent current spikes from 1 mA to ~30 mA. Budget battery life with the dropout current, or choose a PMOS LDO (TLV70450 stays at 3 uA below dropout whereas LT3008-5 rises from 3 uA to 30 uA). Table 9.3 Iq values are for light load with input above dropout. | I_gnd(dropout) up to ~10 x normal (LT1764A); PMOS: no increase | V_in near V_out + V_do, I_load | Battery-operated devices with pnp LDOs | calc | p.616 §9.3.12D, footnote 23 | high |
| AOE-2802 | derating | Many CMOS LDOs are rated for only +5.5 V maximum input (some +10 V); above that use bipolar LDOs (e.g. LT1764A 2.7-20 V, LT3012 4-80 V). | V_in(max) <= 5.5 V (many CMOS), 10 V (some CMOS) | V_in(max incl. transients) | CMOS LDO selection | review | p.616-617 §9.3.12E | high |
| AOE-2803 | protection | Put a transient voltage suppressor at the input of regulator ICs powered from an off-card dc source (nanometer-scale ICs are more fragile than legacy bipolar parts); power low-voltage ICs (6-7 V absolute max) from an on-card regulated rail (e.g. 5 V), not directly from external sources (a 3.7 V Li-ion cell may be an exception). | TVS at off-board dc input; V_absmax <= 7 V parts fed only from on-card rails | power entry, part abs-max ratings | Small dense PCBs fed from external dc | inspect | p.616 footnote 24 | high |
| AOE-2804 | control-loop | LDO output-capacitor requirements vary enormously ("No Cap" to "100 uF tantalum") and instability can show as wrong or zero output or overvoltage: an LP2950 with a 0.1 uF ceramic (< 1 uF minimum, ESR too low) failed; an LM2940 (+5 V, 1 A) bypassed with 0.22 uF instead of 22 uF oscillated and its dc output rose to 7.5 V. Obey the datasheet's C_out(min) and ESR(min,max) and its stability contours versus capacitance, ESR and load current. | C_out >= C_min; ESR_min <= ESR <= ESR_max over I_out (Fig. 9.27) | C_out (F), ESR (ohm), I_out (A) | All LDOs, esp. bipolar pnp-output types | inspect | p.617 §9.3.12F, Fig. 9.27 | high |
| AOE-2805 | control-loop | Do not connect a ceramic capacitor > 1000 pF directly from an ESR-compensated LDO's output (e.g. LP2951) to ground: ceramic ESR (5-10 mohm) is below the lower stable limit because the loop relies on the output-capacitor ESR zero for phase lead; if a ceramic must be used, add ~0.1-2 ohm in series. | ESR_ceramic ~ 5-10 mohm < ESR_min; series R = 0.1-2 ohm | capacitor type, ESR | LP2950/2951-class LDOs | inspect | p.617 footnote 25 (quoting LP2951 datasheet) | high |
| AOE-2806 | decoupling | Regulator loop bandwidths are only tens to hundreds of kHz, so output capacitors must hold the rail during fast load steps; include low-ESR and low-ESL capacitors for low-voltage loads with abrupt multi-amp current steps (e.g. microprocessors). | f_loop ~ 10 kHz-100s kHz; dV ~ dI*(ESR + ESL/t_r) in the short term (derived) | load step dI (A), dI/dt | Processor/FPGA rails | sim | p.617-618 §9.3.12G | high |
| AOE-2807 | decoupling | Plentiful low-inductance SMT capacitors with low-resistance, low-inductance power/ground foils cut an LDO's load-step dip ~10x: a 1 V 6 A LDO (MIC5191) with 1-5 A steps dipped ~40 mV on a through-hole protoboard (10 uF Ta + 2x0.1 uF in; 47 uF X5R + 10 uF Ta out) versus ~4 mV on a PCB with SMT parts (560 uF Al-polymer + 100 uF Ta-polymer + 2x22 uF X5R 0805 at input and output, plus 2x10 uF X5R 0805 at output), recovering to within a fraction of a mV instead of a ~6 mV drop. | dip 40 mV -> 4 mV (1-5 A step) | layout, capacitor set | Low-voltage high-current rails | measure | p.617-618 §9.3.12G, footnotes 26-27, Fig. 9.28-9.31 | high |
| AOE-2808 | power | Line-regulation specs are dc/low-frequency; for fast input-voltage transients larger low-ESR output capacitors are a better defense than input capacitors. | qualitative | - | Regulators with transient-prone inputs | review | p.618 §9.3.12G | high |
| AOE-2809 | power | For low-level or precision analog rails choose low-noise regulators: LT1764/1963 40 uVrms (10 Hz-100 kHz), ADP7102/04 15 uVrms, LT1964 (negative) 30 uVrms with a 10 nF reference-filter capacitor. Switching regulators are acceptable (and almost universal) for digital rails (~1.0-3.3 V). | V_n: 15-40 uVrms (10 Hz-100 kHz) for low-noise LDOs | noise budget (uVrms) | Analog vs digital rails | review | p.618-619 §9.3.12H, footnote 28 | high |
| AOE-2810 | protection | If a regulator drives large output capacitance and its input can be abruptly brought to zero (crowbar, short), fit a diode (e.g. 1N4001) from output to input so the output capacitance cannot source destructive current back into the regulator; same hazard applies to an external reference-filter capacitor. Many LDOs have an internal diode adequate only for modest (< 10 uF) load capacitance. | diode OUT->IN when C_load large; internal diode OK only for C_load < ~10 uF | C_load (F), crowbar present | LM317 and LDOs (Fig. 9.32) | inspect | p.619 §9.3.12I, Fig. 9.32, footnote 29 | high |
| AOE-2811 | power | Linear-regulator output noise = reference noise x (V_out/V_ref) + error-amplifier noise + unsuppressed input ripple; bypassing the 317 ADJ pin stops the reference noise being multiplied by 1 + R2/R1 and improves ripple rejection from 65 dB to 80 dB (typ); add the ADJ protection diode if that capacitor exceeds 10 uF. | V_n,out ~ V_n,ref*(V_out/V_ref) (+ amp noise); PSRR 65 -> 80 dB with C_adj | V_out/V_ref, C_adj (F) | LM317-style (Fig. 9.33A) | calc | p.619 §9.3.13, Fig. 9.33A | high |
| AOE-2812 | power | Filtering the reference directly is more effective: LT3080 with C1 = 0.1 uF across R_SET gives ~40 uVrms (10 Hz-100 kHz) output noise, but slows startup (tau = R_SET*C1 = 100 ms for 0.1 uF and 1 M at 10 V out). CMOS LDOs with an NR pin reach ~40 uVrms (100 Hz-100 kHz) with the recommended 0.1 uF. | tau_startup = R_SET*C_NR; V_n ~ 40 uVrms | R_SET (ohm), C_NR (F) | LT3080, NR-pin LDOs (Fig. 9.33B-C) | calc | p.619 §9.3.13, Fig. 9.33B-C | high |
| AOE-2813 | power | Prefilter the regulator's dc input (e.g. with a capacitance multiplier) to cut line-frequency ripple and broadband input noise; this is easier than raising the regulator's loop gain and bandwidth, and more effective than piling on output capacitance. The datasheet line-regulation figure applies at dc/low frequency where loop gain is high and does not indicate high-frequency supply-noise rejection. | qualitative (see §8.15.1, Fig. 8.122) | - | Low-noise supplies | review | p.619 §9.3.13, footnote 30 | high |

| AOE-2821 | thermal | Plan thermal management whenever a device dissipates more than a few tenths of a watt; package capability: up to ~1 W via leads soldered to PCB; SMT power tabs (SOT-223, DPAK/TO-252, D2PAK/TO-263, DirectFET) a few W; > ~5 W use bolt-down packages (TO-3, TO-220, TO-247) on a substantial heatsink; modules (miniBLOC, Powertap) off-PCB to 100 W or more. | P > few tenths W -> thermal design; > ~5 W -> bolted heatsink | P (W), package | Power semiconductors, regulators, resistors, fast digital ICs | review | p.623-624 §9.4, §9.4.1 | high |
